# Changelog

All notable changes to `pushery/sqlens-for-laravel` are documented here. The format follows [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.0.0/) and the project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [0.27.0] - 2026-10-04

### Changed

- **The PGLS adapter accepts `postgrestools` 0.26.x.** Its version window ended below 0.26.0, so a pipeline that pinned the current release got the tool reported as unsupported and lost its checks. 0.26.0 was measured against the same probe database as 0.25.7: it executes the identical splinter catalog, reports the same findings in the same JSON shape, prints the same `--version` line and answers an unreachable server in the same plain text. The window now ends below 0.27.0, and `@postgrestools/postgrestools@0.25.7` stays a supported pin.

- **An `ALTER TABLE` whose later action cannot be read is undetermined, as that action is on its own.** The classifier reads every action of an action list, and one it could not resolve was left out while the statement kept the kind of its first action. So `ADD COLUMN user_id bigint, ADD CONSTRAINT FOREIGN KEY …` without a constraint name passed as a plain `ADD COLUMN`, and no foreign-key rule ever saw the key, while the same clause on its own was undetermined. Such a statement is now undetermined with the reason the action carries, and the detail names the action by its place in the list. Table options a driver declares, such as MySQL's `ALGORITHM` and `LOCK`, are still read as options. A migration that passed only because a clause went unread now comes back undetermined.

### Fixed

- **A migration that sends a query to another connection is reported as undetermined, with the connection named.** The capture refuses such a query before it runs, in pretend and in shadow mode, and reported the migration as failed, under `CAP.L0.PRETEND_ERROR` or `CAP.L0.MIGRATE_ERROR`, although the migration did nothing wrong. It is now `undetermined` with the reason `migration_on_another_connection`, the reason a migration declaring a `$connection` of its own already carries, and the finding names the connection the query was sent to.
- **A MySQL audit reads the routines of the database it audits, not of every database on the server.** On MySQL a schema is a database, and the routine reading took all of them, so on a shared server an audit of one application reported `SEC.PRIV.ROUTINE_DEFINER` for the routines of every other one as its own. It now reads the connection's database, as the PostgreSQL audit reads its own.
- **`sqlens:audit` accepts a baseline entry for a Postgres Language Server finding.** The audit checked a baseline's rule ids without the external tools' namespaces, so a baseline that accepted one `PGLS.*` finding turned every audit into a misconfiguration (exit 2) that suppressed nothing at all. The lint suite already passed them.
- **Every column of a hand-written `CREATE TABLE` reaches the rules.** After a column with no modifier, the reader of a table body skipped the next one, so `(id bigint, email text, note text)` was read as `id` and `note`, and the list was passed on as complete: `PG.L4.IDENTIFIER_LENGTH` never saw the skipped names, and the disk headroom check counted too few columns. SQL from Laravel's schema builder writes `null` or `not null` after every column and was not affected.
- **A table or column named after an unreserved keyword is read by its name.** An unquoted `type`, `comment` or `date` in hand-written SQL made `ADD COLUMN`, `DROP COLUMN`, `CREATE TABLE` and `DROP TABLE` undetermined, so no rule ran over the migration and a strict run failed over valid SQL. An index on such a column carried no key columns, and a table body skipped the column as if it were a constraint. A name position now takes every keyword the server accepts unquoted, as listed by PostgreSQL's `pg_get_keywords()` and MySQL's `information_schema.KEYWORDS`, and reads it as the object its quoted spelling names. A reserved word is still refused. SQL from Laravel's schema builder is unaffected, because it quotes every name.
- **`sqlens:drift` and `sqlens:postdeploy --expect-shadow` name the databases `capture.shadow.keep_on_failure` kept.** A replay that did not come through clean left the shadow clone on the server, and on PostgreSQL the template with the project's whole schema, while the run printed only the reason no expectation could be built. Both are now named beside that reason as `shadow_kept_on_failure`, and a replay that stops with an error names them in the error, as `sqlens:lint --shadow` already did.
- **External tools follow the run's categories, as rules do.** Once the binary was installed, `sqlens:lint --category=security` and the lint half of `sqlens:security` reported Squawk's safety findings, and `sqlens:audit --category=safety` reported every finding of the Postgres Language Server, all of which are security findings. A tool's verdict now passes the run's level and category gates like a rule's, with security and privacy gated by severity rather than level, and a baseline entry under a tool id those gates leave out is not reported as stale. A tool that could not answer is still reported at any setting.
- **`PG.L5.COLLATION_VERSION_MISMATCH` checks the collations PostgreSQL imports, not only the ones you create.** A column declared `COLLATE "de-DE-x-icu"` uses a collation that `initdb` put into `pg_catalog`, and the audit skipped that schema with the other system schemas, so an index on such a column went unchecked after an ICU or glibc upgrade. Every `pg_catalog` collation that a column, an index or a constraint depends on is now read, and the rest of that schema is not. The fix material names such a collation quoted, as `"pg_catalog"."de-DE-x-icu"`, because unquoted it reads as a different name.
- **Two injection findings of one rule in one PHP file no longer share a fingerprint.** `sqlens:security` reports a call site by its file and the analyzer's identifier, and the fingerprint leaves the line out, so that a line inserted above does not change it. Nothing else told two findings of one rule in one file apart, and in SARIF they carried one `partialFingerprints` value. The fingerprint of a call site now also takes the functions it sits in and the code on its line, and the order of two identical lines in one function; a line inserted above still changes nothing. Where the analyzed file cannot be read at the reported line, the fingerprint stays as it was. The value changes once with this release, so code scanning can show such alerts as fixed and as new on the first upload after the upgrade.

- **`sqlens:lint --shadow` runs a migration whose only pre-scan hits are introspection guards.** A `Schema::hasTable()` or `hasColumn()` guard keeps a migration out of a pretend run, because pretend cannot answer it, and the finding named shadow mode as the way out. Under `--shadow` the same migration was held back just the same, so a re-runnable migration was judged by no rule in either mode. Shadow mode now runs it against its throwaway database, which holds the schema the guard asks about. A side effect, a branch on query results or a call the pre-scan cannot follow still keeps a migration out of both modes, and its finding now says so instead of sending the reader to shadow mode; the rule pages for those three no longer offer it either.

- **`sqlens:lint --shadow` runs a migration that names the linted connection against the shadow database.** Telescope's migration, and any other whose `getConnection()` returns the connection being linted, reached the real connection, which the capture refuses, so the migration failed as `CAP.L0.MIGRATE_ERROR` and every migration after it was reported as not judged. That name now resolves to the shadow for the run, the database the shadow stands in for, and the real connection is set aside rather than closed and given back afterwards. A migration that names any other connection is still refused before its query runs.

- **A `write` block on a connection without a `read` block no longer hides the database the connection reaches.** Laravel opens a read/write split only when a `read` block is set and otherwise connects with the connection's own keys, but SQLens laid any `write` block over them. A connection whose own `database` named a production database and whose unread `write` block named another one was judged by the second name, so the production guard of `--shadow` let it pass. The effective configuration now follows the framework: a `write` block counts only beside a `read` block, a `write` list of one entry is that entry, and a longer list contributes what its entries agree on, with differing hosts as a pool. Before, the list was merged as numeric keys and left the connection's own host in place.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` and `sqlens:postdeploy` name a check that could not answer in the report itself.** Such a check carried its reason as a sentence on its result and nowhere else, so `--format=json` had no finding for it, `summary.counts.undetermined_reason` counted its reason as 0, and a deploy script reading the document at exit 3 could not tell which check held the deploy or why. Only stderr said so, the one stream an archived report does not keep. The check now stands in the findings as an `undetermined` finding with the check id as its rule id, its reason and its sentence, unless its own findings already say it could not answer. The verdict and the exit code do not change, because an undetermined finding never breaches a gate.

- **The level-3 timeout rules no longer take a `SET` that bounds nothing for a bound.** `PG.L3.MISSING_LOCK_TIMEOUT`, `PG.L3.MISSING_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT` and `MY.L3.MISSING_LOCK_WAIT_TIMEOUT` went silent on `SET lock_timeout = 0`, `TO DEFAULT` or `'100us'` (which PostgreSQL rounds to zero), on `ALTER ROLE … SET` and `ALTER DATABASE … SET`, on MySQL's `SET GLOBAL` and `SET PERSIST`, on `= DEFAULT` (a year on MySQL 8.4) and on any value above an hour. The session running the migration stayed unbounded in every case. A preamble now counts only when it begins its statement, sets this session's value and leaves a real limit, and the last one before the locking statement decides, so a later `RESET` or `DEFAULT` takes a bound away again. `sqlens:predeploy` reads the same statements the same way, so the lint and the preflight check no longer answer one `SET` two ways.

- **On PostgreSQL, a constraint added without a name is judged like a named one, and an `EXCLUDE` constraint is judged at all.** `ALTER TABLE … ADD CHECK (…)`, `ADD FOREIGN KEY (…)` and `ADD EXCLUDE …` carry no name, and `PG.L2.CONSTRAINT_NOT_VALIDATED` recognized a constraint add only by `ADD CONSTRAINT`, `ADD PRIMARY KEY` or `ADD UNIQUE`, so these three validated every row, or built their index, under `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` with no finding. They are reported now, with a note that the constraint needs a name for its `VALIDATE`. A named `EXCLUDE` constraint was reported as `undetermined` rather than with its advice, because the canonical form writes the word in lower case and the rule looked for it in upper case. `PG.L4.CONSTRAINT_VALIDATION_PENDING` reports an unnamed constraint added `NOT VALID` as well, and records no debt for it, since there is no name to record it under.

- **A bump of the canonical form version no longer gives every baseline entry a new identity.** Since 0.26.0 a finding's baseline identity includes the statement it is about, and the statement's fingerprint included the form version. Each bump therefore made every accepted finding report as new until the baseline was regenerated, and a regenerated baseline also accepts whatever is new at that moment. The identity now takes the statement's fingerprint with the version held at the form 0.26.0 shipped, so a baseline written by 0.26.x keeps matching and an identity changes only when the statement's own canonical form does. SARIF `partialFingerprints` carry the same value, so a code-scanning alert keeps its history across an upgrade too. The canonical-form page said a bump left a baseline alone; it now says how.

- **MySQL reads `ALTER TABLE … DROP CHECK <name>` as a dropped constraint, not as a dropped column called `check`.** `CHECK` was missing from the MySQL keyword list, so the bare `DROP` and `ADD` shapes took the word for a column name. A migration that dropped a check constraint got `MY.L1.DROP_COLUMN`, `MY.L4.DROP_WITHOUT_DEPLOY_WINDOW` and `MY.L8.NAMING_SNAKE_CASE` for a column that does not exist, and an unnamed `ADD CHECK (…)` was read as an added column. Both are constraints now, also as a later action of a multi-action `ALTER TABLE`, and a quoted column named `` `check` `` keeps its quotes in the canonical form. **The canonical form version moves to 7.** A committed baseline keeps matching: a finding's identity follows the statement's own canonical form, which this fix moves only for a MySQL statement with a lower-case `check` in it and for one it now reads differently.

- **The MySQL grant check of `sqlens:predeploy` suggests a grant on a database as one on the database.** For a database object it proposed ``GRANT DROP ON archive TO …``, and on MySQL `ON archive` names a table called `archive` in the session's default database, so running the suggestion granted a privilege on the wrong object and the deploy still failed. It now proposes ``GRANT DROP ON `archive`.* TO …``. The undetermined answer for an object name that does not split into a database and an object also stops saying the name is unqualified and the connection has no default database, which was not the case.

- **A MySQL activity reading whose `performance_schema` switch cannot be read is no longer taken for calm.** When `@@performance_schema` answered with no number, the reader recorded no gap, so the snapshot called its silence trustworthy and the lock checks of `sqlens:predeploy` read an empty answer as a quiet server, over instrumentation nobody had read. That state now carries a gap of its own, with the new skip reason `instrumentation_unknown` and the undetermined reason `catalog_reading_implausible`, so the silence is not trusted. It is not reported as `instrumentation_disabled`, because nothing says the switch is off.

- **A MySQL schema dump cannot hide a statement behind a `--` that MySQL does not read as a comment.** MySQL opens a `--` comment only when whitespace follows the dashes, so `1 --1` is one minus minus one and the statements after it on that line run. SQLens read every `--` as a comment, so in a dump line such as ``SET @x = 1 --1 ; USE `app`;`` the reader that refuses statements reaching past the throwaway database saw one harmless `SET`, while the server ran the `USE` and the replay continued in another database. The MySQL dialect now opens a `--` comment only before whitespace, as the server does, everywhere SQLens reads MySQL; PostgreSQL, where `--` always opens a comment, is unchanged.

- **The type of a column an `ALTER TABLE` adds ends where the next action begins.** In `ADD COLUMN nickname text, DROP COLUMN legacy` the column was given the type `TEXT DROP COLUMN legacy`, because the type was read up to a keyword and the comma between two actions is none. A rule that asks what type a new column has received a type that does not exist. The type now ends at a comma at the statement's own level; the comma inside `numeric(10, 2)` stays part of it.

- **Behind a transaction pooler, `sqlens:lint` leaves no timeout on another client's backend.** Its session guard bounded the application's connection with a session `SET statement_timeout` and `SET lock_timeout` and restored them afterwards, unless two `pg_backend_pid()` reads in a row came from different backends. A busy pool hands out the same backend twice often enough: measured behind PgBouncer with three clients reading beside a lint run, a backend kept `lock_timeout = 3s` afterwards, and the next client given that backend inherited it. On PostgreSQL the guard no longer writes session state. The reads that need a bound, whether the migration table exists, which migrations ran and what the last batch was, now run inside one short transaction with `SET LOCAL statement_timeout` and `SET LOCAL lock_timeout`, which the rollback at its end takes off again, and without a named prepared statement, so a pooler that tracks prepared statements cannot abort it. A connection that is already inside a transaction gets a savepoint, and its own transaction keeps what it had. `sqlens:drift` and `sqlens:postdeploy --expect-shadow` read the same state the same way. MySQL is unchanged.

- **A refused run no longer deletes a symlink named by `--output`.** `sqlens:lint`, `sqlens:audit` and `sqlens:security` remove the report file a run never wrote into, and they followed a link to decide but deleted the link itself, leaving the file it points at behind, empty. With `--output=/dev/stdout` and stdout redirected into a file, a run as root in a container removed the container's `/dev/stdout`. Only a regular file at the path is removed now; a link and what it points at stay where they are.
- **A shadow run on a server that refuses to create the throwaway database reports it, instead of ending in a stack trace.** A refusal none of the checks before provisioning names, such as `cannot execute CREATE DATABASE in a read-only transaction`, reached Laravel's console handler. It printed the exception on STDOUT, where `--format=json` writes the report, with the connection's host, port and database and the statement, and the command exited 1, the code for findings above the gate. `sqlens:lint --shadow` now reports every migration of the run as `undetermined` with the new reason `shadow_provisioning_failed` and the database's own message, masked like every capture message. Any other failure during provisioning still ends the run.
- **On PostgreSQL, `DEPLOY.CONTEXT.READ_ONLY_TARGET` judges `default_transaction_read_only` for the role the migrations run as.** The check read the setting through the preflight's own session, so a preflight role hardened with `ALTER ROLE … SET default_transaction_read_only = on` stopped every deploy on a writable primary, while a migration role that starts read-only passed and its deploy failed at the first write. The check now resolves the stored role and database defaults the way a fresh session of the migration role gets them, like `DEPLOY.CONTEXT.SETTING.LOCK_TIMEOUT_UNBOUNDED` already does, names that role when its own default decided, and answers `undetermined` with the reason when the defaults cannot say. A migration role's own `off` over a database default of `on` now passes.
- **`SEC.CFG.STATEMENT_LOGGING_SECRETS` no longer recommends `log_statement = ddl` as if it kept credentials out of the log.** `ddl` writes every schema change verbatim, so a role password set in plain text by `CREATE ROLE` or `ALTER ROLE` reaches the log there as well, as does one in a `CREATE USER MAPPING` or a `CREATE SUBSCRIPTION` connection string. The finding still recommends `ddl` for an audit trail without row values, and now says what it still logs and to set role passwords with psql's `\password`, which sends a SCRAM verifier instead of the password. The rule stays silent at `ddl`, because the setting cannot show how passwords are written, and its limitations say so.
- **The last sentence of a server-setting finding starts with a capital letter.** The sentence that says what changing the setting costs (a reload, a restart, a maintenance window or a new cluster) is appended after a full stop by every rule that carries it, and it started lowercase.
- **A tool that hits its timeout no longer leaves the processes it started running.** The npm package of the Postgres Language Server installs `postgrestools` as a node launcher that runs the native binary as its child, and a timeout stopped only the launcher: the native binary went on with its database sessions open after the run had reported that nothing was measured. A stopped tool now takes every process it started with it, read from `/proc` on Linux and from `ps` elsewhere; on Windows the stop already covered the whole tree.
- **The Postgres Language Server's sessions run under SQLens's own session bounds.** Two of the tool's catalog reads call `pg_total_relation_size()` over every relation, which takes an ACCESS SHARE lock on each one. With a migration holding ACCESS EXCLUSIVE on a table, a run waited out its whole timeout, and the stopped tool's sessions stayed in the lock queue. SQLens now hands the tool `PGOPTIONS` with the `catalog.session` lock and idle-in-transaction bounds, a statement bound equal to the tool's `timeout`, read-only transactions and an `application_name` ending in `-pgls`, so such a run ends after the lock bound and leaves nothing waiting.
- **A shadow session the server ends mid-migration is reported as `undetermined` (`shadow_connection_lost`), not as the migration's failure.** A terminated backend, a restart or a dropped network ended the statement without a verdict, and the run reported `CAP.L0.MIGRATE_ERROR` with "no connection to the server". The migration now carries the new reason and the driver's message, recognized with the list Laravel's own connection uses to decide whether to reconnect.
- **On PostgreSQL, `DEPLOY.CONTEXT.SETTING` reads `statement_timeout` for the role the migrations run as, and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` as the server's value.** Both were read for the preflight's own role, so a `statement_timeout` default on the preflight account made the finding vanish while the migration ran without a bound. When either value is out of sight, the check now says so in an `undetermined` note beside a passing result: neither setting ever stops a deploy.
- **On PostgreSQL, the shadow database takes the encoding and locale of the application database.** The template was created from `template0` with the cluster's defaults, so a `LATIN1` or ICU application database got a `UTF8` libc shadow, and a migration writing a character `LATIN1` cannot hold passed in the shadow and failed for real. The template now copies the encoding, locale provider, `LC_COLLATE`, `LC_CTYPE`, locale and ICU rules from `pg_database`.
- **On MySQL, a backslash no longer moves where capture thinks a string literal ends.** A bound string was written with its quotes doubled and nothing else, so `C:\` became `'C:\'`, a literal MySQL never closes, and a second statement behind it stayed inside it, out of every rule's sight. Read the other way, `\'` was taken for the end of a literal, so a column comment Laravel writes through `addslashes()`, such as `comment 'What\'s this?'`, made its migration undetermined, and a value the pretend log had already quoted was substituted a second time into its own literal. Both directions now follow the literal syntax the driver declares, the one the statement splitter reads.
- **On PostgreSQL, the jsonb `?` operators are read as operators, not as placeholders.** PDO needs a `?` that is SQL written as `??`, and Laravel's own `whereJsonContainsKey()` writes it that way. Capture counted both characters as placeholders, so a migration with a partial index, a check or a backfill over a jsonb key was reported as undetermined and called result-dependent. In pretend mode, where Laravel has already put the values into the logged statement, the operator itself was replaced by a value, and the rules read a statement the server never receives. A `??` now counts as the one `?` the server receives, a statement captured without bindings keeps every `?` it has, and a pretend capture is taken as the complete statement it is.
- **On PostgreSQL, an escape string `E'…'` and a nested block comment end where PostgreSQL ends them.** The splitter, the canonicalization stages, the literal mask and the binding substitution read `E'it\'s'` as ending at the escaped quote, so a statement behind it in the same entry stayed inside a literal and out of every rule's sight, and a bound value was substituted into it. A block comment ended at its first `*/`, although PostgreSQL nests them, so `/* a /* b */ c */ DROP TABLE users` was refused as an unrecognized statement and its migration reported as undetermined. MySQL, which does not nest comments, reads as before.
- **`PG.L9.VIEW_SELECT_STAR` and `MY.L9.VIEW_SELECT_STAR` find the star behind a PostgreSQL backslash and in every branch of a set operation.** The scan read every literal with MySQL's backslash escape, so on PostgreSQL a `'\'` before the star ran on to the end of the statement and took the star with it, and only the first select list of a view was read, so `SELECT a FROM t UNION ALL SELECT * FROM u` passed. Literals are now read by the engine's syntax, the branches joined by `UNION`, `INTERSECT` or `EXCEPT` are read as well, and a branch that is no select, such as `TABLE u`, makes the view undetermined instead of clean.
- **A statement whose bindings cannot be written into its text is reported as `binding_not_rendered`, with the binding named.** It was reported as `pretend_limit`, which calls the migration result-dependent and advises the shadow mode, also when the run already was one and when all that stopped it was a binary or array value. The finding now says which binding SQLens could not write, or how many placeholders and values it counted.
- **A binary value in a data migration no longer stops the lint.** In shadow mode a byte string that is not text, a binary UUID for instance, is written as the engine's hex literal (`X'…'` on MySQL, `'\x…'::bytea` on PostgreSQL) instead of making the migration undetermined. In pretend mode Laravel's own query log refuses to escape such a value, and that was reported as `CAP.L0.PRETEND_ERROR`, telling you to fix an error the migration does not raise; it is now reported as undetermined (`binding_not_rendered`) with Laravel's message. A finding that quotes an error message ending in a period no longer ends that sentence with two.

### Security

- **`sqlens:format` no longer lets a formatted file configure SQLFluff into running code, and reads no SQLFluff configuration from outside the project.** SQLFluff treats its input as a Jinja template unless told otherwise, and measured on 4.3.0 a `-- sqlfluff:templater:jinja:library_path:<dir>` line in a formatted file imported and ran the Python modules in that directory. The backend now passes `--templater raw` and `--library-path none`, which neither a configuration file nor a line in the text overrides, so the file is formatted as SQL and no Python is loaded. It also passes `--ignore-local-config` and names the `.sqlfluff` at the project's root with `--config`, so a `.sqlfluff` in the home directory or in a directory above the project no longer changes the output on one machine and not on another. A literal such as `'{% x %}'`, which the Jinja templater refused as broken template syntax, is now formatted.
- **A capture finding no longer prints the row a database constraint refused.** When a migration failed on a constraint in a shadow run, the finding carried the database's message as it was, and that message quotes the row: PostgreSQL's `Key (email)=(…) already exists.` and `Failing row contains (…)`, which lists every column, and MySQL's `Duplicate entry '…' for key '…'`. A value a migration inserted from the environment, such as an administrator's address or a password hash, reached the CI log, the JSON report and SARIF. `CAP.L0.MIGRATE_ERROR`, `CAP.L0.PRETEND_ERROR`, `CAP.L0.DOWN_FAILED` and `CAP.L0.DOWN_NOT_INVERTIBLE` now replace each such value with `<redacted>` and keep the rest of the message, the column, the constraint and the key, for the shapes PostgreSQL 18 and MySQL 8.4 were measured writing.

## [0.26.0] - 2026-10-01

### Added

- **`CAP.PRESCAN.DYNAMIC_CALL` holds back a migration that makes a call the pre-scan cannot resolve.** `call_user_func(...)`, `Http::$method(...)`, `$class::dispatch()`, `$client->$method(...)` and `$f(...)` name their target only at run time, so no pre-scan catalog matched them, and a pretend capture ran them for real, whatever they sent. Such a migration is now undetermined, and the finding names the form of the call. A call with a variable method name on the migration's own `$this`, `self` or `static` is not flagged, because the pre-scan then reads every method of the migration. The rule is stable, so a default run holds these calls back: a migration that only passed because nobody could read its call now comes back undetermined. **The shipped registry holds 244 entries**, three more than 0.25.0: this rule and the two run notices under Fixed, `DEBT.STANDING_UNKNOWN` and `LINT.SHADOW.ORPHANS`. By category: **148 safety**, 59 security, 17 idiom, 10 performance, 7 convention and 3 privacy. By maturity: **239 stable**, 3 preview, 2 experimental. **163** answer to the level gate; the other **81** carry a severity, unchanged at 7 critical, 33 high, 29 medium, 8 low and 4 info. None of the three carries a `downtime_class`, so that count stays at 58 entries.

### Changed

- **`DerivesDowntimeClass::downtimeClassFor()` may answer with a `DowntimeUndetermined`.** A rule that derives its downtime class per statement now says why it could not name one, and the collector stamps that on the finding. An implementation that returns `?DowntimeClass` still satisfies the contract; code that calls the method receives the new type where a class could not be determined.

- **`sqlens:security` reads the injection half only from a result written with `--error-format=sqlens`.** A plain `--error-format=json` result from a PHPStan run whose configuration never loaded the SQLens extension is the same document a codebase without raw SQL produces, and the security report counted it as examined and clean. The extension now registers the `sqlens` error format: it writes PHPStan's JSON result plus a trace, PHPStan refuses to start with it unless the extension is loaded, and the trace records the internal errors PHPStan hit, since a file it could not analyze to the end is a file nobody examined. A result without the trace, or with an internal error in it, is reported as not run, with the reason. Change `--error-format=json` to `--error-format=sqlens` in the step that writes `sqlens.security.analyse.result_path`.

- **The baseline file is at schema version 3, and a version 2 file is refused with the command that regenerates it.** A finding's baseline identity was meant to include the canonical statement it is about, and it never did, so two findings of one rule in one migration on one table were told apart by their order alone. When a statement was inserted above an accepted one, the acceptance moved to the new finding, which nobody had reviewed, and if the accepted one was fixed in the same change, the gate passed over a finding no one had accepted. The identity now includes the statement, for rule findings and for the findings squawk reports, and the SARIF `partialFingerprints` carry the same value. Each entry also records what its finding was about in a required `kind` field: `migration`, `catalog` or `callsite`. Run `php artisan sqlens:baseline` once after upgrading and commit the new file; an entry you had added by hand for an audit finding has to be added again, with `"kind": "catalog"`.

- **`ext-tokenizer` is now declared in the manifest.** The shipped code reads PHP with the tokenizer: the fix material for a Laravel snippet escapes each value for the string it lands in, and finding the application's models reads which class a file declares before loading it. The manifest did not say so. `nikic/php-parser`, which this package already requires, requires the extension as well, so no installation that worked before is refused.

### Fixed

- **On a read/write split, `sqlens:lint` reads the session it bounds.** Its session guard asked `pg_backend_pid()` and read the current timeouts with plain selects, which a `read`/`write` configuration sends to the replica, while its `SET`s and the restore went to the primary. The pooler check therefore asked a session it never bounds: a transaction pooler in front of the primary went unnoticed and the bound landed on a foreign backend, and the restore set the primary to the replica's timeouts. The check whether the migration table exists went to the replica as well, a session without a bound that answers "no migration table" while it lags. All of these now run on the write side.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` compares the server version pin of the profile it runs under.** The preflight checks are built once, when the command's dependencies are resolved and before it applies its profile, and the version-skew check read its `assume_server_version` pin then, from the base configuration, while the lint half of the same run read the profile's. With the pin only in the predeploy profile, the check said no pin was configured and a stale one went unseen; with a stale base pin and a correct profile pin, it blocked a deploy that had been checked against the right version. The check now compares the pin the run resolved.

- **A suite command no longer leaves its profile in the configuration when it returns.** Every suite command wrote the profile it resolved, that profile's settings and `--min-severity` into the application's configuration and left them there, so the next command in the same process ran under them: an application command calling one suite command after another, a queue job, an Octane worker. `sqlens:predeploy` after an `sqlens:audit` found the audit's `local` configured, which outranks its own default, and blocked on `critical` instead of `high`. `--profile=local` after a `ci` run kept the `ci` level and both strict settings. Each command now puts back what the configuration held when it returns, and a key the configuration did not set stays unset.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` refuses a server its lint half refuses, instead of passing it.** Behind the `mysql` driver, a MariaDB answered the lint half of a preflight with a refusal, and the preflight went on without it: no pending migration was linted or counted, the deploy checks read "nothing pending" and passed, and the gate ended with exit 0 moments before a deploy. `sqlens:predeploy` and the MCP tool `predeploy` now stop with the misconfiguration exit and name the reason, as `sqlens:lint` does.

- **A MySQL finding whose downtime class could not be determined says so, and the run no longer reads as online.** Several MySQL rules take the class of a statement from the online-DDL matrix, and some entries depend on the live table: a `DROP COLUMN` is instant unless the column carries a functional index or one of three other conditions holds, and an `ENUM` redefinition depends on where the new member goes. A static run cannot settle that, and those findings carried neither a class nor a reason, which reads as a finding that makes no claim about downtime. The histogram did not count them, and with the `online` of `MY.L4.DROP_WITHOUT_DEPLOY_WINDOW` beside a column drop the run reported `worst_downtime_class: "online"` for a drop that may rebuild the table. Such a finding now carries `downtime_undetermined` with its reason and detail in place of `downtime_class`, `summary.downtime_undetermined` counts them, and `worst_downtime_class` is `null` while one is present and no finding names `rewrite`. The console, GitHub, SARIF and agent formats name it as well. **The envelope's `schema_version` is 9**: a gate that decides from `counts.downtime_class` adds `summary.downtime_undetermined` to what blocks, as the documented recipe now does.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` checks the freeze horizon and the autovacuum setting of the tables an ordinary migration names.** Both checks matched each table against its schema-qualified catalog name, and `Schema::table('orders', …)` names `orders` without a schema. Neither check found such a table, and both passed without having looked: a deploy whose `ALTER TABLE` would wait behind an anti-wraparound vacuum, or that touches a table with autovacuum switched off, got a green gate. A bare name now matches the table an unqualified reference finds, the way the lock-blocker check already matches it.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` sees an anti-wraparound vacuum already running on a table in the search path.** The freeze-horizon check paired running vacuums with tables by a name written the way the search path needs it, which is `orders` for a table in `public`, while its age reading names the table `public.orders`. A vacuum on such a table was never paired: the finding left out that one was already running, and a table below the reporting threshold with a vacuum on it passed. Both sides use schema and name now, and only the vacuums of the database being checked count.

- **`sqlens:lint` in pretend mode no longer runs a migration for real when it captures on a connection other than the default.** With `sqlens.connection`, `--connection` or the MCP tool's `connection` naming a connection that is not the application's default, only the capture connection was pretending while the migration method ran, and a migration written the way `make:migration` writes one sends its `Schema` and `DB` calls to the default connection. Those calls ran for real: `up()` created tables and wrote rows in the default database, and the rollback reader, which reads `down()` in every capture mode, dropped them. The capture itself came back empty. The method now runs with the capture connection as its default, the way Laravel's migrator runs a migration.

- **The pre-scan in front of a pretend capture reads everything the capture runs.** It read the calls written inside `up()` and `down()`, while loading a migration runs its top-level code, instantiating it runs its constructor, and `up()` runs every helper it reaches. A side effect moved into a private method, the constructor or the top of the file passed the pre-scan, and the pretend run then sent the request, the mail or the job for real. The pre-scan now follows `$this->m()`, `self::m()`, `static::m()`, a function of the file called by name and a method handed over as `[$this, 'm']`, and it reads every method once one is called by a name only known at run time. A helper nothing calls is still left alone.

- **A capture no longer lets a migration reach another connection.** Pretend mode intercepts the capture connection alone, and shadow mode points the default connection at the throwaway database, so a migration that names a connection of its own, `DB::connection('reporting')` or `Schema::connection('reporting')`, sent its statements there for real during a lint. In shadow mode those statements were also reported as the shadow's own. While a capture runs a migration method, every query on any other connection is now refused before it reaches its server, and the capture records the migration as failed, naming the connection it reached.

- **A pretend capture pretends what a migration file runs while it loads.** Loading a migration runs its top-level code and its constructor, and both ran before the pretend began: what they sent to the database was written for real, and what they sent to another connection reached it, in shadow mode as well. Loading now happens inside the same frame as the migration method, so those statements are captured like the method's and a query to another connection is refused. A file that cannot be loaded fails its own migration instead of ending the run, in both capture modes.

- **The false-positive measurement refuses a run that did not judge its corpus, instead of writing a report over it.** On a database without a migration table, `sqlens:corpus-measure` could not read which migrations were pending, and it still wrote a report and exited 0: every expected pass read as correct and every expected failure as missed. It now names the collection, the connection and the reason, exits 1 and writes nothing, and it does the same when the migration table lists a corpus migration as already run. The reproduction steps on the false-positive-rate page install the migration table first.

- **Shadow mode builds its throwaway databases where it says, also on a connection configured by `DB_URL`.** Laravel lays a connection's `url` over its other keys, the database among them, and every connection into a template, a clone or the admin database was built as the source's configuration with only `database` changed. Under `DB_URL` those connections landed in the application's own database: replaying the schema dump dropped and recreated its tables there, the migrations ran there, and `sqlens:drift` read its expected schema from the live database and compared it with itself. The URL is now resolved before the database is changed, and before its first statement every such connection asks the server which database it reached and refuses with `shadow_connection_collides` when that is not the throwaway one. The production check, the collision check between `capture.shadow.connection` and the connection under examination, and the check that `capture.shadow.direct_connection` reaches the same server all read a `url`-configured connection the way Laravel resolves it.

- **`sqlens:predeploy`, `sqlens:postdeploy` and the `predeploy` MCP tool no longer report a reading of one database under the name of another.** The reading goes through `sqlens.preflight.connection`, and the verdict is reported for the connection that was asked about. Asked with `--connection` about a connection on another database, the run read the preflight connection's database and reported its locks, replication lag, grants and settings as the state of the one asked about, so a deploy there could proceed on a reading of somewhere else. A connection that reaches another database is now refused by name. The migration connection and a read-only role on the same database still work together, as before.

- **A PostgreSQL reading can no longer be steered by a function or operator in a schema on the reading role's path.** Qualifying the catalog functions a reading calls left the operators it uses reachable: a role with `CREATE` on a schema in that path could define `||` for two `name` values and take over every `schema || '.' || table` comparison, which is how the deploy checks and the security reader match tables. Every reading now pins its search path to the catalog for the length of its transaction, after recording the application's own path, and the lookups of a bare table name as a migration writes it resolve on that recorded path: grants, locks, statistics, vacuum and autovacuum state, the tables given to the security reader, and the schemas an unscoped reading covers. Types, sequence defaults and foreign-key definitions of objects in the application's schemas are now reported schema-qualified, such as `public.mood`, `nextval('public.orders_id_seq')` and `REFERENCES public.users(id)`, and they read the same whatever search path the reading connection is configured with. A column default that calls a function in another schema, such as `extensions.uuid_generate_v4()`, is recognized as that function.

- **The general query log and statement logging checks no longer pass a production server because the command ran with a non-production `APP_ENV`.** `SEC.CFG.GENERAL_LOG`, `SEC.CFG.GENERAL_LOG_PERSONAL_DATA` and `SEC.CFG.STATEMENT_LOGGING` judge their setting only on production, and they took `app.env` as the answer for every connection. That value describes the process running the audit, so a pipeline with `APP_ENV=testing` or a laptop with `APP_ENV=local` pointed at a production server reported a pass saying nothing was exposed. `app.env` now speaks only for the application's own server, the one its default connection or `sqlens.security.runtime_connection` reaches, whichever connection name, database or role leads there. Another server is production when its connection or database name says so, not production when `SQLENS_SERVER_LIFETIME=disposable` declares it a server the pipeline creates and destroys, and undetermined otherwise, where it used to pass. A host configured as a list counts as the application's own only when it is the same list.

- **A migration that hands several statements over in one call has each of them judged.** `DB::unprepared()` takes a whole batch, and the framework records the call as one entry. The capture read that entry as one statement and classified it by its first command, so every statement after it reached no rule: a `DROP TABLE` or a `TRUNCATE` behind a `CREATE INDEX` passed, on PostgreSQL and on MySQL. Each entry is now split into its statements in both capture modes and each is judged on its own, and the statements after a split entry move along in the numbering. A stored routine stays one statement: a semicolon inside the `BEGIN … END` body of a trigger, procedure, function or event, a PostgreSQL `BEGIN ATOMIC` body included, no longer ends it.

- **An `ALTER TABLE` with several actions has each of them judged.** The statement took its kind from its first action, so `ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN nickname text, DROP COLUMN legacy` was an added column to every rule, and the drop behind it reached none: without lock timeouts only the timeout rules answered, and with them the migration passed without a finding, on PostgreSQL and on MySQL. Each later action that does something other than the first is now classified on its own and judged as part of the same statement, so the drop is reported as the drop alone is, and a type change behind an added column as the change alone is. A finding about the whole statement is reported once, and MySQL's `ALGORITHM` and `LOCK` clauses are read as options of the statement rather than as actions.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` and the `predeploy` MCP tool stop a deploy on a finding about a pending migration.** The verdict read the instance checks alone. A finding about the migration the deploy was about to run, a password literal at critical for one, stood in the same report with `overall_status: fail` and `blocked_by: severity`, while the command exited 0 and the tool answered that nothing blocked. Both now read one verdict over both halves: a failing check or a finding over the profile's gate exits 1, and a pending migration that could not be judged holds the deploy back where the profile sets `strict_undetermined`, as `sqlens:lint` does under it, and `--allow-undetermined` covers it as it covers an unanswered check. The tool's `status` reports such a migration as undetermined even where the profile lets the deploy proceed.

- **`SEC.PII.UNENCRYPTED_COLUMN` runs.** It declared the security suite alone, which no runner selects, so no command ever ran it: with the privacy pack on and preview rules admitted, a report held neither a finding nor an undetermined for it and did not count it. It now runs in `sqlens:audit` and in the audit half of `sqlens:security`. `sqlens:security` also runs the privacy category beside the security one, as it promises security and privacy findings; with the pack off, as it is by default, there are no privacy rules to run and the report is unchanged.

- **`sqlens:baseline` leaves the committed file as it is when its run judged no migration.** A server it could not reach, a connection without a migration table, a database on which every migration had already run, and an ignore list naming a rule id no rule carries all ended the lint run before it read a migration, and the command wrote that empty list over the file with `Wrote 0 finding(s)` and exit 0. Every accepted finding was gone, and the next `sqlens:lint` reported them all as new. Such a run now writes nothing, under `--update` and `--dry-run` as well, names the reason in the run's own findings and exits `3`; a run that `sqlens:lint` refuses exits `2`, as it does there.

- **The `lint_shadow` MCP tool answers `undetermined` when the guard allowed a run and no shadow could be provisioned.** Behind a transaction pooler, on a read replica, or with a shadow or direct connection that reaches the wrong database, the capture judges every migration undetermined and creates no database. The tool answered `status: "ok"` with no reasons over such a run and said its findings came from a real run against a throwaway database, while `lint_pending` read the same outcome as undetermined. Both tools now read the undetermined reasons of a run the same way, and `lint_shadow` speaks of a real run only when there was one.

- **The governance contract says what a new rule ships as before 1.0.** GOVERNANCE.md, the README, the contributing guide and the Boost skill promised, without a condition, that a new rule arrives as `preview` and is opt-in. Every rule added so far ships `stable`, from one named default, and that is the policy before 1.0: a 0.x minor is where the package makes its breaking changes, and a `^0.N` constraint does not install one on its own. The promise now names 1.0, and each of these files says what a new rule ships as before it.

- **The release gate that holds the rule contract compares a rule's stability tier and level, and it compares against 0.25.0.** GOVERNANCE.md promises that a rule moves into a level's defaults only in a major. The gate that runs before a release compared only the category and the severity, against a snapshot that still described 0.0.0, so a promotion to `stable`, a rule moved to a lower level and a rule that stopped running without a deprecation all passed it. It now refuses them with the verdict `sqlens:api-snapshot --against` gives, and that classifier refuses a category change as the gate already did, where it used to allow one in a minor.

- **Shadow mode keeps the timeouts a migration sets for itself.** Every statement a migration sent in shadow mode that began with `SET` was dropped before the rules saw it, along with a transaction bracket the migration wrote itself and any statement that named the migrations table. A migration that set `lock_timeout`, `statement_timeout` or `lock_wait_timeout` before a statement taking a strong lock was therefore reported by the timeout rules in shadow mode and cleared in pretend mode. Shadow mode now passes on every statement the migration sent, as pretend mode does. The tool's own session settings are applied before the capture starts and were never among them.

- **`capture.shadow.keep_on_failure` keeps the database when a migration fails, and names every database it keeps.** A shadow run reports a failed migration as a result rather than by throwing, so the run went on to the teardown of a successful one and dropped the database the setting was meant to keep. It was kept only when the run itself threw, and then nothing named it. The databases of a run with a failed or undetermined migration now stay, each named in the findings with the reason `shadow_kept_on_failure`, and a run that throws names them in its error.

- **Shadow mode runs each migration in the transaction `migrate` gives it.** Laravel runs a migration in a transaction wherever the schema grammar supports transactional DDL, which PostgreSQL's does, unless the migration sets `$withinTransaction = false`. Shadow mode called the migration in autocommit, so a caught error that aborts the transaction, `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` inside a transaction block and an enum value used in the transaction that added it all passed in shadow mode and failed at deploy. Shadow mode now applies the same condition, and such a migration is reported as `CAP.L0.MIGRATE_ERROR` with the server's own message.

- **The debt ledger reads its dates as dates.** An empty or missing `first_seen`, and words like `today` or `yesterday`, were read as the present moment or a day ago, and `2025-02-30` as the 2nd of March, so an old debt looked new on every run and never reached a level or `deploy.debt.fail_at`. Such a `first_seen` now leaves the debt's age unknown, and a `review_at` that is not a calendar day, which was compared as text and never came due, refuses the ledger with the entry named. An entry that is not an object, or has no `kind` or `object`, refuses it too, where a stray entry used to be dropped without a word.

- **`sqlens:drift` and `sqlens:postdeploy` read the reference schema through the shadow connection.** The throwaway database is built and migrated through `capture.shadow.connection` or `capture.shadow.direct_connection` when one is set, and was then read back through the application's host and account with only the database name swapped. On another server the database was not there, behind a transaction pooler the read went through the pooler the build had to avoid, and on MySQL an account granted only the application's database was refused. The reference is now read through the connection that built it.

- **On MySQL, the shadow database takes the collation of the application database.** Its character set and collation were read through the shadow connection's account, which needs no privilege on the application database and is shown no row for it in `information_schema.SCHEMATA`, so the shadow was created with `utf8mb4_unicode_ci` and `sqlens:drift` reported every table that inherits the database collation as described differently. They are now read through the connection being examined.

- **The MySQL grant on the shadow-mode page is enough to build the shadow database.** It named `CREATE` and `DROP` on the shadow name pattern, and a role holding only those cannot replay a schema dump, which ends with the migration rows as `INSERT` statements, or run a migration, so `sqlens:drift` came back with `shadow_mysql_replay_failed` on every project that followed it. The page now grants every privilege on the pattern and nothing elsewhere, and says why.

- **`sqlens:mcp` refuses a laravel/mcp release it is not written against, before a client connects.** Under an SDK older than 0.8, which `laravel/boost` up to 2.4 still installs, the server started and the client's first handshake ended the process in a fatal that the client saw only as a disconnect. It now names the installed release and the command that installs a matching one.

- **Guard profiles no longer stop the boot on Laravel 12.0 to 12.7.** The strict mode guard asked Eloquent whether automatic eager loading is on, through a method Laravel added in 12.8, and on an earlier release every request and command of an application that named a guard profile ended in `Cannot instantiate abstract class Illuminate\Database\Eloquent\Model`. It now asks only where the switch exists; before 12.8 there is no automatic eager loading that could mask the guardrail.

- **Every command that reads migrations works on Laravel 12.0 to 12.9.** Laravel binds its migrator by class name only from 12.10, and below that `sqlens:lint`, `sqlens:baseline`, `sqlens:security`, `sqlens:predeploy`, `sqlens:drift`, `sqlens:postdeploy`, `sqlens:format` and the MCP lint tools ended with a `BindingResolutionException` before their first step. The package now binds it where the framework does not, to the same instance the framework's `migrator` is.

- **`sqlens:audit --host=` reads the host it names on a connection configured through `url`.** The framework lays a URL's components over the connection's keys, so the URL's host replaced the pinned one: the catalog was read from the instance the URL names while the report named the pinned host. The URL is now resolved into keys before the host is pinned.

- **Shadow mode loads a migration that declares a named class.** A migration in the shape `vendor:publish` keeps, a named class in a file that returns nothing, ended `sqlens:lint --shadow`, its `--roundtrip` and `sqlens:drift` with a `TypeError`, and a roundtrip's second leg would have declared the class a second time. Shadow mode now loads migrations the way pretend mode does.

- **`sqlens.agent.mcp.connection` takes effect.** The setting was documented and validated and read by nothing: `lint_pending`, `lint_shadow` and `predeploy` ran against the application's default connection, or against any configured connection a client named. Set, it is now the connection those tools run against when the client names none, and the only one a client may name.

- **`sqlens:lint --file` opens no connection for a migration that writes a string value.** Laravel writes each binding into the pretend log through the connection's read handle, and resolving that handle connected: without a reachable server the whole migration failed as `CAP.L0.PRETEND_ERROR` with none of its statements linted, and with one the fast path connected. Strings are now quoted for the log without a server, exactly as the driver's own handle quotes them, so the logged statements are the same either way.

- **Migration and format paths resolve against the application root.** `sqlens.migration_paths`, `--path` on `sqlens:lint` and `sqlens:baseline`, `sqlens.format.paths` and the paths given to `sqlens:format` were resolved against the working directory, while `--file` already used the application root. Started from anywhere else, as a monorepo hook, a deploy script or `php app/artisan` do, the same configuration found no migrations and refused a `--file` inside a configured path as outside it. A relative path now means the application root's, as it does for Laravel's own `migrate --path`, and `sqlens:format` names files relative to it in its report.

- **`sqlens:mcp` negotiates the protocol revision the way MCP defines it.** A client asking for any revision other than `2025-06-18` was refused with `-32602`, so a client that prefers a newer revision and also speaks this one never connected. It is now answered with `2025-06-18` and decides itself whether to continue. A `protocolVersion` that is not a string is still refused.

- **The strict mode guard judges the application after it has booted.** It checked whether automatic eager loading masks the lazy-loading guardrail while this package booted, before the application's own providers, so turning it on in `AppServiceProvider::boot()`, the usual place, was never reported. The check now runs once every provider has booted, and it also reports, as `guardrail_overridden`, a guardrail the profile turns on that a later provider switched back off.

- **The preflight grant check asks about the account the migrations run as.** It read the migration role from the connection's `username` key, which is not the account Laravel migrates with when the connection is configured through `url`, has a read/write split, or, from Laravel 13.17, a PostgreSQL `direct` block. It also took the migration connection from `database.migrations.connection`, which Laravel never reads. On MySQL a stock `username` of `root` beside a `url` made the check pass a pending `ALTER TABLE` the real account could not run. The account is now resolved the way the framework resolves it, and the migration connection is the one `sqlens.security.migration_connection` names, or the default.

- **The deploy commands read the server the migrations run on.** `sqlens:predeploy`, `sqlens:postdeploy` and `sqlens:drift` dropped a connection's read/write split and read its base keys, so on a split configured the way Laravel's documentation shows they read `127.0.0.1`, or no host at all, instead of the primary. The shadow reader of a drift comparison did the same. They now read the write side, resolved the way Laravel resolves it. A write side offering several hosts is refused before anything connects, naming every host, unless `sqlens.host` pins one of them.

- **The redundant-index rule no longer counts an invisible MySQL index as coverage.** An `INVISIBLE` index is maintained on every write and ignored by the optimizer, and `MY.L7.INDEX_REDUNDANT` treated one as covering a visible index, so it advised dropping the index the lookups use. An invisible index now never covers. One that a visible index covers is still reported, and of two identical indexes the invisible one is the one reported.

- **`GEN.L3.DML_ON_SCHEMA_CHANGED_TABLE` no longer fires on MySQL.** It read the migration's own `$withinTransaction` instead of the resolved transaction mode, so on MySQL, where every DDL statement commits implicitly and the migrator wraps nothing, it reported a schema lock held across the data write that cannot be held there, beside `MY.L3.MIXED_DDL_DML_NOT_ATOMIC` saying the opposite. It now reads the resolved mode, which also turns a migration whose transaction context could not be resolved into an undetermined verdict rather than a failure.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` and `sqlens:postdeploy` refuse a MariaDB server behind the `mysql` driver.** Both read a MariaDB's locks, settings and grants as MySQL 8.4's. Postdeploy, which runs no lint, never refused one, and predeploy refused only when the application connection showed the banner. Both now ask the handshake of the connection they read through and stop with exit 2 before the first check, quoting what the server reported.

- **SQLens bounds the metadata-lock wait of its own MySQL sessions.** The capture, reader and shadow sessions set `max_execution_time` and `innodb_lock_wait_timeout`, and neither reaches the metadata lock an `ALTER TABLE` queues behind, whose `lock_wait_timeout` defaults to a year: a shadow migration behind another session's lock waited regardless of `capture.shadow.timeout`. All three now set `lock_wait_timeout` to the same budget, and the application's borrowed connection gets its value back afterwards. The run of a schema change stays unbounded on MySQL, which bounds read-only `SELECT` statements only.

- **A MySQL migration written in the manual's own spellings is judged instead of reported undetermined.** `MODIFY COLUMN`, `CHANGE COLUMN`, `KEY` where the grammar writes `INDEX` (`ADD KEY`, `ADD UNIQUE KEY`, `ADD FULLTEXT KEY`, `ADD SPATIAL KEY`, `DROP KEY`), `ADD UNIQUE INDEX`, `ADD FULLTEXT INDEX`, `ADD SPATIAL` without either word, and a foreign key added without a `CONSTRAINT` name did not classify, and one such hand-written statement left its whole migration undetermined, so nothing in it was checked. They now classify as the grammar's forms do: an enum change written as `MODIFY COLUMN` reaches `MY.L4.ENUM_CHANGE`, and an unnamed foreign key carries its columns and the table it references. The same spellings as a later action of an `ALTER TABLE` were silently dropped from it, among them the `ADD UNIQUE KEY` in the remedy `MY.L5.FK_NULLABLE_IN_UNIQUE` suggests; they are judged too.

- **Every column of a multi-column drop is judged, and a redefinition anywhere in a MySQL action list reaches its rules.** Laravel drops several columns in one statement: `dropColumn(['a', 'b'])` and `dropTimestamps()` emit `drop a, drop b`. A later action of the same kind as the first was left out, so `MY.L1.DROP_COLUMN`, `PG.L1.DROP_COLUMN` and the deploy-window rules reported the first column only and named only it in their fix. Each column is now reported with the fix for its own column, and a finding about the whole statement, such as a missing lock timeout, is still reported once. On MySQL the redefinition rules read only the first clause of a statement, so `ADD COLUMN x INT, MODIFY y BIGINT` never reached `MY.L2.COPY_ALTER_TYPE` and the second of two `MODIFY` clauses never reached `MY.L4.ENUM_CHANGE`. Each now reads the clause of the column it judges, and the position clause of a later action is no longer taken for the redefinition's own.

- **`MY.L3.ALGORITHM_LOCK_UNEXPRESSIBLE` names the schema builder's modifiers instead of saying the builder cannot emit the clauses.** The finding, its fix material and its page said Laravel's MySQL grammar has no way to emit `ALGORITHM=` or `LOCK=`, and sent every reader to a raw statement. The grammar emits `ALGORITHM=INSTANT` for a column with `->instant()` from Laravel 12.40.1, `LOCK=` with `->lock()` from 12.46.0, and `ALGORITHM=INPLACE` for an index, a primary key or a foreign key with `->inplace()` from 13.33.0, and ignores a modifier it does not have without a word. The finding now says so, and since the rule reads the statement a migration sends, it goes away once a clause is really in it. The comment in the `laravel_snippet` of its `algorithm_lock_hint` fix changed with it, and its page gives the downtime class as derived, which is what a finding of this rule carries.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` judges MySQL's `sql_mode` and `lock_wait_timeout` by what the migration runs under.** `DEPLOY.CONTEXT.SETTING` read the server's global values and said a migration was about to run under them. Laravel sets `sql_mode` on every connection from its `strict` and `modes` keys, so a project with `'strict' => true` on a server that is not strict was stopped over truncation its migrations cannot cause, and one with `'strict' => false` on a strict server went through. `SQL_MODE_NOT_STRICT` now judges the value the migration connection runs with and names the server's beside it. `LOCK_WAIT_TIMEOUT_UNBOUNDED` still reads the server's value, since Laravel does not set that one, and stops the deploy only when a pending statement takes a metadata lock on an existing table before its session sets a `lock_wait_timeout` of at most an hour, or when not every pending migration could be captured. A migration that follows `MY.L3.MISSING_LOCK_WAIT_TIMEOUT` is no longer stopped by the server default; the value is reported as a note instead.

- **The MySQL metadata-lock check no longer passes when the instrument that would see a blocker is off.** `performance_schema.metadata_locks` is filled by the `wait/lock/metadata/sql/mdl` instrument, which can be switched off at run time while `performance_schema` stays on; the table then answers with an empty set. The activity reading asked only for `@@performance_schema`, so it took that empty set for a server nobody was locking, and `DEPLOY.PREFLIGHT.METADATA_LOCK_BLOCKER` passed over a lock it could not see. The reading now asks for the instrument in the same query and names it as a gap when it is off or not listed, and the check answers `undetermined`, with the statement that switches it back on.

- **MySQL shadow mode runs the migrations with the server's foreign key checks, not the dump replay's.** The replay switches `foreign_key_checks` and `unique_checks` off, because `mysqldump` writes a child table before its parent, and the migrations then ran on the same session. With the checks off MySQL drops a table another one references and adds a foreign key to a table that does not exist, both of which the deploy refuses, so a shadow run passed a migration the deploy fails. The session is replaced after the replay, and such a migration now fails in shadow mode as it does on deploy.

- **MySQL shadow mode no longer takes an account whose grants reach only its own database for one that may create the throwaway database.** The privilege check looked for `ALL PRIVILEGES`, `CREATE` and `DROP` anywhere in a `SHOW GRANTS` line, whatever database the line names, so the usual application grant `GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON app.*` passed it, and so did `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLES, DROP ROLE`. `CREATE DATABASE` then failed, and the run ended in an exception carrying the connection's details instead of `undetermined`. The check now reads the level and the whole privilege names of each line and asks about the name the run is about to create, which the documented `sqlens\_shadow\_%` grant still covers; it also reads `partial_revokes`, under which that pattern is literal. A server that refuses `CREATE DATABASE` anyway now ends the run as `undetermined` with `shadow_insufficient_privileges`.

- **`SEC.AUTH.ROLE_WILDCARD_HOST` and `SEC.AUTH.ROLE_WILDCARD_HOST_PRIVILEGED` no longer call an address range or an underscore "any host".** Both read every `%` or `_` in a MySQL host as an open host, so `'admin'@'10.0.0.%'`, the narrowing their own advice recommends, was a high finding, which the `ci` profile blocks on, and `db_host.internal` read as reachable from anywhere. MySQL matches an IP wildcard value only against IP addresses, so an address pattern is now a range and is not reported, and neither is an underscore without a `%`. A pattern over host names such as `%.example.com` is reported once, at medium, with its own sentence, whatever the account holds; `%` and a pattern of nothing but wildcards and dots remain the open host. An account whose only far-reaching privilege is `REPLICATION SLAVE` stays on the high rule and is now told what it can read instead of being told to drop `SUPER` and `FILE`. The examples use CIDR notation, since the MySQL manual lists wildcards in a host value as deprecated.

- **MySQL shadow mode rebuilds a schema whose view reads a view that sorts after it.** `mysqldump` writes each view first as a stand-in selecting constants and replaces the stand-ins at the end, and both the stand-ins and the drops in front of the final views are whole version-gated comments such as `/*!50001 CREATE VIEW v AS SELECT 1 AS id*/`. The dump reader skipped every such comment as session state, so the final view was created before the view it reads existed, and every shadow run and drift reference for that schema ended as `undetermined` with `shadow_mysql_replay_failed`. A whole-statement directive is now read by what it contains: a view, trigger, routine, event or table of the dumped schema is replayed, a `SET` is still skipped as session state, and anything else, such as a `DROP DATABASE`, `FLUSH` or `ANALYZE`, is skipped with a reason of its own.

- **A MySQL literal with a backslash-escaped quote in it canonicalizes as one literal, so `MY.L2.NO_PRIMARY_KEY` no longer flags a table that has a primary key and a column comment such as `the customer's note`.** Three canonicalization stages each ended a literal at `\'`, while MySQL escapes a delimiter with a backslash and Laravel writes `->comment()` through `addslashes()`. The rest of the statement then read as a literal that never closes: its keywords stayed lower case, a `PRIMARY KEY` went unseen, an `ALTER TABLE` action behind the literal went missing, and the text inside the literal was rewritten. The stages now share one scanner that asks the driver whether a backslash escapes, as the literal mask already did; PostgreSQL, where it does not, is unchanged. **The canonical form version moves to 6**, and with it every statement fingerprint, because the version is part of the hash. The baseline file moves to schema version 3 in this same release, so a project regenerates its baseline once.

- **`#[SqlensAllowDestructive]` and `sqlens.allow_destructive` work on MySQL, and for `DROP SCHEMA` on PostgreSQL.** The opt-in consented to a fixed list of PostgreSQL rule ids, so `MY.L1.DROP_TABLE`, `MY.L1.DROP_COLUMN`, `MY.L1.TRUNCATE`, `MY.L4.DROP_WITHOUT_DEPLOY_WINDOW` and `PG.L1.DROP_SCHEMA` stayed visible and blocking however the migration consented, although each of them tells its reader to consent exactly that way. All five are in the family now, and every shipped rule that advises the opt-in is checked to be in it.

- **`MY.L6.PK_NOT_BIGINT` names a `MEDIUMINT` primary key by its type and its real ceiling.** The canonical column type folded MySQL's `mediumint` into `integer`, so a key that runs out at 8,388,607, or 16,777,215 unsigned, was reported as an `integer` key with room up to 2,147,483,647 or 4,294,967,295: 256 times the room it has. `mediumint` now keeps its own name, which the rule's ceiling table already knew.

- **Splitting a schema dump and canonicalizing a long statement take time linear in their length.** All five scanners copied the rest of their input at every step, so the cost grew with the square of one input's length: a 1.1 MB MySQL schema dump took 2.3 s to split before shadow mode could start, and a 446 KB statement 2.0 s to canonicalize. They now compare in place, and the splitter checks for an empty statement only once a `DELIMITER` has matched. On the same machine the dump now splits in 0.3 s and the statement canonicalizes in 0.5 s, with unchanged output.

- **`PG.L2.CONSTRAINT_NOT_VALIDATED` flags a constraint added to a table that the same migration filled from a query, however the query is written.** Such a table has its rows from birth, so the validating scan reads all of them under the lock. Only `CREATE TABLE … AS SELECT` counted as filled, so a table created `AS TABLE s`, `AS (SELECT …)`, `AS VALUES`, `AS WITH … SELECT` or `AS EXECUTE` passed as born empty, and its foreign key or `CHECK` drew no finding. A table created from a query `WITH NO DATA` has no rows, and a constraint added to it no longer draws one.

- **An audit names every object the way the database stores it, so `PG.L8.NAMING_SNAKE_CASE` reports a mixed-case name in the catalog.** The PostgreSQL reader folded the names it read back to lower case, although PostgreSQL stores a quoted name as it was written: a table created as `"Orders"` with a column `"createdAt"` was reported as `public.orders` and `createdat`, the naming rule never reported either, and two tables `orders` and `"Orders"` read as one. On MySQL with `lower_case_table_names = 1` the reader also folded column, index, constraint, trigger and event names, although that setting folds only database and table names. An audit ignore entry or a drift exclude that names such an object in the folded spelling needs the spelling the database shows.

- **`PG.L2.SET_NOT_NULL_SCAN` reads every column name PostgreSQL accepts, and its remediation quotes the column the way the migration does.** The rule read only names made of lower-case ASCII letters, digits and underscores, so `SET NOT NULL` on `"2fa_enabled"`, `élan` or `"first name"` drew no finding, and the named `ADD CONSTRAINT … NOT NULL` form read `"first name"` as `first`. The remediation wrote the column unquoted, so for `"Email"` its first step named the column `email`. A `CHECK (… IS NOT NULL)` prepared for such a column now excuses it as it does for any other.

- **The remediation of `PG.L2.INDEX_NOT_CONCURRENT` builds the index the migration asked for, only concurrently.** Its build step was reassembled from the index name, the table and the key columns, so `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX` came back as a complete-looking `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` without `UNIQUE`, which accepts duplicates. A method, an `INCLUDE` list, a partial index's `WHERE`, an expression and `IF NOT EXISTS` were dropped the same way, and all but the last left a `{{columns}}` placeholder. The step is now the migration's own statement with `CONCURRENTLY` added after `INDEX`.

- **A migration that only validates a constraint no longer draws `PG.L3.MISSING_LOCK_TIMEOUT`, `PG.L3.MISSING_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT` or the lock-blocker deploy check.** `ALTER TABLE … VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` was counted with the ACCESS EXCLUSIVE forms of `ALTER TABLE`, so the migration that follows the recommended `NOT VALID` step was told that every statement wanting the table queues up behind it, and the deploy check reported a long-running session in front of it. It takes SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE, which no read and no write waits behind. `PG.L3.RISKY_OPS_SINGLE_TX` no longer counts it either. A statement that validates and does something else still counts.

- **The column definitions of a `CREATE TABLE` reach the rule layer in a real run.** Since 0.15.0 the body was read only where a column name still carried its quotes, and the canonicalization that runs before the reading removes every quote a name does not need, so on either engine a `CREATE TABLE` arrived without its columns and a rule asking what a column is got no answer. A body member that opens with `LIKE`, or with an unnamed `CHECK` on MySQL or `EXCLUDE` on PostgreSQL, still leaves the whole body unread rather than half-read.

- **`PG.L4.IDENTIFIER_LENGTH` checks the columns a `CREATE TABLE` defines, which is where `Schema::create()` makes most columns.** It read only a statement's targets and key columns, so a column name over PostgreSQL's 63-byte limit in `Schema::create()` drew no finding while the server truncated it and said so only in a NOTICE. Its fix material, which gives an index or a constraint a name of its own, is now offered only for those: a finding about a table or a column name carries none, where it used to suggest the index call.

- **The lock-blocker deploy check reports the lock each statement really takes.** Adding a foreign key takes SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE, but a session that had only read the table was reported in front of it as if the statement took ACCESS EXCLUSIVE and every read would queue. Such a session is no longer reported, and one that wrote to the table is reported with the lock the statement takes, which stops writes and lets reads through. A `DROP INDEX` that is not concurrent takes ACCESS EXCLUSIVE on the index's table and was not looked at at all, because it names only the index. The check now asks the catalog for that table.

- **Changing a maintenance storage parameter of a table no longer counts as a strong lock.** `ALTER TABLE … SET (fillfactor = …)`, `RESET`, and the same for `toast_tuple_target`, `parallel_workers` and the vacuum and autovacuum settings take SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE, which no read and no write waits behind, but `PG.L3.MISSING_LOCK_TIMEOUT`, `PG.L3.MISSING_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT`, `PG.L3.RISKY_OPS_SINGLE_TX` and the lock-blocker deploy check treated them like every other `ALTER TABLE`. Any other parameter, `user_catalog_table` for one, still counts.

- **The freeze horizon deploy check measures each table against the threshold autovacuum uses, and covers index builds and drops.** A table's own `autovacuum_freeze_max_age` or `autovacuum_multixact_freeze_max_age` in `reloptions` can only move its horizon closer, but the check held every table against the cluster's setting, so such a table read as further from its horizon than it was. The threshold is now the lower of the two. `CREATE INDEX` and `DROP INDEX`, concurrent or not, were left out on the grounds that they run beside a vacuum; the SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE an anti-wraparound worker holds conflicts with the lock each of them takes, so they wait behind it and are now checked, a dropped index through the table it belongs to.

- **The deploy check on `lock_timeout` judges the role the migrations run as, and lets through migrations that bound their own locks.** PostgreSQL's `reset_val` answers for the role that reads it, and the preflight reads through an account of its own, so a default stored with `ALTER ROLE … SET` on the migration role stayed invisible and the deploy stopped, while the same default on the preflight account cleared `DEPLOY.CONTEXT.SETTING.LOCK_TIMEOUT_UNBOUNDED` for migrations that still waited without a bound. The check now reads the stored defaults in `pg_db_role_setting`: the migration role's own, then the database's or every role's, then the server's value, and it answers `undetermined` when a default on the reading account hides the one it needs. A pending migration that sets `lock_timeout` before its first strong lock, as `PG.L3.MISSING_LOCK_TIMEOUT` asks, no longer stops the deploy: the value is reported as an `info` note, as MySQL's `lock_wait_timeout` already was. A `SET LOCAL` counts only inside its migration's transaction, and a zero, `DEFAULT` or a value that rounds to zero counts as no bound.

- **The fix material of `PG.L5.COLLATION_VERSION_MISMATCH` refreshes the database's own collation with `ALTER DATABASE` and lists the indexes that sort under it.** For the database collation, the case a glibc or ICU upgrade produces, the first step searched `pg_depend`, where PostgreSQL records no dependency on the pinned default collation, and the last step was `ALTER COLLATION`, which does not accept a database. The database's material now lists every index with a key column in the default collation, read from `pg_index.indcollation`, and ends with `ALTER DATABASE … REFRESH COLLATION VERSION`.
