For an explicitly created collation the first step resolves the schema-qualified name the finding carries with `regcollation`; compared as a bare name inserted into the query, it was not a valid value.

- **A `DELIMITER` line or a psql command that follows a comment is read as one.** The splitter took a comment for statement content, so after `-- helpers`, a comment ending the line before, or a block comment, a MySQL `DELIMITER` was glued into the next statement and the routine behind it was cut at the first semicolon of its body. The same happened to a psql command such as the `\restrict` that `pg_dump` writes after its header comments. Comments and blank space no longer count as content.

- **The PostgreSQL shadow mode builds its template from the file `php artisan schema:dump` writes.** That file is `pg_dump` output written for `psql`: current `pg_dump` wraps each run in the psql commands `\restrict` and `\unrestrict`, and Laravel appends the rows of the `migrations` table as a `COPY … FROM stdin` block. Replayed as one text, it built no template, and every shadow run on PostgreSQL ended `undetermined` with `shadow_template_build_failed`. The file is now read the way psql reads it: the psql commands and the rows are skipped, every other statement is replayed on its own, and the template holds the structure and no rows. The run never needed the `migrations` rows, because it calls each pending migration directly. A block of rows that never reaches its terminator is reported as `shadow_pgsql_schema_dump_missing`, before anything is created.

- **`PG.L2.TYPE_CHANGE_REWRITE` reports a `numeric` or `decimal` target with a precision as `undetermined` instead of a rewrite.** The type-change matrix dropped the precision and classified every such target as a full-table rewrite, so `$table->decimal('total', 12, 2)->change()`, the ordinary way a money column grows, drew a blocking finding with a work order attached. On PostgreSQL 18.4, growing the precision at the same scale keeps the table as it is and changing the scale rewrites it, and the statement holds only the target, so the rule now says it cannot tell. A bare `numeric` target is still read as a rewrite.

- **The PostgreSQL type-change rules read Laravel's `->change()` as the question it is.** `->change()` names the column's type on every call, so flipping the nullability or the default of a `bigint`, `integer`, `boolean` or `uuid` column drew a blocking `PG.L2.TYPE_CHANGE_REWRITE` finding with a six-step plan, and an `integer` column a `PG.L4.TYPE_NARROWING` finding about truncation. On PostgreSQL 18.4, restating the type a column already has rewrites nothing. A statement that sets the same column's nullability beside its `TYPE` is now `undetermined` on the rewrite axis, without a plan, and the narrowing rule claims nothing for it. A `TYPE` clause on its own, or one with `USING`, is reported as before.

- **The host-based authentication checks say what they read: the file on disk, not the rules the server has loaded.** All six claimed the opposite, and `SEC.AUTH.HBA_PARSE_ERROR` built on it, saying that a broken line authenticates nobody and the next matching line decides who connects. On PostgreSQL 18.4, `pg_hba_file_rules` shows an edit before any reload, and a file holding a broken line is not loaded at all: a reload is refused while the previous rules stay in force, and a restart fails. The finding, its page and the empty-reading verdict now say so, and the page of `SEC.AUTH.HBA_TRUST` notes that a fix shows up in the view before the reload puts it in force.

- **The MySQL copy rules leave a statement alone once it pins `LOCK=NONE`, the form both of them recommend.** `MY.L2.COPY_ALTER_TYPE` and `MY.L2.COPY_ALTER_CHARSET` told a reader to issue the change with `ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE` so the server refuses rather than copies, and then reported that statement as a blocking copy, the shipped example of the charset rule included. A statement pinned with `LOCK=NONE`, or with `ALGORITHM=INPLACE` or `INSTANT` and no lock level that holds writes back, either runs while writes go on or is refused, and neither rule reports it any more. `ALGORITHM=COPY` and `LOCK=SHARED` are still reported.

- **`MY.L3.MIXED_DDL_DML_NOT_ATOMIC` no longer counts a session setting as a schema change.** A data migration that opened with `SET SESSION lock_wait_timeout`, the line `MY.L3.MISSING_LOCK_WAIT_TIMEOUT` asks every migration to open with, was reported as a schema change followed by a write, with a message about an implicit commit that `SET` never causes. A schema change after the setting is still reported.

- **The shipped good examples are migrations the package's own rules pass at the example's level.** `explain_rule` hands a good example to an agent as the way to write the migration, and thirteen of them drew a finding from a rule at or below their own level. `PG.L5.FK_NO_INDEX` recommended a plain index and an unvalidated key on a table that already holds rows, which `PG.L2.INDEX_NOT_CONCURRENT` and `PG.L2.CONSTRAINT_NOT_VALIDATED` report; its pair is now a table the migration creates, and the note names `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` for a table with rows. `PG.L4.TYPE_NARROWING` recommended widening to `bigint`, which rewrites the table, and now shows the expand and contract its own fix material describes. The two PostgreSQL enum examples add their `CHECK` with `NOT VALID` and validate it in a later migration. The expand and contract examples back-fill in a migration of their own with a `WHERE`, and name the opt-in their contract drop needs, as `PG.L1.DROP_SCHEMA` now does. Two MySQL examples gain the primary key their tables lacked, `PG.L9.TYPE_IMPLICIT_CAST` indexes the key it creates, the `GRANT PROXY` pair grants to a named host rather than `%`, and the two `down()` examples show the `up()` they undo.

- **`PG.L2.SET_NOT_NULL_SCAN` reports a `SET NOT NULL` that no valid check on its table proves.** The rule stayed silent as soon as a `CHECK (col IS NOT NULL)` for a column of that name appeared anywhere in the migration, while PostgreSQL skips its scan only for a valid check on the same table: a check on another table, one added `NOT VALID` and never validated, and one added after the `SET NOT NULL` all left the table scanned under `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` behind a clean report. The check now has to constrain the same column of the same table and be valid before the statement, read from the migration's statements in their order. `PG.L4.CHECK_ENUM_CHANGE` had the same blind spot the other way round: a constraint dropped on any table made a first enum column read as a change, and now only a drop on the same table does. `MigrationContext::hasNotNullCheckFor()` and the `columnsWithNotNullCheck` and `dropsAConstraint` properties are removed, which breaks a custom rule that read them; the ordered `statements` carry the table and the order they could not.

- **The PostgreSQL timeout rules count the statements outside `ALTER TABLE` that block reads or writes.** `PG.L3.MISSING_LOCK_TIMEOUT` and `PG.L3.MISSING_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT` read every statement the classifier gave no kind of its own as taking no lock, so a migration that moved an index to another tablespace, created or dropped a trigger or a policy, replaced or dropped a view, altered a sequence or dropped a schema with `CASCADE` drew no finding without a timeout, while PostgreSQL holds ACCESS EXCLUSIVE or SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE for each. Those forms are now read one by one, as measured on PostgreSQL 18.4, and the forms measured as harmless, such as `COMMENT ON`, `GRANT` or `CREATE FUNCTION`, stay uncounted. A trigger, policy or rule on a table the same migration creates stays silent, like any other lock on that table.

- **`sqlens:audit` refuses a reading whose default scope resolves to no schema, instead of reporting a clean run over nothing.** With `sqlens.catalog.schemas` empty the audit reads the schemas the session resolves. On PostgreSQL `current_schemas(false)` leaves out every schema the reading role has no `USAGE` on, so the account the documentation recommends, reading an application whose tables live outside `public`, resolved no schema at all; on MySQL a connection configured with an empty `database` opens without a current database. Either way the reading covered nothing and reported itself complete. It now stops with an error naming what the session resolved and which schemas exist, as a mistyped schema already did, and the audit role page says how to name the schemas or grant `USAGE`.

- **An object the audit leaves out on purpose no longer fails a strict run.** The objects an extension installs are left out of the catalog reading by default, and each was reported as an undetermined `AUDIT.CATALOG.UNREAD.EXCLUDED_BY_CONFIG` saying it was not read and was "unknown, not fine", so under the `ci` profile the audit of every application with `citext`, `pg_trgm` or `postgis` in its schema ended with exit code 3, although the package calls the exclusion a decision and not a gap. The notice keeps its id, reports as `not_applicable` with the reason `excluded_by_config`, and says it was left out on purpose; the report header no longer lists it as a part of the reading that is missing. A part of the reading that genuinely did not arrive is still undetermined and still fails a strict run.

- **A recorded debt whose standing the catalog cannot give is reported as `DEBT.STANDING_UNKNOWN`, no longer as an object the catalog does not show.** Every expand without its contract on PostgreSQL, every debt on MySQL and every debt whose catalog read failed was reported as `DEBT.OBJECT_NOT_FOUND`, whose message sends the reader after a dropped table, a schema outside the run's scope or a missing privilege, although the catalog had not been asked or had not answered. The new finding is undetermined as before and names why: `debt_standing_not_in_catalog` when no catalog question settles the debt, `catalog_read_failed` when the read failed. On PostgreSQL an expand debt is now asked about: while a table still carries the added column the question stays open, because the account does not name the column it replaces, and when no table carries it the object is reported as not found. A configuration that tolerates `debt_object_not_found` under `allow_undetermined` for these debts names the new reason instead.

- **The pre-scan holds back a migration that starts a process, sends a mail or runs code from outside the file with PHP's own means.** `exec()`, `shell_exec()` and a command in backticks, `system()`, `passthru()`, `proc_open()`, `popen()`, `pcntl_exec()`, `mail()` and `mb_send_mail()` reached the pretend run while the `Process` and `Mail` facades did not, so a lint run executed them for real. They are `CAP.PRESCAN.SIDE_EFFECT` now, and `include`, `require`, `eval()` and an object of a class named at run time are `CAP.PRESCAN.DYNAMIC_CALL`. With the preview tier enabled, `CAP.PRESCAN.INDIRECT_CALL` also reports the constructor of a project class wherever it stands, an argument included, because a constructor runs project code as a method does; an object built to be thrown, `stdClass` and `DateTimeZone` are not reported.

- **The configuration no longer promises that the pre-scan stops a call into your own code in a default run.** The comment beside `indirect_calls.allowlist` said the pre-scan flags a migration that reaches its effect through your own code, and the check that does it is `preview`, so under the shipped `'stability' => []` it never ran and pretend executed the call. The comment, the rule's page and the pre-scan notes now say so, and how to turn it on: `'stability' => ['preview']`, or shadow mode. A run whose header reads `admitted_stability: ["stable"]` did not run it.

- **A project checked out below a directory called `vendor` keeps its own migrations.** Whether a migration belongs to a package was decided by a `vendor` segment anywhere in its absolute path, so a checkout in a CI workspace such as `/builds/vendor/app` read every migration of the project as a package's: `sqlens:lint` reported `LINT.SKIPPED.ONLY_VENDOR_MIGRATIONS` instead of linting, and a password literal in the project's own migration passed the Critical rule. The dependency tree is now the vendor directory Composer installs into, a configured `vendor-dir` included, and any `vendor` directory below the project root.

- **MySQL shadow mode replays only what builds the schema, and refuses a dump that would reach past the disposable database.** The replay ran every statement of the dump on a session of the server the application's database lives on, so a dump in the `mysqldump --databases` form moved that session into the application's database with `USE` and ran the dump's `DROP TABLE IF EXISTS` there. `USE`, statements on a database, a `SET` of server-wide state, `GRANT` and account statements, an object named with its database, and any statement a schema dump does not build a schema with now refuse the whole dump before anything runs, as `undetermined` with `shadow_mysql_dump_refused` and the line. What `php artisan schema:dump` writes is unaffected, and so is a dump made with the MariaDB client, whose `/*M!` comments are read as comments.

- **A shadow run says which throwaway databases of an earlier, killed run it removed from the server.** The orphan sweep drops the shadow databases a killed run never dropped, and its report was discarded, so a lint run removed databases and its report did not mention them. The run now reports `LINT.SHADOW.ORPHANS`: a pass naming each database it removed, or undetermined (`shadow_orphans_remain`) naming what it found and could not remove, or saying that it could not list the shadow databases. A run the production guard blocked does not look, because it does not reach the server at all; the sweep's documentation no longer promises that it lists them.

- **Shadow mode's production guard judges the connection the throwaway databases are built on, not only the one being examined.** With `capture.shadow.connection` or `capture.shadow.direct_connection` set, shadow mode creates, fills and drops its databases on that connection, and the guard asked only about the connection under examination, so a shadow connection on a server that also holds production databases was never judged. A shadow or direct connection whose name or database reads as production now refuses the run as `production_connection`. The confirmation question of `sqlens:lint --shadow`, `sqlens:drift` and `sqlens:postdeploy --expect-shadow` names that connection as well when it is not the one being examined.

- **The runtime guards no longer write the values a statement carries into the log.** `unbound_raw_sql` reports exactly the statements whose text holds a literal where a binding belongs, and it logged that text as written, so the address or token it had just recognized reached the log that `include_bindings = false` keeps bindings out of. The slow-query and runtime-DDL guards logged literals the same way. Every literal in a logged statement is now its length, `'alice@example.com'` becoming `'<string(17)>'`, read with the engine's grammar on PostgreSQL and MySQL. On an engine SQLens has no grammar for, such as SQLite, everything from the first quote to the last is logged as `<masked>`.

- **`unbound_raw_sql` no longer reports the statements Laravel writes itself.** A JSON path such as `"meta"->>'email'` was read as a comparison with a literal, and every question of the schema builder, `Schema::hasTable()`, `getColumns()` and the rest, was logged as a security violation, because the builder writes the names it asks about as literals and packages ask on every boot. A JSON operator is no longer read as a comparison, and a statement the connection's own schema grammar compiles is recognized by its shape, on PostgreSQL and MySQL. A number concatenated into a statement is still not reported: the framework writes numbers into statements itself, the integer keys of every eager load among them.

- **A database error on a connection over a Unix socket no longer carries the socket path into a report.** From Laravel 13 on, a query exception on a connection configured with `unix_socket` names `Socket:` in place of `Host:` and `Port:`, and no redaction knew the segment, so the path of the directory the server runs in reached finding text, JSON, SARIF and CI annotations. The segment is redacted like the host, and a configured socket path is taken out of any text that quotes it.

- **With the privacy pack on, finding the application's models no longer loads files that are not models.** On an application without `app/Models`, every PHP file under `app/` was handed to the autoloader under a name guessed from its path, and the autoloader includes that file: a script under `app/` ran, and a helper file that Composer's `files` entry had already included was included a second time, which ends every SQLens command with a fatal error. A file is now read first and loaded only when it declares the class its path names.

- **A privacy dictionary of the project's own no longer loses terms without saying so.** A term added through `extra_terms` joins the `identifiers` group, and a dictionary of the project's own that did not declare that group gave it no signal, so a column the project had named was never reported. The group is now declared with the strong signal where the dictionary lacks it. A term naming a group the file does not declare, a misspelled `Pii` for instance, was skipped; the file is now refused, with the term and the group named.

- **`SEC.PRIV.RUNTIME_DDL` no longer reports the deploy account's rights when the audit runs over the migration connection.** The rule reads grants to the runtime role, and that role is the account the audit connected as. Over the migration connection it is the deploy's account, whose own grants were reported as the runtime role's, with a `REVOKE` for exactly what the next migration needs. The rule now judges grants only on a run over the connection `security.runtime_connection` names. A run without `--connection` counts as a run over the connection `sqlens.connection` pins, where it used to count as one that named no connection.

- **A guard profile with a misspelled key or log level is refused when the application boots.** The config validator refuses both, but it runs only inside a `sqlens:*` command, and the guard is armed at every boot. A misspelled key, `lazy_loadng` for instance, left its guardrail off, and a level such as `warn` made the logger throw inside the guard, so every violation without a severity of its own was lost. An unknown key, a level PSR-3 does not name and a section that is not a map of keys now stop the boot with a message naming them, as an unknown log channel already did.

- **The MCP server no longer hands a failing tool's error text to the client.** With `app.debug` on, which is Laravel's default where an MCP client runs, a tool that failed was answered with its exception's message, and a database error's message carries the connection's host, port and database and the statement with its values. The answer now names the kind of failure and, for a database error, its SQLSTATE; the whole error goes to the application's log. The server also runs with `app.debug` off, so a malformed request is answered as an error instead of ending the session.

- **The MCP tools run under the profile the command would.** `lint_pending` accepted a `profile` and ignored it, and `lint_pending`, `lint_shadow` and `predeploy` all ran on the base configuration while their run header named the configured profile. A project whose `ci` profile raises the level got a stricter verdict from `sqlens:lint --profile=ci` than from `lint_pending` with `profile: "ci"`, and `predeploy` ignored the project's `profiles.predeploy` section. The tools now choose the profile in the command's order, the parameter where there is one, then `SQLENS_PROFILE`, then `sqlens.profile`, then the command's own default, and apply its settings for that call only. A name that is not a profile is answered `undetermined` with `refusal.id` set to `unknown_profile` instead of running on a laxer one. `lint_pending` now accepts every name `--profile` accepts, including a profile the project has no section for.

- **Reports are written exactly as they were produced.** The JSON and SARIF reports, `sqlens:format --format=json` and `sqlens:doctor --format=json` wrote their document through the console formatter, which reads `\<` as an escaped bracket and `<info>` as a style. A finding quoting an ordinary CHECK constraint such as `!~ '[\<\>]'`, or a file name or tool path with the same characters, left a document no JSON parser reads, and a name shaped like a style tag lost its tags, so the report named another object. The console, GitHub and agent reports and the annotations and console lines of `sqlens:format` and `sqlens:doctor` now print that text as it is as well, and show a control character in it as a visible escape such as `\x1B`, so an escape sequence in a table name no longer acts on the terminal or the CI log.

- **Fix material writes each value for the place it fills.** A value measured from a migration or the catalog was put into its template as it was, also inside a quoted literal. The PostgreSQL enum material for the label `don't_know` was SQL no server accepts, and for a label that closes the literal it was two statements; run on PostgreSQL 18.4, the second one dropped a table. A table name with a quote broke the Laravel snippet of the batched backfill and of the MySQL algorithm clause in the same way. A value inside a quoted SQL literal now has its quotes doubled, and one inside a string of a `laravel_snippet` gets the escapes that PHP string needs. A value with a backslash that would land inside an SQL literal stays a placeholder, because MySQL and a PostgreSQL server with `standard_conforming_strings` off read the backslash as an escape.

- **A baseline entry accepts only the finding it describes.** An entry suppressed a finding by its fingerprint alone, while its `rule_id`, `category` and `severity`, the fields a reviewer reads, could say anything. An entry edited to read `convention` hid the finding it matched, with no run reporting the mismatch. An entry whose rule, category or severity differs from the finding it matches now suppresses nothing: the finding stays visible and carries a sentence naming what the entry records, and the entry is not reported as stale. `sqlens:baseline --update` rewrites such an entry from the finding and names each one it rewrote, so the diff shows what is really being accepted. An entry written by `sqlens:baseline` always matches its finding; one that outlived a release that re-rated its rule now shows its finding again until `--update` rewrites it.

- **URL-configured connections are read the way the framework connects through them, in three more places.** The Postgres Language Server was pointed at a connection's raw keys: a connection configured by `url` alone named no host, so the tool did not run, and one that kept Laravel's defaults beside its `url` sent it to `127.0.0.1`. On a read/write split it also ignored the one read host the audit was pinned to. It now connects to the server the audit read. `SEC.PRIV.CONNECTIONS_UNSEPARATED` compared the raw `username`, `host` and `database` of the runtime and migration connections, so two accounts behind two URLs were reported as one identity, and one account behind two URLs went unreported. It now compares what the URLs name, and the migration connection as the framework migrates through it. And an audit over a URL-configured connection no longer reports `CAP.L0.INSTANCE_DIVERGENCE` because Laravel's default port and database stand beside the `url`.

- **A statement a deploy check proposes names the object it found and nothing else.** Five checks propose SQL for what they report: `DEPLOY.LEGACY.INVALID_INDEX` and `DEPLOY.LEGACY.INVALID_INDEX_NAME_COLLISION` a `DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY`, `DEPLOY.LEGACY.CONSTRAINT_NOT_VALIDATED` a `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT`, `DEPLOY.LEGACY.ORPHAN_TRANSITION_OBJECT` and `DEPLOY.LEGACY.OSC_ARTIFACT` a `DROP`, and `DEPLOY.LEGACY.CONSTRAINT_NOT_ENFORCED` an `ALTER CHECK … ENFORCED`. Each wrote the catalog's names into the statement as they were, and a name is whatever the role that created the object chose: an index named `x; DROP TABLE users; --` turned the proposal into two statements, and psql runs the second one even when the first fails. Every name in a proposed statement is now quoted, part by part, with the quote character doubled inside it, so it can only name that object. The proposal now also reaches names it could not reach before: a `MixedCase` name that PostgreSQL would fold, and a `#sql-` table, whose unquoted `#` MySQL reads as the start of a comment. On MySQL the statement stands in a doubled backtick span, so the backticks of a quoted name do not end it. The name in the finding's own sentence and the location a baseline entry matches are unchanged.

- **`sealed_by: privilege` is reported only when the account's grants were read and allow nothing but reading.** Every catalog reading proves its read-only seal with a write that must be refused, and that write creates a temporary table. MySQL checks the grant before the read-only flag, so an application account with `INSERT`, `UPDATE` and `DELETE` and without the right to create temporary tables had its probe refused by the grant and was reported as sealed by its grants, while a reviewer checking those grants found write privileges. The refusal was also taken as the whole proof: with the session's read-only flag off, the reading went ahead on a session that accepted an `INSERT`, on MySQL and on PostgreSQL alike. After such a refusal the reading now lists the account's own grants on MySQL and reports `privilege` only when every privilege in them only reads; otherwise it reads the transaction's read-only flag from the server and reports `session` when the flag is on. When neither proves the seal, the reading stops with an error. PostgreSQL asks for the read-only mode before the grant, so a sealed audit there still says `session`.

- **The MySQL privilege preflight reads each grant with the account and the object it applies to.** `DEPLOY.PREFLIGHT.MISSING_PRIVILEGE` read the privilege views as one set of names for the whole user name, so an `ALTER` on `app.audit_log` answered for `ALTER TABLE app.orders`, and the grants of `'deploy'@'localhost'` answered for `'deploy'@'%'`, a separate account. The gate then passed a deploy whose `migrate --force` stopped at that statement. A requirement is now met by a global grant, by a database-level grant whose name reaches the object's database, or by a table-level grant on that table, with `_` and `%` read as wildcards unless the server runs with `partial_revokes`. When the accounts of one user name disagree, the object is reported `undetermined` with each account named, because which one `migrate` becomes depends on the host it connects from. And a user name the views show no account for is reported as what it is: the views are filtered to the reading connection, which sees nobody else's grants without `SELECT` on the `mysql` schema. That case used to be reported as a role question.

- **`SEC.PRIV.CONNECTIONS_UNSEPARATED` names the two connections and no longer prints who they authenticate as.** When two connection names shared one identity, the finding printed it as `user@host/database`, taken from the configuration, into the console, JSON, SARIF and GitHub annotations: the database user, an often internal host name and the database name. The identity is now compared as a digest, and the sentence says that the two connections share one user, host and database without naming them.

- **`SEC.PRIV.ROUTINE_DEFINER_UNSAFE_PATH` reports a pinned search path that does not end with `pg_temp`, and no routine advice recommends leaving it out any more.** PostgreSQL searches the caller's temporary schema for tables, views and types before the path unless the path names it last, so a SECURITY DEFINER routine pinned to `app`, `pg_catalog` or the empty path reads a caller's temporary table as if it were its own, with its owner's rights. The rule reported only a `pg_temp` placed early, and its own advice, like that of `SEC.PRIV.ROUTINE_DEFINER_MUTABLE_PATH` and `SEC.PRIV.ROUTINE_DEFINER_NO_PATH_IN_MIGRATION`, recommended exactly those paths. It now reports every pinned path whose last entry is not `pg_temp`, describes the substitution as a table's rather than a function's, because PostgreSQL never searches `pg_temp` for functions, and every finding, page and example ends the path with `pg_temp`. SECURITY DEFINER routines already pinned without it are reported at `medium` from this version on.

- **A reader session that could not prove it is read-only is probed once per run, and the deploy commands name it.** The read-only probe ran again on every read, so a session whose seal did not take accepted one write per check, each rolled back: seven in a `sqlens:predeploy` run and four in `sqlens:postdeploy` on PostgreSQL 18.4. Every check reported the refusal as an ordinary `catalog_read_failed`, and `sqlens:audit` probed twice before it stopped. The session now keeps its refusal and does not probe again. `sqlens:audit` ends on the first one, and in `sqlens:predeploy`, `sqlens:postdeploy` and the MCP `predeploy` tool the check that met it and every check after it answer with the new undetermined reason `reader_session_unsealed` without reading.

- **The production-only logging rules ask where the server runs only when the log is on.** `SEC.CFG.GENERAL_LOG`, `SEC.CFG.GENERAL_LOG_PERSONAL_DATA` and `SEC.CFG.STATEMENT_LOGGING` asked about the environment before they looked at the value. With the log off, which is the engine default, a run outside production reported a pass saying the log was set and what it writes down, and a run whose `app.env` this package does not recognize reported `undetermined`, which the `ci` profile turns into a failed build. The value is now judged first: a log that is off is no finding in any environment, and the environment decides only for a log that is on.

- **A role that can reach a dangerous attribute is reported with the path to the role that holds it.** `SEC.PRIV.ROLE_SUPERUSER`, `SEC.PRIV.ROLE_CREATEROLE` and `SEC.PRIV.ROLE_BYPASSRLS` printed the first membership chain the account had, whichever role was at its end. An account that was a member of a harmless role created before its superuser role was told to revoke the membership of the harmless one, which left the route to the superuser open. The finding now names the chain to every role that holds the attribute itself, shortest first.

- **A membership granted `WITH SET FALSE` no longer counts as a way to a role's attributes.** `SEC.PRIV.ROLE_SUPERUSER`, `SEC.PRIV.ROLE_CREATEROLE`, `SEC.PRIV.ROLE_BYPASSRLS` and the list of accounts that bypass row-level security walked every membership. Since PostgreSQL 16 a grant carries its ADMIN, INHERIT and SET options separately, and `SET ROLE` follows only a chain of SET TRUE grants, so an account given a superuser role `WITH SET FALSE`, the hardening meant for exactly this, was reported as able to become a superuser with one `SET ROLE`. The attributes and paths now come from such chains only. The reachable roles that the questions about ownership and granted rights read still cover every membership.

- **A membership without INHERIT and without SET no longer makes an account the owner of a table.** The row-level security rules treat the account the audit connects as like the table's owner when it can use the owner's rights, and every membership counted for that. A grant with neither option gives no rights and no `SET ROLE`, and PostgreSQL applies row-level security to the member. It is also the grant a `CREATEROLE` role receives in every role it creates, so an account that had created the owner role was reported as bypassing the policies of the owner's tables. Only chains of SET grants followed by INHERIT grants count now, for this and for deciding which grants the runtime role holds.

- **Every statement a security finding hands over names its objects quoted and in the form the engine accepts.** `SEC.PRIV.GRANT_PUBLIC` printed `REVOKE … ON <name> FROM PUBLIC` without the kind of object, which PostgreSQL reads as a table, so on PostgreSQL 18.4 the statement failed for a schema, a routine, a database and a mixed-case table, five of six in one measured run. None of the eleven security findings that close with a statement quoted the names it took from the catalog, and those names are chosen by whoever may create an object: pasted into `psql`, the advice for a table named `x; drop table app.victim; --` dropped `app.victim`, and `ALTER TABLE app.Orders` switched on row-level security for the table `orders` instead. `SEC.PRIV.GRANT_OPTION` and `SEC.PRIV.GRANT_OPTION_STRUCTURAL` spelled PostgreSQL's `REVOKE GRANT OPTION FOR` on MySQL as well, where it is a syntax error. The readers now write each object as a GRANT or REVOKE names it, with its kind and every part quoted, the findings set the statement apart as code, and on MySQL they use `REVOKE GRANT OPTION ON`. A MySQL account whose name holds a quote is written with the quote doubled, and a grant on an overloaded PostgreSQL routine carries the routine's argument types, where the overloads used to be merged into one grant.

- **`SEC.PRIV.GRANT_SERVER_ADMIN` no longer reports the MySQL audit account for the privileges its setup asks for, and no longer repeats `FILE` and `PROCESS`.** An audit account set up the way the documentation says, with `SHOW_ROUTINE` and `PROCESS`, got a `high` finding about exactly those, which the shipped `security.min_severity` of `high` turns into a failed build. On the account the audit connects as, `PROCESS`, `REPLICATION CLIENT` and `SHOW_ROUTINE` are no longer reported by this rule; anything else that account holds still is. `FILE` and `PROCESS` have findings of their own, `SEC.PRIV.GRANT_FILE` and `SEC.PRIV.GRANT_PROCESS`, and one grant carrying them got a second finding from this rule; it now leaves them to those. A migration that grants them is still reported.

- **`SEC.RLS.NO_POLICY` no longer advises switching row-level security off.** The rule looks only at tables the project lists as holding tenant data, and it offered `DISABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` as the alternative to writing the missing policy. On such a table that turns a table nobody can read into one every role with `SELECT` reads in full, which `SEC.RLS.DISABLED` then reports at `high`. The finding now asks for the policy, and for a table that holds no tenant data after all, to take it out of `sqlens.security.rls.tables` first.

- **A permissive `USING (true)` base narrowed by a restrictive tenant policy is no longer reported as open.** `SEC.RLS.POLICY_ALWAYS_TRUE` reported the documented pattern of a permissive base with a restrictive tenant condition at `high`, and said the table was as exposed as one with no policy at all, which shows nothing. Measured on PostgreSQL 18.4, a tenant reads 1 of 3 rows through that pair and cannot write another tenant's row. Both always-true rules now leave a permissive policy alone when a restrictive policy with a real condition applies to every role and command it does, and report it as before when one does not, for example a restrictive policy only `FOR SELECT` or only for another role. `SEC.RLS.CHECK_ALWAYS_TRUE` also reports a write path that stays open while a restrictive policy narrows only the reads, and the sentence no longer reads "the policy policy".

- **A drift finding about a routine's body no longer prints the body.** `DEPLOY.DRIFT.DIVERGENT` printed both sides of a changed routine verbatim, and a routine body is where a catalog most often holds a credential: a key rotated directly in production went into the console, JSON, SARIF and the CI log twice, old and new. The body is still compared in full, and the finding now names each side by a short `sha256` digest.

- **`SEC.PRIV.ROUTINE_DEFINER_NO_PATH_IN_MIGRATION` reports a PostgreSQL SQL function written `LANGUAGE sql SECURITY DEFINER`.** The rule stands down on MySQL's `SQL SECURITY`, and in the commonest spelling of a PostgreSQL SQL function, `sql SECURITY` read as exactly that: a definer function or procedure in `LANGUAGE sql` without a pinned `search_path` got no finding, on one line or two. The language clause is now set aside before MySQL's spelling is looked for, and MySQL routines, with or without their own `LANGUAGE SQL`, are still left alone.

- **The injection rules read the subquery methods and Eloquent's `fromQuery()`.** `fromSub()`, `selectSub()`, `joinSub()`, `leftJoinSub()`, `rightJoinSub()`, `straightJoinSub()`, `crossJoinSub()`, `joinLateral()`, `leftJoinLateral()`, `insertUsing()` and `insertOrIgnoreUsing()` accept a string and put it into the statement as written, and `fromQuery()` runs a complete statement on the model's connection. None of them has `raw` in its name and none was in a list the analyse suite read, so `fromSub("select … {$tenant}", 'o')` and `Order::fromQuery("… '{$email}'")` produced no finding at all. `SEC.INJ.RAW_INTERPOLATION` now reports a runtime value in a string subquery, with advice to hand the method a closure or a builder instead, and in a `fromQuery()` statement, whether it is called statically on a model, on a builder, on a model instance or on a relation. `SEC.INJ.RAW_SQL_WITHOUT_REASON` asks for a reason for `fromQuery()`, so a project that calls it without `#[RawSql(reason: …)]` gets that finding on upgrade; a string subquery is a fragment, like `whereRaw()`, and is not asked about. A subquery given as a closure or a builder carries no raw text and is not looked at, and neither is a model's own `fromQuery()` method, a value object's named constructor of that name, or a receiver the analyzer cannot resolve.

- **`SEC.INJ.RAW_INTERPOLATION` reads the raw methods called statically on a model.** `Order::whereRaw("tenant_id = {$tenant}")` is the shortest way to write a raw condition in Eloquent, and the model hands it to a new query builder exactly as `Order::query()->whereRaw(…)` does. The second spelling was reported and the first was not, because the analyse suite read a static raw call only on the `DB` facade. Every `*Raw()` fragment method and every string subquery is now read on a model as well, by its name or as `self::`/`static::` inside it. A class that is not a model, a model that declares the method itself, and a class held in a variable are not read. `SEC.INJ.RAW_SQL_WITHOUT_REASON` does not ask about these calls, just as it does not ask about `whereRaw()` on a builder.

- **A guard profile you add takes the keys it leaves out from `production`, instead of switching those guardrails off.** A shipped profile published in part has always been filled from the shipped profile of the same name, but a profile of your own had nothing to be filled from, and the guard reads a switch that is not set as off. A `staging` profile naming only `strict.lazy_loading` therefore ran with runtime DDL, unbound raw SQL, slow queries and destructive commands unwatched, although every shipped profile watches them, and the configuration check listed each missing key as harmless. Such a profile now starts from the shipped `production` profile and keeps every key it sets, including a switch it turns off and the connections it names. A key still missing from a guard profile in the configuration in use, for example in a cached configuration written before an upgrade, is now a configuration error that says the guardrail is off, where it used to be a notice calling it harmless.

- **`sqlens:security` ends on a misconfiguration (exit code 2) where `sqlens:audit` and `sqlens:lint` do.** `--connection` naming a connection `config/database.php` does not define reached no instance, each half reported that it examined nothing, and the run ended with 0, also under the `predeploy` profile; the same name in `sqlens.security.audit_connection` was already refused. A baseline that could not be read was treated as a crash of one half, so the run ended with 0, or 3 under `ci`, over a file the project believes is in force. The flag is now checked before anything connects, and a half that could not read the baseline, could not find its connection or refused before reading anything ends the run on 2. A connection that exists but uses a driver this package does not support still ends as before: that half reports that it examined nothing, and `strict_undetermined` decides what that costs.

- **`DROP TABLE a, b` and `TRUNCATE a, b` are read as statements about every table they name.** The classifier read the first name and stopped, so `DROP TABLE orders, users` was a drop of `orders`, and `users` existed for no rule. A `down()` running that line in a migration whose `up()` created only `orders` was reported as symmetric by `GEN.L1.DOWN_MORE_DESTRUCTIVE`, although the rollback takes `users` and its rows with it. Every name in the list is now a target, on PostgreSQL for `DROP TABLE` and `TRUNCATE` (with `ONLY` and `*` on a member) and on MySQL for `DROP TABLE`, and the rule judges each table on its own. A list member that cannot be read as a name makes the statement unclassified rather than one table shorter. The canonical fingerprint of such a statement includes its targets, so a baseline entry for a finding on a multi-table `DROP` or `TRUNCATE` no longer matches and has to be taken again.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` spends its time budget on the whole run and reports what the run really cost.** The clock started after the pending migrations were linted, so opening the session and the whole lint half ran outside `budget_ms`, although the configuration promises it bounds the whole run. And `run.time_budget_ms_consumed` was the budget minus a remainder floored at zero, so a run that took eight times its budget reported exactly its budget, the one value a deploy script reads as on time. The clock now starts before anything else, the lint half's time comes out of what the catalog checks get, and the consumed time is measured from the start, the way `sqlens:postdeploy` measures it, so an overrun shows as one. A project whose lint half is slow can now see catalog checks reported `undetermined` for the time budget where they used to run; `--budget` or `sqlens.preflight.budget_ms` raises it.

- **A catalog finding keeps its identity when the migration that introduced its object is renamed, moved or squashed.** The migration an audit finding points to is there to show where the object came from, and 0.15.0 promised it never enters a fingerprint. It did: after `schema:dump --prune`, a migration moved into a folder or a renamed one, the same finding on the same object had a new fingerprint, so its baseline entry stopped matching and went stale, and GitHub code scanning closed its alert and opened a new one. The path of that migration no longer enters the fingerprint of a catalog finding; for a migration finding the file remains its identity. A catalog finding that carried such a path gets its identity without it once, with this release: a baseline entry taken for it has to be taken again, and its code scanning alert is reopened once. A catalog finding without one keeps its fingerprint.

- **A baseline entry is judged only by the suite whose findings it is about.** `sqlens:baseline` writes the lint suite's findings, and the audit reads the same file, so every audit counted each of those entries as stale, telling a reader to prune lines the next `sqlens:lint` needed. Under `sqlens.baseline.stale = error` every audit of a project with a lint baseline ended on a misconfiguration (exit code 2), and a lint run treated an entry for a catalog finding the same way. Each suite now judges only the entries of the kinds it reports: the audit the entries about catalog objects, the lint suite those about migrations and call sites. An entry whose kind no suite of its rule reports, which no run could ever judge, is refused by name like an unknown rule id. A plain `sqlens:baseline` run keeps the entries about catalog objects, which it cannot write, instead of deleting them with the rest of the file. An entry added by hand for an audit finding carries `"kind": "catalog"`.

- **`DEPLOY.PREFLIGHT.DISK_HEADROOM` no longer passes over an object whose size it could not read.** A target the statistics reading returned no size for was skipped, so once one target was measured the others counted as zero bytes: a batch rewriting a 1 GiB table and a table nobody could measure passed against 3 GiB free. The check now ends `undetermined` (`object_statistics_unread`) while any target is unmeasured, names each one and says what the measured ones come to. A table the same batch creates with a column list holds no rows yet and needs no room, so a migration that creates a table and indexes it passes instead of ending undetermined; a table created by `CREATE TABLE … AS SELECT` holds rows and is measured like any other. The sentence about unreadable free space now counts every object the batch touches.

- **The ids `DEPLOY.CONTEXT.SETTING` reports each setting under are known wherever a rule id is read, and link a page that exists.** Each setting finding carries its own id, such as `DEPLOY.CONTEXT.SETTING.LOCK_TIMEOUT_UNBOUNDED`, four on PostgreSQL and five on MySQL, and none of them reached the rule registry: a baseline entry or an ignore naming one was refused as an unknown rule id, `explain_rule` did not know it, and the link in the finding led to a page that does not exist, four of them on findings that stop the deploy. `DEPLOY.CONTEXT.SETTING` is now registered as a family, so every member is accepted where a rule id is expected, each finding links the family's page, which describes every setting, and `explain_rule` answers a member of any family with the family's entry.

- **`sqlens:format` reports a file it could not write back as undetermined instead of as rewritten.** The write run listed every file whose formatting differed as reformatted and never looked at whether the write succeeded, so a file in a directory it could not write into was reported as formatted, `outcome: formatted` in the JSON report, and the run ended with exit code 0 over an unchanged file. Such a file is now `undetermined` with the reason `format_file_unwritable`, and the run ends with exit code 3, as it does for any file it could not format.

- **Declaring a server `disposable` no longer sends anyone to `sqlens:predeploy` for the server checks it withholds.** The finding's own sentence was corrected in 0.13.0, and three shipped texts kept the old pointer: the comment above `security.server.lifetime` in the published config, the message the config validator prints for a mistyped value, and the description of the `server_is_disposable` reason. `sqlens:predeploy` judges no server fact, so a project that followed them had its server checked nowhere. All three now name `sqlens:security` and `sqlens:audit`, run against the host that is operated, and the config comment no longer says a deploy command keeps the verdict out of an environment file's reach.

- **The catalog settings `table_prefix`, `prefix_scope`, `report_partitions_individually`, `include_extension_objects` and `extensions.allow` take effect.** `sqlens:audit`, `sqlens:drift` and `sqlens:postdeploy` built their catalog request from `sqlens.catalog.schemas` alone, so each of these settings stayed at its default while the config validator accepted it, and a connection's own Laravel `prefix` was ignored as well. A project with a prefix got findings about `acme_orders` rather than `orders`, a `strict` scope still judged the tables of the other tenants, an allowed extension stayed excluded, and a prefix that matched nothing was never reported. All three commands now build the request from the configuration, on the connection they read. A project that set one of these keys and saw no effect sees it now.

- **`DEPLOY.PREFLIGHT.DISK_HEADROOM` reads object sizes under the shipped `use_statistics = false`.** The preflight withheld its statistics reader whenever the switch was off, so the headroom check ended `undetermined` (`statistics_unavailable`) on every pending migration that adds a column or an index, and the free space an operator declared in `deploy.predeploy.available_disk_bytes` was never compared. The check now always gets the reader: with a declared free space it compares, and without one it reports the estimate as `filesystem_headroom_unreadable`, as its page describes. `use_statistics` still decides whether the deploy gate raises a finding's severity by table size.

- **The audit applies the project-wide `sqlens.ignore` and `sqlens.suppression.allow_undetermined`.** `sqlens:audit` and the audit half of `sqlens:security` handed their suppression layer no project list, so a rule a project had switched off there with a written reason stayed visible, counted against the gate and went into SARIF, while `sqlens:agent-rules` told agents the rule was off everywhere. An allowed undetermined reason was honored by the lint suite alone. Both now reach the audit through the same reader the lint suite uses, an entry's `suites` still limits it, and a rule id in `sqlens.ignore` that no rule answers to stops the audit as it stops the lint suite.

- **A narrowed run leaves the baseline entries it could not have matched to a run that can.** `sqlens:security`, a run under `--level` or `--category`, and `sqlens:lint --file` each apply part of what a full run applies, and each counted every entry of a rule it left out, or about a migration it did not read, as stale. Under `sqlens.baseline.stale = error` such a run ended on a misconfiguration: `sqlens:security` for every project whose baseline held an entry outside security and privacy, and a pre-commit `sqlens:lint --file` for every baseline with an entry about another migration. A run now judges only the entries of the rules it applied, and over `--file` it leaves the entries about the other migrations alone. A squawk rule counts as applied when the run's level admits it and squawk runs, which over `--file` takes `sqlens.tools.squawk.fast_path`. The other entries are neither stale nor unchecked in that run.

- **`sqlens:lint` leaves the baseline entries of a migration that already ran alone.** It reads the pending migrations, so on a database where a baselined migration had run, every entry about it matched nothing and was reported as stale: after `migrate` on a developer machine, and on every server that lints after its migration step. A reader who pruned them removed lines the next pipeline needs, on a fresh database where the migration is pending again, and under `sqlens.baseline.stale = error` the lint ended on a misconfiguration. An entry about a migration the project still has and the run did not read is now left alone, and one about a migration that is gone is still stale. The migrations a project has are read from the run's paths and the ones the application registers, so a run over `--path` also leaves the entries about the other paths alone.

- **`sqlens:security` counts and names the stale baseline entries of its halves.** The run dropped them, so `summary.stale` was 0 in every report and a baseline entry that had stopped matching went unmentioned. Under `sqlens.baseline.stale = error` such an entry ended the run on `2` as it should, but the half it belonged to was reported as having examined nothing and stopped before reading anything, with the ids of its own findings given as the reason. The report now lists the stale entries of both halves, and a half that read everything and ended on a misconfiguration only afterwards counts as run; the exit code is unchanged.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` and the MCP lint tools no longer take a stale baseline for a run that never happened.** Under `sqlens.baseline.stale = error` a stale entry ends a lint that judged every pending migration on the misconfiguration exit, and both read that exit as a refusal. `sqlens:predeploy` printed that the pending migrations could not be linted and that the preflight checked nothing, wrote no report and named the entry nowhere. `lint_pending` and `lint_shadow` answered that nothing about the migrations was determined, beside the findings the run had determined. The preflight now runs its checks and holds the deploy back with exit `2`, ahead of any finding, the `predeploy` tool's gate says the baseline did it, and the report lists the lint half's stale entries under either policy. The lint tools answer with the run, and their gate says the baseline ended it. A lint run that stopped before reading anything is still refused.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` counts the findings its lint half suppressed.** The report of a preflight was built from findings alone, so `summary.suppressed` was 0 and `suppressed_by_source` empty beside a lint half that had hidden every finding the baseline accepts, in the command's JSON and in the `predeploy` tool alike. It now carries the lint half's suppressions and counts them as `sqlens:lint` does. The verdict is unchanged: a suppressed finding never blocked.

- **`sqlens:agent-rules` accepts `security.min_severity = none`.** The configuration documents `none` as reporting security findings without blocking on them, and the config validator and `--min-severity` read it that way. The rule catalog behind `sqlens:agent-rules` took it for an unknown severity, so the command ended on a misconfiguration, `--check` failed in CI for every project that had set it, and the message listed the valid values without `none`. It is now read as the severity axis switched off, and the message for a value that really is unknown names `none` among the accepted ones.

- **A forgotten `dump` and a log channel on `php://stdout` no longer break an MCP session.** `sqlens:mcp` keeps standard output for the protocol with an output buffer, and both write past it with `fwrite`: Laravel's console dumper writes to `STDOUT`, and Monolog opens `php://stdout` itself. A dump in a service provider or a query listener of the host application, or a log line on such a channel, therefore landed between the frames, and the client's parser failed on it with nothing on standard error to say why. While the server runs, dumps go to standard error with the marker every captured line carries, and a log channel whose `path` or stream is `php://stdout` writes to `php://stderr`; standard error names each channel that was re-pointed.

- **The MCP tool `get_findings` reports how many findings the run suppressed.** It read the report's top-level `suppressed`, which holds the list of suppressed findings, as their count, so every report written with `--format=json` came back with `suppressed: 0`, however many findings a baseline or an ignore had hidden. It now reads `summary.suppressed`, and answers `null` for a report that says neither, rather than a zero nobody measured.

- **The MCP tool `get_debt_ledger` counts from the newest date in the ledger, as it says.** Without `as_of` it took the largest `first_seen` as a string, not as a date. A hand-written `yesterday` sorted after every date and became the reference, which PHP then read against the clock, so an unchanged ledger gave different ages on different days; `unknown` ended the call with an exception; and `2026-1-5` beat `2026-03-01`, leaving every later debt without an age. The reference is now the newest date the ledger's own date reading accepts, compared as a date, and a debt whose date it cannot read stays in the answer with `age_days: null` and an `age_unknown` sentence saying why.

- **`sqlens:drift` sees a changed PostgreSQL function whose body is written in the SQL standard's form.** A `BEGIN ATOMIC … END` or `RETURN …` body is parsed when the function is created and kept apart from `prosrc`, which stays empty, and the catalog reader took every routine body from `prosrc`. Two different bodies of that form read as the same empty text, so a function changed straight in production was reported as unchanged. Such a body is now read through `pg_get_function_sqlbody()`, the server's own rendering of the parsed body, which is the same text for the same body on both sides of the comparison.

- **`sqlens:baseline` keeps the entries of migrations that already ran when it rewrites the file.** It lints the pending migrations, and a write without `--update` replaced the file with what that run found. On a database where some migrations had already run, the accepted entries about them fell out of the file with exit 0, and the next `sqlens:lint` on a fresh database, where they are pending again, reported them all as new. A write now replaces only what the run judged: entries about migrations the project still has and the run did not read stay, and the command says how many. An entry about a migration that no longer exists is still dropped.

- **A pretend lint no longer judges a migration that declares its own connection against the one it captures on.** Laravel's migrator runs a migration with `protected $connection = '…'` on the connection it names, and a pretend run captured its statements on the run's own connection and judged them with that connection's driver, catalog and version, which reads as a pass for a database the migration never touches. Such a migration is now left unrun and reported as `CAP.L0.UNDETERMINED_CAPTURE` with the new reason `migration_on_another_connection`. A migration that names the connection being linted, or none, is captured as before.

- **`PG.L2.CONSTRAINT_NOT_VALIDATED` reports a `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` that runs in the same migration as its `NOT VALID` add.** Laravel runs a PostgreSQL migration in one transaction, and a lock is held until its transaction ends, so the validation scanned the whole table under the lock the add took: `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` for a check or a not-null constraint, and `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` on both tables for a foreign key. No rule reported that arrangement, and the shipped good examples of `PG.L2.CONSTRAINT_NOT_VALIDATED`, `PG.L2.SET_NOT_NULL_SCAN`, `PG.L4.CHECK_ENUM_CHANGE`, `PG.L4.CONSTRAINT_VALIDATION_PENDING` and `DEPLOY.LEGACY.CONSTRAINT_NOT_VALIDATED` showed it. The rule now flags the `VALIDATE` and hands over the second migration, and the examples keep the validation in a migration of its own. A migration with `$withinTransaction = false`, a table the migration created and left empty, and an add whose tables were all created in the migration stay silent. When the transaction context cannot be resolved, the finding is undetermined.

- **The pre-scan sees a surface a migration resolves out of the container.** The side-effect catalog named the facades and a few helpers, so `Cache::forget(…)` was reported while `app('cache')->forget(…)` ran for real under pretend. Spatie's published `create_permission_tables` migration ends that way, and a lint run cleared the real permission cache key and exited with 0. A literal key handed to `app()`, `resolve()`, `app()->make()`, `->makeWith()`, `->get()`, `Container::getInstance()->make()` or `App::make()` now meets the same catalog as the facade it stands for, and so do the helpers `cache()`, `session()` and `broadcast()`. Where the call made on that surface also matches an entry for a method on any receiver, as the `dispatch` in `app('events')->dispatch(…)` does, the finding names the surface the key resolves, the event dispatcher, and not a job. `sqlens.capture.prescan.side_effects.additional` accepts the same form, `app('key')`, for a service of your own.

- **`sqlens:predeploy` no longer stops a deploy over a healthy replica because its reading role may not see the replica's state.** PostgreSQL lists every replica to a role without `pg_read_all_stats` and leaves `state` and the lag empty. `DEPLOY.PREFLIGHT.REPLICATION_LAG` read that as a replica not streaming and reported it at high, measured on PostgreSQL 18.4 with a login-only preflight role, which is the account the deploy readers page recommends apart from its grant. The reading now reports such a replica as withheld, with `insufficient_privilege`, and the check answers `missing_privilege` and names `GRANT pg_monitor`.

## [0.25.0] - 2026-09-25

### Added

- **An audit states what its read-only guarantee rests on, and which bounds held.** Every catalog reading opens with a write that must be refused, and the refusal names its cause: the session's own read-only flag, or the account's missing write privilege. The audit knew which one it got and said neither. The JSON header now carries `run.sealed_by` (`session` or `privilege`) and fills `run.session_timeouts` with the bounds the session read back from the server, and the console header prints both as `sealed-by` and `session-timeouts`. PostgreSQL refuses DDL inside a read-only transaction before it looks at a grant, so an audit there says `session` under any account. **The envelope's `schema_version` is 8**: a consumer that pins the version reads the new field as the change it is. The predeploy console header prints its session timeouts now as well.

### Fixed

- **Every report states the day it was judged on, not only the audit's.** `run.run_day` came back `null` from lint, predeploy, drift and postdeploy runs: those commands finish their header by deriving it from a first one, and the derivation lost the day, while the audit and the security run build their header in one step and kept it. A consumer comparing lint reports across midnight read that `null` as a producer that handed no clock down. A derived header now keeps the day of the run it describes.

## [0.24.0] - 2026-09-23

### Added

- **The false-positive rate is published, together with the corpus it was measured over.** [False positives, measured](https://docs.pushery.com/sqlens-for-laravel/false-positive-rate/) carries the rate, the collection it was taken over — 14 migrations an upstream project really shipped, annotated case by case with the verdict each rule should reach — the five ways a case is classified, and the command that reproduces the report. The number stands beside what it does not cover: 228 of the 241 rules this build ships have no case in the corpus yet, and PostgreSQL has no collection at all, so its rate is unknown rather than good. The README says the same thing in one sentence and links to the page.

### Changed

- **The measurement behind that rate now runs the whole rule pack.** It took the strictness level from the host application, which is 0 unless a project raised it, so every rule above the capture layer stayed silent — and the classifier booked that silence as a missed finding where the corpus expects one, and as a correct silence where it expects none. Both were verdicts about rules that never ran. A case is also filed under the level its rule declares rather than the level a finding happened to carry, so a rule that correctly says nothing is no longer counted at level 0, and the report records the server version each collection was measured against: the same migrations classify differently on MySQL 8.0 and 8.4.

- **Messages, notes and comments that opened with a warning sign now read as plain sentences.** Three texts a report shows are worded differently: the session time zone sentence of `PG.L2.TYPE_CHANGE_REWRITE`, the offline remediation for a server setting, and the note on `data_checksums` in the server settings register. The published `config/sqlens.php`, the Boost skill and the documentation portal explain the same things without the emphasis. Nothing about what a rule reports or when it fires has changed.

### Fixed

- **An audit behind PgBouncer in transaction mode reaches its summary.** SQLens reads inside a transaction, and with `max_prepared_statements` set the pooler renames every prepared statement on its way to the server. PDO can remove one again with a plain `DEALLOCATE pdo_stmt_…`, which is not renamed, so the server refused it and the rest of the reading failed with `SQLSTATE[25P02]` on SQLens' own `SET LOCAL statement_timeout`. The connection SQLens builds for itself is now opened with `Pdo\Pgsql::ATTR_DISABLE_PREPARES`, so it holds no named statement to remove. Your application's connection keeps its own settings; the transaction-pooler section of [Read/write splits](https://docs.pushery.com/sqlens-for-laravel/read-write-split/) explains when it needs the same attribute.

## [0.23.0] - 2026-09-21

### Added

- **Five undetermined answers named a reason that describes a different state — four new reasons, and `unknown_server_version` now says what it covers.** A consumer branches on the reason, so a reason whose own description sends the reader somewhere else is a wrong answer, even beside a correct sentence. `SEC.CFG.TLS_MIN_VERSION` reported a TLS version name this build has never measured as `unsupported_engine`, about a fully supported PostgreSQL. It now reports `setting_value_unrecognized`, and so does `SEC.CFG.PATCH_EOL` for a version string it cannot read as a release series, which used to say `unknown_server_version`. **The difference is the remedy, not the wording**: `unknown_server_version` is a run with no version to reason from, and `assume_server_version` answers it. These two checks judge the live value, so a pin changes nothing, and the server, which accepted the value, is not what needs fixing. `SEC.PII.UNENCRYPTED_COLUMN` in a run assembled without the model reading reported `model_not_found`, which sends a reader to the application's models when no model was looked for; it now reports `model_reading_not_wired`. `SEC.PRIV.CONNECTIONS_UNSEPARATED` with both connections named and their credentials unreadable in the run reported `not_configured`, whose own definition is a setting the project has not made; it now reports `connection_identity_unresolved`. `SEC.AUTH.HBA_PARSE_ERROR` over an authentication-rule reading that came back empty without refusing reported `catalog_read_failed`, the catch-all for an unanticipated error that carries an error code; it now reports `catalog_reading_implausible`. `unknown_server_version` itself stays one reason for an absent and an unreadable version banner, because a pin answers both, and its description now says so. **A consumer branching on the old value for any of these five will stop matching** and should read the new one. As with the last reason added, the reason set is not part of the versioned run-field register, so the report schema does not move.

### Fixed

- **A column whose name is a reserved word was reported as not snake_case, on both engines.** `MY.L8.NAMING_SNAKE_CASE` and its PostgreSQL twin judged the canonical SPELLING of an identifier rather than the identifier, and a canonical spelling keeps its quoting whenever the bare name would not survive unquoted — which is exactly what a keyword does. So a column named `action` on MySQL or `text` on PostgreSQL was flagged with the reason *“it contains a character outside a-z, 0-9 and underscore”*, and both halves of that sentence were about the backticks. **Nothing about the name was wrong**, so the only two answers available to a reader were to rename a perfectly good column or to stop reading level 8. The rule now reads the bare name: the qualifying dot is found outside the quoting, so a quoted name containing one is no longer cut in half, and a doubled quote collapses back to the single character it stands for. **What is inside the quoting is still judged** — `` `Action` `` and `` `my column` `` are reported exactly as before, which matters because a renderer quotes precisely the names most likely to be wrong. Found by running the full rule pack over 14 migrations an upstream project really shipped, where it was 2 findings of 65 and the only wrong ones.

- **A published config that still carries `mode` stopped every command — and the 0.20.0 notes said it would not.** 0.20.0 removed the key with the sentence *„a published config that still carries it gets a notice rather than a refusal“* and shipped the refusal: nothing told a key this package retired from a key it never had, so `mode` fell into the unknown-key path and every `sqlens:*` command exited on misconfiguration. **It stopped green, which is the part that matters:** a package that runs nothing raises no red line, so a project that upgraded lost its checks without a signal. A retired key is now named, ignored, and the run continues — under a header of its own, because the key is SET, while the notice for a defaulted key tells a reader to RE-ADD what is missing and would have told this one to restore the line it should delete. `sqlens:doctor` reports it as `retired`, in the console and under a new `config.retired` list in `--format=json`, where every notice used to read `absent`. **An unknown key is still fatal, deliberately.** The retired keys are a list, not a pattern: a key one letter away from `mode` is refused exactly as before, because the typo protection is the half of this validator that was always doing the work. **Delete `mode` from your published config** — nothing has read it since 0.20.0.

- **On a managed MySQL, a grant on a database or table whose name contains a space was dropped from the security reading, and a grant to a user whose name contains one was filed under an account that does not exist.** When the grant tables are refused — the ordinary case on a managed server — the reading falls back to `SHOW GRANTS` for the connecting account, and it parsed each line with a pattern whose identifiers were `\S+`. MySQL writes identifiers in backticks and allows a space inside them. Measured on 8.4.10: a grant on a database named `my app` matched nothing and was taken for a role membership, so the grant vanished and the rules reading it reported clean; a grant to a user named `app user` matched with the grantee cut at the space. **Widening `\S+` would not have been enough:** a backticked name may also carry a comma, a parenthesis, a doubled backtick, or the words `ON` and `TO`, and a pattern anchored on those words reads the wrong one. Each line is now split by its structure, with backticked names opaque and the keywords recognized only outside them. A GRANT that carries an ON and still does not parse is named as a skip rather than dropped.

- **The cross-source dedupe layer's header now says what it can and cannot pair, and why a migration's grant and the live grant stay two findings.** It said the rule id was *„the only thing two such findings are guaranteed to share“*. It is not a guarantee — it is the first of **three** mismatches, measured over the whole `SEC.PRIV.*` family, 7 migration-side rules against 15 catalog-side ones. The ids differ by construction (the migration side carries an `_IN_MIGRATION` suffix). The object type is the harder half: not one of the 7 declares `judgedObjectTypes()`, so each takes the fallback and names the statement's subject **table**, while its catalog counterpart judges a `Grant` or a `Role`, and the object name follows from that. The 0.22.0 notes said such a pair "still cannot meet". It will not be made to: the migration half of `sqlens:security` judges pending migrations, so a pending `GRANT … TO PUBLIC` is the next deploy granting it again rather than the live grant reported twice. The two findings carry two remedies, revoking the grant and changing the migration, and suppressing one as the other would hide that the revoke does not survive the next deploy.

- **Shipped prose named rule ids this package has never had, and a guard now reads them.** The published `config/sqlens.php` and a shipped comment said `SEC.PRIV.GRANT_TO_PUBLIC`; the rule is `SEC.PRIV.GRANT_PUBLIC`. The config comment is the one a project copies when it publishes the file, so a copy made earlier still carries the wrong name and is worth correcting by hand. Three more comments said `PG.L7.REDUNDANT_INDEX` for `PG.L7.INDEX_REDUNDANT`, `PG.L6.PK.UUID_V4` for `PG.L6.PK_UUID_V4`, and `MY.L1.SQL_MODE` for a family that sits at levels 5 and 6. No check read rule ids out of prose before — the ones that existed cover the ignore list, the MCP answer and the documentation pages, all of which take an id as *input*. A guard now reads every rule-id-shaped token in `src/` and `config/` against the shipped registry, whole rather than up to its third segment, so the next one fails the build instead of reaching a reader.

- **The MySQL DDL-privilege artifact now says why `CREATE ROLE` and `DROP ROLE` are not structural, and a guard reconciles it against the server in the direction that was missing.** Both were always handled correctly — they are administration of the server and are reported by `SEC.PRIV.GRANT_SERVER_ADMIN` — but `deliberately_absent` named their neighbor `CREATE USER` and not them, so re-checking the list against a new server meant opening another rule's vocabulary to learn why two names were missing. The existing check proved every name in the artifact is one the server grants; the new one proves the reverse against `SHOW PRIVILEGES`: every privilege the server names after the DDL it permits is listed, explained, or mapped to a canonical case — without a fourth file.

- **A regular-expression result is never read as a bare condition any more, and the one place that folded the engine giving up into a count of zero now reads it as unknown.** `preg_match()` answers `1`, `0` — or `false` when the engine gives up on a subject — and under `!`, `if (…)` or a cast the last two become one branch, so a subject nobody judged is handled like one judged and rejected. A guard now reads every call in the shipped tree and refuses that form. It found a second site, which a search for bare conditions walks past because it is not one: the placeholder count behind the binding check added `(int) preg_match_all(…)`, so an engine that gave up counted as zero named placeholders. That is an under-count, the one direction the count must not err in, since zero can agree with an empty binding list and pass. It now answers a count no binding list can match, which the comparison reads as the mismatch it is.

- **On PostgreSQL, one table had as many canonical forms as it had unquoted spellings — `CREATE TABLE Orders` and `CREATE TABLE "orders"` now canonicalize alike.** PostgreSQL folds every unquoted name to lower case before anything reads it, so both statements create the same table, and the canonical form kept `Orders` as written: the stage that knows the keywords left every other word alone, and the stage before it leaves a standalone bare word to that one. The fold now happens where the keywords are known, per driver. PostgreSQL lower-cases a bare word that is not a keyword, and a QUOTED `"Orders"` stays distinct, because on PostgreSQL it is a different table. MySQL is deliberately not folded: whether `Orders` and `orders` are one table there depends on `lower_case_table_names`, which is case-sensitive on Linux. The fold is ASCII only, in a qualified name as much as in a bare one, because that is what PostgreSQL does in a UTF-8 database: `create table Élan` creates `Élan`. A qualified name used to fold the `É` as well, so `Élan` and `"élan"`, two tables, shared a canonical form. **The canonical form version moves to 5**, and with it every statement fingerprint, because the version is part of the hash. **A committed baseline is not affected, and it never was.** Its entries are keyed by rule and location; the statement fingerprint every producer hands `FindingFingerprint` has been empty since the baseline command existed. The 0.21.0 notes said the opposite for form 4 — that every baseline entry gets a new identity and a project regenerates its baseline once — and so did the canonical-form page. Both were wrong, and following them does harm: a regenerated baseline re-accepts whatever is reported at that moment, new findings included. The page now says what the code does, and there is nothing to regenerate for this bump either.

## [0.22.0] - 2026-09-21

### Added

- ⚠️ **A known server version with no matrix row no longer reports `unknown_server_version` — new reason `setting_expectation_missing`.** Every setting rule answered `undetermined` with the same reason for two different situations, because `ServerSettingMatrix::for()` returns the same `null` for both: the version could not be resolved, and the version IS known while this package ships no expectation for that variable at it. ⚠️ **The two send an operator to different places**, which is why this is not a cosmetic rename — *„I do not know which server this is“* points at the version pin, *„I know the server and have nothing on file“* points at the matrix, or at us. Somebody with a correctly pinned server was told their version was the problem, and the sentence beside it said so too. Both sentences now name what is actually missing, and the version-known one prints the version, because which version had nothing on file is the fact a reader would otherwise have to guess. ⚠️ **A consumer branching on `unknown_server_version` for a settings finding will stop matching** and should read the new value instead; the reason set is not part of the versioned run-field register, so the report schema does not move for it. Both directions are pinned by arms that differ in one thing — whether the subject carries a version at all — because the pair is the claim and either half alone is satisfied by a rule that simply renamed one reason to the other.

- ⚠️ **SCHEMA VERSION 7: the run header names the day it judged on, as `run_day`.** Rules that judge on a date — a support window closing, a debt acknowledgment expiring — were reproducible only by luck at a boundary: the report named no day, so re-examining a verdict across midnight meant guessing which side the run had been on. The clock is read once per run and handed to the rules **and** to this header, so the day in the report is the day that was judged on rather than a second reading that usually agrees. ⚠️ **It is a new schema version and not a field on 6, and that is the whole cost:** a field added to a version consumers already hold would tell them they had it all along, and schema 6 shipped with 0.12.0. The version rise is classified `allowed_in_minor` by the package's own breaking-change detector, which says why in its code: every version names the fields it added in a register the envelope's tests hold against the number, so a rise is an addition by construction. ⚠️ **`run_day` is the LAST key of the header, and the position is contract rather than tidiness** — the register guard compares the post-v3 keys in header order, so a v7 field standing in front of a v6 one would announce itself to a consumer on schema 6. An arm asserts it is last and that the value traveled rather than being re-derived. ⚠️ **And the clock turned out to have SIX producers, not the four the design described:** three of them reach the header through the run-context collector, which reads like a configuration reader. A named arm now pins that exact set — a seventh entry is a seventh clock, and the collector itself must create none, because five producers call it once per run and a reading there would compete with the one the rules got. The announced-and-shipped check, which covered only version 4, now covers 5, 6 and 7: a register naming a field the header never carries reads like a promise, and only the union caught the other direction.

### Changed
