- ⚠️ **BREAKING for a caller that constructs a driver or the driver registry with a date string: `$today` is now a `Today` value object.** Nothing in this package passed one — the service provider deliberately passes `null` — so the change is invisible to an ordinary install; a third-party caller that handed in a `Y-m-d` string updates one argument. The optional `AcceptsRunClock` interface is **additive**: a driver that does not implement it keeps the documented fallback of one clock reading per `rules()` call, which is what every driver did before, so no implementer breaks. ⚠️ **A required parameter on `Driver::rules()` was the obvious design and was measured and rejected:** two of that method's five callers only ENUMERATE rules — the shipped rule-registry artifact and the rule-id-to-operation-class map — and neither judges a database or has a „today“. A required parameter would force both to invent a date inside an artifact that is compared against a golden file, which is a non-deterministic artifact.

- ⚠️ **One clock per run, and there were FIVE readings.** `gmdate('Y-m-d')` was called wherever a day was needed: twice in the lint runner, twice in the audit runner, once in the postdeploy command. Each call is its own reading, so a run crossing midnight registered debt candidates against one calendar day and collected standings against another — inside one report, with nothing saying which side either was on. The two-clock defect the previous cycle fixed between two rule families existed **within a single debt pass** the whole time. Each run now reads the clock once, at its entry, and hands the value down; the shipped tree contains `gmdate('Y-m-d')` in exactly two places, and an arm names both rather than counting them. ⚠️ **The value is an OBJECT rather than the string it wraps**, because the property that has to be provable is identity and not equality: two readings agree on almost every run and differ exactly when it matters, so a string could only ever establish that two readers saw the same DAY. ⚠️ **And the anti-vacuity control of the existing guard had to be rewritten, because it was calibrated to the old COUNT** — `toBeGreaterThan(2)` over the files that read the clock, which turned red the moment those readings were consolidated. A control pinned to today's quantity fails on progress and passes on regression in the same breath; it now names the two files and says why each is allowed. ⚠️ **Both shipped drivers lose their class-level `readonly` and carry it per property instead**, which keeps every guarantee except on the one field the run clock sets: a readonly CLASS leaves a `with…()` method no field to write on PHP 8.4, and the 8.5 clone-with form that does is not available on the version this package promises. Caught by the integration gate rather than locally, because the development machine runs 8.5 — a local syntax check measures the machine, not the declared floor.

- ⚠️ **The `sqlens:agent-rules` block now says that it records no package version, and why — a consumer asked for one, and writing it would have cost every consumer a red integration branch per release.** `--check` compares the WHOLE generated content (`$desired !== $existing`), not the `catalog=` hash in the marker — so the suggested middle, *a version in the block but out of the hash*, does not exist: anything in the generated text arms the check. With in-house packages merging on an automatic update without a waiting period, a version line would turn `check-quality` red in every consuming project on every release of this package, until somebody re-ran the command. ⚠️ **A version-blind comparison is the other way out and this command refuses it in its own words**: check and write are one path, because two would agree until they did not. ⚠️ **And nothing is lost, which is why the line is a sentence rather than a field:** a green `--check` already establishes that the list matches the rule set of the version installed, and which version that is takes one `composer show`. What the sentence prevents is the failure actually measured in a consuming project — a hand-written version beside generated content, **12 of 14** markers reading `never` against an installed version three minors ahead, because prose outside the block is unguarded and exits 0 however wrong it is. An arm now pins the content-not-marker distinction by editing inside the block while leaving the marker line byte-identical; the three neighboring arms replace the file or the whole section, so none of them could tell the two apart. **Consumers regenerate once**, as with any change to the generated text.

### Fixed

- ⚠️ **The cross-source dedupe layer can finally fire, and `sqlens:security` can finally say what it hid.** The layer collapses "a migration grants to PUBLIC" and "the catalog holds that grant" into one report entry, and it had never once fired: `sqlens:security` resolves its two halves in separate passes with a resolver each, so a lint pass held no catalog candidate to build an entitlement set from and an audit pass returned early on every candidate. The audit half runs first anyway, so its **visible** findings now ride into the lint half. ⚠️ **Visible, not every audit finding, and that is the safety of the whole thing** — feeding the hidden ones too would let one baseline entry on the catalog side take the migration side down with it, and the fact would leave the report entirely: accepted in one place, erased in another. ⚠️ **And a second defect went with it, which consumers see directly:** `SecurityOutcome` carried finding lists only, so `suppressed_by_source` in the `sqlens:security` report was a **permanent zero** — which reads as "nothing was hidden" rather than as "this report cannot tell you". Both halves hand their book up now and the aggregate concatenates it; each entry was made exactly once by exactly one half, over disjoint candidate sets, so `sqlens:audit` on its own keeps the count it always had. ⚠️ **This is necessary and not sufficient, and that is measured rather than suspected:** `identity()` includes the rule id, and the two sides of one risk are named differently (`SEC.PRIV.GRANT_PUBLIC_IN_MIGRATION` against `SEC.PRIV.GRANT_PUBLIC`). Across all 21 rules, zero run in both suites and exactly one of the eight `_IN_MIGRATION` ids has a twin — so a real pair still cannot meet, and the arms covering this say so in their own header rather than reading as proof of a working collapse.

- ⚠️ **`PG.L4.ENUM_ADD_VALUE` told a reader to add an enum value the migration never names.** The capture read the added value with `'([^']*)'`, which ends at the FIRST quote — and a quote inside a PostgreSQL literal is written by doubling it, which is the grammar rather than an edge case. Measured: `ALTER TYPE mood ADD VALUE 'it''s fine'` came back as `it`, and `'a''b''c'` as `a`. ⚠️ **The damage is that it was a WRONG answer and not a missing one.** The class docblock promises that a shape the pattern does not recognize leaves the placeholder standing *„rather than guessing at a literal“* — but the pattern MATCHED, so the truncated text counted as established and the remediation printed it as the value to add. The form now used is the one `IndexPredicate::LITERAL` already carries and documents with the same example, and the value is un-doubled on the way out. ⚠️ **And the promise itself is now asserted**, which it never was: an unquoted value returns `null` while the addition is still recognized, so a reader who writes something this class cannot parse gets a placeholder instead of an invention. That missing arm is how the truncation survived — a promise no arm checks is a comment. Nine arms, including both edge doublings, both `IF NOT EXISTS` forms, and an anti-vacuity control, because every value arm reaches through `?->` and an `of()` that returned null for everything would leave all of them green.

- ⚠️ **`DEPLOY.PREFLIGHT.FREEZE_HORIZON` told a deploy no anti-wraparound vacuum was running, from a session that could not have seen one.** PostgreSQL does not REFUSE `pg_stat_activity` to a role without `pg_read_all_stats` — it **masks** the foreign rows. So `query` reads `<insufficient privilege>`, the filter that recognizes a worker by its query text matches nothing, the read succeeds, and nothing throws: the check's `catch` never fired and the result was a clean pass. Measured on 18.4 with a role holding only CONNECT: two foreign sessions, both masked, zero rows through the vacuum filter, no exception. ⚠️ **The condition is not exotic — a least-privilege migration role is exactly what this package's own `SEC.PRIV.*` rules recommend**, so following that advice cost this check silently. The visibility is now asked before the emptiness is interpreted, with `pg_has_role` on `pg_read_all_stats` rather than `has_table_privilege` on the view: the question is not whether the view may be read, which it may, but whether foreign sessions appear in it. ⚠️ **It is an `undetermined` on ONE axis, not a shutdown.** The age clocks come from `pg_class` and are readable by anybody, so a table near its horizon is still reported as a finding; only the case that used to be a *pass* — nothing near, nothing visibly running — becomes undetermined, and the sentence says which half was answered. The probe is asked only in that case, because a query bought for an answer nobody reads is a query on somebody's production instance at the moment before a deploy. Proven against a real role, created and dropped by the test, one `grant pg_read_all_stats` apart from its own positive control. ⚠️ **And a neighboring suspicion was measured and REFUTED rather than built:** `backend_type` for a masked session is **NULL**, not an empty string, so `PgsqlActivityReader`'s existing `backend_type is null` filter is correct and passes 2 of 2 masked rows — changing it to compare against `''` would have dropped the filter to zero rows and defeated the masking detector with the masking it counts. The earlier probe had *rendered* the value, and a NULL prints as an empty string.

- ⚠️ **An ordinary index takes four column operations off their pinned fast path, and the online-DDL matrix named only the FUNCTIONAL one.** Measured on 8.4.10 with the algorithm pinned, each operation run twice differing in nothing but a plain `KEY`: dropping a plain column, a stored generated column and a virtual generated column are all accepted `ALGORITHM=INSTANT` unindexed and **refused with error 1845** once indexed; a `utf8mb3 → utf8mb4` column change is accepted `ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE` unindexed and **refused with 1846** once indexed. The statement does not fall back and run slowly — it fails, so a pinned migration breaks rather than drags. ⚠️ **The control is what makes the charset row mean anything:** that entry already refuses `latin1 → utf8mb4` with the same 1846, because the stored bytes change — so the arm fires that condition on an *unindexed* column, and the pair chosen for the index rows changes no stored byte at all. ⚠️ **The scope came from the measurement rather than from the report:** two operations were reported, the probe was pointed at all three entries carrying the functional-index condition, and two more refuse identically. Nothing beyond what was measured carries the new condition, and an arm pins that list in both directions. ⚠️ **And the entry's own `rebuilds_table: false` is conditional on the pin**, which the same run showed: without `ALGORITHM=INSTANT` the drop rebuilds the table. That is compatible with the package's model — the remediation tells you to pin — but it is now said in the notes, because a migration that does not pin gets exactly the cost the entry rules out. ⚠️ **Decided toward a condition rather than a pessimistic reclassification**, against the precedent set for `convert_character_set`: measured, both entries already resolve to `undetermined` on the shipped path, because `MatrixContext::blind()` decides nothing and 13 of the 50 entries carry conditions. The feared cost of a condition is therefore already paid, while a pessimistic axis would replace an honest "I do not know" with a definite claim that is wrong whenever the column is unindexed.

- ⚠️ **`PG.L2.TYPE_CHANGE_REWRITE` warned about an outage that a project following this package's own advice does not get.** Since PostgreSQL 12 an `ALTER COLUMN … TYPE timestamptz` is metadata-only when the session's `TimeZone` is UTC, and a rewrite otherwise — and `PG.L6.TIMEZONE_NOT_UTC` is the rule that tells a project to run on UTC. The type-change matrix classifies by the target's **dominant real case**, which it says of itself; for this one target the dominant case is the free one, and we are the reason. Measured against 18.4 with `relfilenode` as the signal: UTC no rewrite at 0.7 ms, `Europe/Berlin` a rewrite at 25.9 ms, the two runs differing in nothing but the session setting — with controls in both directions (`int → bigint` rewrote, `varchar(10) → varchar(20)` did not), because a reading of "no rewrite" is exactly what a broken detector produces. **Both directions carry it**: `timestamptz → timestamp` under UTC is free as well, which the ticket had not named. ⚠️ **The wording is the whole risk and is deliberate.** A heuristic that names its exception is easier to wave away — someone not on UTC reads "this may not apply" and stops. So the finding does not offer an excuse, it hands over the decision procedure: `SHOW TimeZone;` in the session that will run the migration. A caveat you can answer in a second is not an invitation to skip the check; an unanswerable one is. The finding still prints the ACCESS EXCLUSIVE warning, and an arm holds that an unconditional target such as `bigint` gets no such caveat — otherwise the fix would teach a reader to skim every rewrite finding. ⚠️ **The definitive answer belongs to the audit suite**, which has a connection and could simply read `TimeZone`; it is not built here because the rule receives no server state at all, and handing a rule runtime state would be the first time any rule receives it — that seam does not exist yet.

## [0.21.0] - 2026-09-20

### Added

- ⚠️ **CANONICAL FORM VERSION 4, and every fingerprint changes with it — read this before upgrading.** `ALWAYS`, `GENERATED`, `STORED` and `VIRTUAL` were in neither driver's keyword list, so `generated always as identity` folded only its `AS`: the same DDL in two casings produced two canonical strings, which is **two fingerprints for one statement** — the exact defect this package's canonicalization exists to prevent, in its own words. ⚠️ **The consequence is wider than the four words, and it is not a side effect but the design:** the form version is part of the canonical fingerprint, and `FindingFingerprint` folds that fingerprint in — so **every** baseline entry gets a new identity on this upgrade, not only entries about generated columns. A project with a baseline regenerates it once; accepted findings come back until it does. That is what a form bump has always meant here (forms 2 and 3 did the same), and the version exists so the change is announced rather than discovered. ⚠️ **Why these four slipped through is the same reason the query vocabulary did, recorded in that file twice already:** the keyword guard's corpus is generated from the SCHEMA builder, and `GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY` is written by hand or through `->change()`, never by the builder's ordinary path — so no corpus statement ever carried them. **Both engines**, because the MySQL list was measured rather than assumed and carried the identical gap; `VIRTUAL` and `STORED` are the commoner spellings there. The four words appear in no shipped rule pattern (measured, with a control), so nothing was ever mis-reported — what moved was the identity.

- ⚠️ **BREAKING, and small: `sqlens:doctor --strict` is now `--strict-tools`.** One flag name meant two things. `sqlens:audit --strict` treats an undetermined result as a failure — `strict_undetermined` — while doctor's asked whether a strict-**tools** run would fail: two config keys behind one name, on two commands a reader meets in the same session. `sqlens:lint`, `sqlens:security` and `sqlens:format` all spell the tools question `--strict-tools`, so doctor was the outlier and doctor is what moved. **No alias, deliberately** — an alias would keep exactly the ambiguity this removes, and `sqlens:doctor --strict` would still mean something different from `sqlens:audit --strict`. ⚠️ **The shipped agent skill already asserted the new state:** *"`--strict` is an audit option only"* was false while it read as true, and the rename is what makes it a fact. ⚠️ **A guard now holds the whole option surface to one meaning per name** — every option of every registered command must appear in a declared table exactly once, in both directions, so a name reused for a second meaning has nowhere to go and a new option cannot ship without somebody writing down what it is. Meaning is not in a signature, so the table is declared rather than derived, and the arm says so instead of implying otherwise.

- ⚠️ **`SEC.PRIV.PROXY_TO_ADMIN_IN_MIGRATION` — a migration that grants `PROXY` on the server's own account, which hands over every privilege of that account while naming none of them.** `GRANT PROXY` is not a privilege over data or over the server: it is permission to **connect as** another account, so the grantee authenticates with its own credential and then holds the proxied account's privileges instead of its own. `GRANT PROXY ON 'root'@'localhost' TO 'app'@'%'` is a root session for the application, and a review that reads privilege lists sees nothing in it. **The shipped registry holds 241 entries**, one more than 0.20.0. By category: 145 safety, 59 security, 17 idiom, 10 performance, 7 convention and 3 privacy. By maturity: 236 stable, 3 preview, 2 experimental. **160** answer to the level gate; the other **81** carry a severity — 7 critical, 33 high, 29 medium, 8 low and 4 info. It carries no `downtime_class` — a grant is not an operation on a schema — so that count stays at 58 entries. ⚠️ **It needed its own rule because of GRAMMAR, not because of area.** `PROXY` reads as administrative — 8.4 reports it under `Context = 'Server Admin'` — and the obvious move was a row in the administrative-privilege artifact. That artifact describes names appearing in a `GRANT … ON *.*` shape, and `PROXY` takes an **account** as its object: `GRANT PROXY ON *.*` is rejected with `ERROR 1064`, measured. An entry there could never have matched, and the arm that offers every listed name to a real server would have stayed red forever — which is how this was found. It sits in that artifact's excluded set now, pointing at the rule. ⚠️ **It judges the TARGET, and a rule reporting the shape would have been switched off in a week.** Proxying is a legitimate authentication arrangement, and measured on 8.4.10 a stock server ships one of its own accord: `root@localhost -> ''@''` sits in `mysql.proxies_priv` on a fresh install. So the rule fires on an administrative **proxied** account — the `mysql.` prefix the server uses for its own, and `root` — and the `good` fixture keeps the mechanism rather than removing it. ⚠️ **What it cannot see is declared rather than left to be discovered:** an arbitrary privileged account such as `admin@%` is invisible, because a statement does not say what an account holds; that question needs the catalog. `root` is a convention rather than a reserved name, so a renamed one is not reported. And the severity does not vary with the target — `mysql.infoschema` carries no `SUPER` where `mysql.session` does, and both report at High, because a project account should be impersonating neither. Silent on PostgreSQL by grammar: it has no `GRANT PROXY`, and that exemption is declared in the fixture allowlist rather than left as a missing file.

### Fixed

- ⚠️ **An empty baseline let the breaking-change gate pass, and it did not look like silence — it printed "3 change(s), none of them breaking".** The missing file was named and the empty one was not, and the asymmetry is what made it dangerous: an empty *candidate* surface reports every released rule as removed and goes loudly red, while an empty *released* surface reports every candidate rule as **new** — all of them `allowed_in_minor` — so nothing blocks and the classification passes. A release removing every rule in the package would have gone through on that output. Measured with controls: `{}`, `{"rules": []}` and `{"rules": "broken"}` each gave zero blocking changes, while a genuine removal and an emptied candidate each gave the red they should. **Both ends are fixed, because one alone does not hold.** The comparison refuses an empty baseline with the same sentence as a missing one, and the write path now reads the rule registry through `ShippedJson` instead of a hand-rolled decode — so a registry of `{}` or `{"entries": []}` can no longer produce an empty surface and write it without a word, and that file now sits inside the bundled-artifact policy rather than beside it. ⚠️ **`extension_contracts` carries the same fallback expression and is NOT affected**, measured rather than assumed: it blocks in both directions, because that loop iterates the union of both key sets while a missing rule is classified as an addition. The same expression has opposite consequences depending on what the loop enumerates.

- ⚠️ **`ShippedJson` refused a missing `entries` list and accepted an empty one — with a message that argued for refusing both.** The refusal reads *"an empty register would make the rules that read it silently correct about every server, which is a pass and not an undetermined"*, and `{"entries": []}` produces exactly that register. Two callers pass a required key and both go quiet on an empty list: the server-setting matrix judges no setting, and the MySQL administrative-privilege register holds no name as administrative — a security rule that reports nothing while running cleanly. The check is tied to `$requiredKey` rather than applied to every read, and that is load-bearing: the three callers that name no key — the baseline's emittable ids and the two suppression sources — are **correct** to accept an empty file, because nothing accepted and nothing suppressed both mean *everything* is reported, which is the loud direction. ⚠️ **Two other loaders needed the same guarantee and each built it themselves**, locally: the privacy and money dictionaries both throw on "contains no usable terms at all". That is the drift this class was created to end, one level up — *a decision made ten times is a decision made differently*. Also removes an import of a class that exists nowhere in the repository: the policy is real and is called `BundledArtifactPolicyTest`, and the `{@see}` pointed a reader into nothing.

- ⚠️ **`tls_version` was `changeable: restart` and is a reload — the field words the remediation, so a wrong value is wrong ADVICE rather than untidy data.** The same field once carried `initdb` for `data_checksums`, and that finding told a reader to migrate a cluster where `pg_checksums --enable` does it in place. Every `changeable: restart` entry was swept against a real 8.4.10 with controls in both directions: `secure_file_priv` is READ ONLY in both scopes and correctly stays `restart`; `tls_version` refuses `SET SESSION` as a GLOBAL-only variable, accepts `SET GLOBAL`, and `ALTER INSTANCE RELOAD TLS` is what carries a new value to new connections — a reload, with no downtime to plan. The entry is deliberately still not judged, because the honest check is a set comparison rather than a value comparison, but it is reportable and the field will be read by whatever rule judges it later: a wrong value waiting for a rule is a trap with a timer. ⚠️ **The first sweep was discarded because its controls went red** — it quoted every value, and MySQL answers a quoted argument to a numeric variable with error 1232. Both `restart` entries take strings so the result would have stood, but a sweep whose control fails is measuring its own quoting rather than the server.

- ⚠️ **Two shipped docblocks stated something the server contradicts, and each carried a justification a reader is right to lean on.** `MetadataLockCheck` earned its silence with *"the `metadata_locks` instrument is disabled by default in several distributions"* — that default flipped in 8.0, and on 8.4.10 it is `ENABLED=YES` out of the box, with 480 of 1259 instruments off so the query demonstrably sees disabled ones. The load-bearing reason is that both `performance_schema` and the instrument can be **turned** off, not that either ships that way, and the old sentence invites the one change that breaks the check: a reader who measures the default, finds YES, and concludes the caution is obsolete. `SqlModeFlags` treated a combination word arriving in the value as a remote case, *"somehow still carried"* — measured over all three read paths, `TRADITIONAL` comes back **alongside** its full expansion, which is the normal state of any server set that way. It costs nothing, because `has()` finds the expansion, but the old text drew the opposite conclusion and a guard built on it would pin an untruth. Neither changed a result; both are traps for the next edit. ⚠️ **Also switches the preflight's `sql_mode` reading to `SqlModeFlags`.** The audit suspected a defect; measured, the two are equivalent on 8.4.10 — no MySQL flag contains either needle as a substring, and the server folds the value to upper case whatever casing it was set with. This is consistency rather than a fix, and the new arm pins the equivalence so the next reader does not measure it again.

- ⚠️ **`RuntimeDdlGuard` knew two of MySQL's three comment forms, and the missing one did not make it lenient — it switched the guardrail off.** `opensWithComment()` tested for `--` and `/*`. MySQL has a third, `#`, and a form this method does not know does not fall to `undetermined`: it falls through to the keyword reading, which sees `#` as the leading word, does not find it among the DDL keywords, and returns in silence. Measured against 8.4.10 with a control: `# app=web route=checkout` followed by a `CREATE TABLE` created the table and drew no violation at all, while the same statement behind a block comment drew the `undetermined` it should. So an application whose query-tagging middleware writes the hash style had this guardrail **disabled rather than enabled**, with nothing on the surface to say so — and the docblock names query-tagging middleware as the very reason the check exists. ⚠️ **The direction is what makes it worth a fix rather than a note:** too much `undetermined` is noise somebody sees, and a silent pass reads exactly like a clean run. Two arms, because widening what counts as unreadable is one edit away from a guardrail that never accuses anybody: naked DDL still reports, and a statement that merely contains a hash inside a string literal still does not. ⚠️ **MySQL's double-dash needs a following space — `--x` is minus-minus there, and this check calls it a comment anyway.** That is the cautious direction and it stays; it is now written down, because the asymmetry otherwise looks like a bug worth "fixing" in the direction that loses findings.

- ⚠️ **A column encrypted through a custom cast was reported as "stored in the clear" the moment that cast carried an ARGUMENT.** `EncryptedCasts::isCustomCastable()` returned false on sight of a colon, reasoning that *"a parameterized built-in carries a colon; a class does not"* — and the second half of that is untrue: Laravel documents a custom cast with arguments in exactly that shape, `Hash::class.':sha256'`. So `OpaqueCast::class` answered `undetermined (CustomCastOpaque)`, which is right, and `OpaqueCast::class.':aes-256-gcm'` — **the same cast class** — fell through to unprotected and drew a finding telling the reader the column is *"readable to anyone with SELECT on the table, and present in every dump, replica and backup of it"*. On a privacy rule that is not a missed finding but a false statement about a column's protection, and it contradicts what the class says about itself: a custom castable may encrypt or may not, so the answer is `undetermined`. The arguments now come off first, which is what the framework does in `parseCasterClass()` before it decides anything. ⚠️ **The arm that should have caught this was green and its own message argued for the bug:** *"a colon means a built-in"*, over four cases that were a built-in with an argument, a built-in with an argument, a bare built-in and a bare class — the one shape that breaks the rule, a class WITH an argument, was the only one never asked about. ⚠️ **And the obvious cure is wrong, which is why a second guard ships with the fix:** stripping the argument and asking `class_exists` alone answers TRUE for `datetime`, because PHP resolves class names without regard to case and `DateTime` exists — every `datetime:Y-m-d` column would become an opaque custom cast and the rule would fall silent over a column genuinely in the clear, trading a false positive for a false negative nobody notices. Measured across the framework's 27 built-in cast names, that collision is the only one, and it is the built-in most likely to carry an argument in real code. So the check mirrors Laravel's order — strip, built-in list, then `class_exists` — and `EncryptedCasts::PRIMITIVES` is held against `Model::$primitiveCastTypes` by a drift test, the same wall and the same answer as the encrypted family: the property is `protected static`, so a package cannot ask at runtime, and a hand-typed copy that drifts silently is what the test exists to prevent. A further arm pins the `datetime` collision itself, so the day PHP or Laravel changes it, the comment explaining the check cannot quietly become stale.

- ⚠️ **`MY.L2.NO_PRIMARY_KEY` reported `DROP PRIMARY KEY, ADD PRIMARY KEY` in one statement — which is the SAFER of the two shapes.** The all-clear scanned for a **later** statement that adds the key back, and a multi-action `ALTER TABLE` has none: measured against 8.4.10 through a real capture, the canonical model classifies the whole thing as **one** statement of kind `drop_constraint`, and the add is not reflected in the kind at all. So the rule flagged the migration that rebuilds the table once and never leaves it without a key, while the two-statement form — which really does open that window — passed cleanly. **A rule that pushes a reader toward the worse of two shapes is the kind that gets switched off.** The all-clear now reads the reported statement's own canonical text first, which is the move `dropsThePrimaryKey()` already makes one method up and for the same reason: the kind is too coarse to tell these apart, and the text is where the information is. ⚠️ **Two arms, because one would have been a switch-off:** the one-statement replacement draws no finding, and a lone `DROP PRIMARY KEY` still draws one — a check that simply returned true would pass the first and fail the second. Red proof over both. ⚠️ **Found as a SUSPICION rather than a measurement**, carried by a deep-audit run that had neither a server nor `vendor/` and could only read source; confirming it took one capture against a real MySQL.

- ⚠️ **Three values the CLI accepted and then silently ignored — in a package whose own rule is that an unknown value is a NAMED misconfiguration.** `--level=12` and `--debt=recrod` were both refused by name; these three were not. **`--budget` on both deploy gates:** read as `ctype_digit($b) ? (int) $b : null`, and `null` is the signal for *"not given, take the configured value"* — so `--budget=abc` ran with the default and said nothing, leaving an operator believing they had bounded the run they were about to gate a deploy on. The two gates also disagreed about `0`: `sqlens:postdeploy` required a positive number and `sqlens:predeploy` accepted zero, so one flag took two different value sets depending on which gate you typed it at. Both now read one implementation, and zero is refused with the rest — a budget of zero milliseconds is not a fast run, it is one whose deadline passed before the first statement. **`--probe=<typo>` on `sqlens:doctor`:** the name was taken unchecked and the report loop walks only configured names, so `--probe=prodd` matched nothing and every connection printed `undetermined (no connection is open — pass --probe to open one)`. **The diagnostic told the reader to pass the flag they had just passed**, and *that name does not exist* was nowhere in the output; it now names the value and lists the configured connections. ⚠️ **And the deploy gates resolved `--format` AFTER the run.** Both refused an unknown format rather than falling back — that part was already right — but the refusal arrived after a full catalog reading against the production instance, at the moment before a deploy. `sqlens:drift` already did it in the other order and said why in its own comment; both gates now match it. ⚠️ **One red proof found a weakness in its own arm, and it is recorded:** reverting the budget cure left `sqlens:predeploy` still answering `Misconfiguration` — for an unreachable database, not for the budget. An arm that stopped at the exit code would have been green over the defect, so it asserts the message instead.

- ⚠️ **`sqlens:drift` had TWO verdict paths, and under the shipped defaults the report mode exited 3 in CI on a command documented to exit clean.** `DriftExitPolicy`'s docblock says it holds *"the whole verdict, in one place"*; the command answered one case before ever calling it — an unbuildable reference — and answered it the other way, returning `UndeterminedInStrictMode` in **report** mode where the policy says report mode never blocks. **That was not an edge case but the ordinary CI outcome:** the reference is a shadow replay, `deploy.shadow.allowed_environments` ships as `['local', 'testing']`, so in a `ci` environment the production guard refuses, no reference is built, and the run ends 3. ⚠️ **Four sources described that case and three agreed with each other** — the command's class docblock (*"the default prints everything and exits clean"*), the policy's report arm, and the `--allow-undetermined` help text. So the behavior was the outlier and the behavior moved: the case is now a fifth input to the policy, the command derives no exit code of its own, and an arm asserts over its source that it names neither drift verdict any more — with a red proof that restoring the old branch fails it. ⚠️ **Report mode exiting clean there is not a silent green, on the condition the command's own docblock names:** the mode and the reason are printed. A report run makes no claim about the schema — that is what gate mode is for — and a mode that turns red on its first run against a real database is switched off rather than adopted. In gate mode an unbuildable reference still blocks, and `--allow-undetermined` is the one way through, the same one a partial blind spot gets, because it is the same statement about reach with a wider subject. ⚠️ **A second defect fell out of the merge: the early return bypassed `waived()`**, so a gate run let through by the hatch over a missing reference was recorded as an earned green rather than a waived one — the exact distinction that method exists to keep. The flag help now says it applies in gate mode, since in report mode it changes nothing. `DriftExitPolicyTest` could not have caught any of this: the path sat before the policy it tests.

- ⚠️ **The shipped `good` example for the SET-NOT-NULL rule did not reach NOT NULL, and its own note claimed it reached "the same guarantee".** It ended after validating a `CHECK (col IS NOT NULL)`: measured on 18.0, the column is then still `is_nullable = YES`. The template had the third statement; the **published example** did not, and the example is the thing that gets copied. It is not unsafe — the check does reject a new null, with `23514` — it is **incomplete**, which is the harder defect to notice: the migration works and the next catalog read disagrees with what the reader was sent out to do. ⚠️ **The arm for it executes the shipped string and reads the catalog back**, because asserting on the text would pin today's wording while an example is a promise about a resulting schema. Red proof: putting the old pair back fails it with that sentence. ⚠️ **And the recommendation itself moved from the PostgreSQL 12 detour to the two-statement 18 form** — on 18 a not-null constraint is a `pg_constraint` row of its own with `contype = 'n'` and accepts `NOT VALID`, so `ADD CONSTRAINT … NOT NULL col NOT VALID` plus `VALIDATE` replaces four statements with two, and the package's floor is 18. **The detector already treated that form as correct and drew no finding on it**, so the package recognized the answer while never handing it over — two halves that each read correctly alone. ⚠️ **What the safe sequence removes is the LOCK CLASS, not the reading, and the prose now says so:** measured over two million rows, the bare `SET NOT NULL` and the `VALIDATE` that replaces it both take about 46 ms; one holds ACCESS EXCLUSIVE and the other SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE. A page promising a faster statement would be wrong. ⚠️ **The new sequence's half-state is the OPPOSITE of the detour's, so it has its own debt note rather than the shared one.** Measured with a null row already present: the first statement is accepted and the catalog then reports the column as NOT NULL while the null row is still there — the `VALIDATE` is what finds it, with `23502`. The old half-state was honest by comparison, the column stayed nullable. So this gap cannot be found by looking at the column, and what finds it is `convalidated = false`, which `sqlens:predeploy` reads through a query deliberately not filtered by `contype`. The four-statement detour is documented as the route below 18, and the rule stays silent on it.

- ⚠️ **The deploy grant check refused a legitimate setup: it required TABLE ownership for a `DROP TABLE`, and PostgreSQL accepts the SCHEMA owner too.** Its own documentation says *"only the table owner, the schema owner, and superuser can drop a table"*. So a migration role that owns the schema while another role created the tables inside it was reported `OWNERSHIP_MISSING` at **Critical** with a **blocking** downtime class, and sent to `ALTER TABLE … OWNER TO` — an ownership transfer it does not need and may not want, because transferring ownership changes who can drop the table later. The question the check asks is now the one the server asks: does this role own the relation, **or** the namespace it lives in. ⚠️ **Measured on 18.0 rather than read off the manual, and the same measurement settles the other direction:** with the table owned by one role and the schema by another, the old question answers false, the new one answers true, and the server accepts the `DROP` — while `ALTER TABLE … ADD COLUMN` by that same schema owner is **refused**, with *must be owner of table*. So the widening stops at `DROP`, and an arm holds it there: without one, a later edit could fold the two arms back together and nothing would notice. ⚠️ **One sentence for both privileges was the other half of the defect.** The two classes exist so a project is told which of them it is short of, and both were told the same thing; the drop text now says either owner will do, and the alter text says owning the schema is not enough because `ALTER TABLE` requires the table itself. The arms assert on that difference rather than only on the outcome, since an outcome-only check passes on one text used twice.

- ⚠️ **Seven shipped comments described a state the package left behind, one of them in the published config.** The `suites` placeholder promised semantics that *"arrive with the lint core"* — the lint core shipped long ago and the key still does not exist, measured against the schema with a control (`suites` absent, `level` present). So its arrival condition had passed and it is removed rather than introduced. ⚠️ **The config's own opening paragraph already warned that uncommenting a placeholder fails loudly**, so the behavior was documented and deliberate; what was not defensible was shipping a promise whose deadline is gone. The other six: the capture collector said the driver rule packs *"land later"* while `LintRunner` hands it the run's active rules; the base run-context collector called itself *"trivial for now"* and said version collection *"lands with the capture and audit suites"* — both shipped, and the collection landed in the runners instead, which is the right place because only a runner knows which instance it addressed; the reporter manager named **four** built-in formats and there are **five**, omitting the `agent` one the README recommends to an agent; the exit-code resolver said *"no command wires this yet"* and three do; the captor factory said the server version *"arrives with the version-pin unit"* and `LintRunner` attaches it on the next line. ⚠️ **And one method argued against itself with a reason on each side:** the postdeploy budget's docblock said it shares `preflight.budget_ms` on purpose because *"a second key would be a second number to keep in step"*, three lines above a comment saying the command was moved off that key because *"two commands making two promises need two numbers"*. The code reads its own key; the docblock was the half nobody updated, and a reader who trusted it would have tuned the wrong one. ⚠️ **No guard, deliberately, and the reason is recorded:** a check forbidding an unknown commented key would contradict the config's documented allowance for loud placeholders, and *"this arrival condition has passed"* is not mechanically decidable. Two claims were checked and left alone because they are TRUE — the empty canonicalization stages, and PostgreSQL keeping WAL segments *"for now"* past a configured limit.

- ⚠️ **The online-DDL matrix published the BEST case for an axis that predicts an outage, and the rule's prose published the worst — each right about a different pair, neither saying which.** `mysql.table.convert_character_set` carried `algorithm: inplace`, `permits_concurrent_dml: true`, `lock: none`. Measured on 8.4.10 over four pairs, that is true for exactly one: `utf8mb3 → utf8mb4`. `latin1` and `ascii` to `utf8mb4` are refused in place with error 1846 and fall back to a copy that blocks writes for the whole run — and so is the narrowing back to `utf8mb3`, which the notes did not mention. The legacy `latin1` table is the one anyone actually runs this on. ⚠️ **And the verification arm was GREEN, because its recipe converted from `utf8mb3` — which the recipe said out loud in its own reason string.** Nobody was careless: the decision was written down, and it was the wrong one for these fields. `LockImplication` states what they are for — *"the distinction decides whether a rewrite is an outage or merely slow"* — so they are a prediction about a deploy, not an enumeration of what the server can do. **Being wrong pessimistically costs an unnecessary maintenance window; being wrong optimistically costs an outage.** The axes are now the pessimistic ones, the recipe converts from `latin1`, and with that change the verification arm *falsifies* the old axes where it used to confirm them. ⚠️ **A condition was deliberately NOT added, and the reason is a trap worth recording:** `MatrixResolver::applyConditions` turns a violated condition into an `undetermined` resolution, and `narrowest()` picks its entry *before* conditions run — so a two-entry scheme cannot fall through. Copying the sister entry's condition would therefore have made the commonest case *classless* rather than conservative, and the class `rewrite` is right for every pair because `rebuilds_table` is. An arm holds the class through the mapper, not merely the absence of the condition. ⚠️ **And the remediation note's advice became wrong by being made consistent:** it said *"pin ALGORITHM and LOCK with the values the online-DDL matrix records"*, which now means asking for the block explicitly. It points at the matrix for the two cases and instructs `LOCK=NONE` — the one move that works without knowing which case you are in, because the server then refuses instead of silently blocking. The rule's own docblock table gained the source charset it never named.

- ⚠️ **Sixteen shipped translation keys had no reader, and the guard that called them "LIVE" was checking something else.** The `shadow.*` family described refusals that are built from `UndeterminedReason` and `GuardBlockReason` descriptions, in English prose, never through the translator — nothing in the shipped tree asked for any of them but `shadow.confirm`. `ShadowMessagesTest` listed all seventeen and asserted each **resolves**, which is true of any key present in the language file and says nothing about whether anything reads it. `LocaleParityTest` is inverted (it asks whether translations are complete) and `ShippedLocaleTest` asks about resolution, so a dead key passed all three. The cost was never the space: whoever set out to change a shadow refusal found the keys first, edited one, and nothing happened — the sentence lives in the enum. ⚠️ **A new belt holds every leaf key of the shipped locale to a reader**, either a literal or a derived concatenation prefix, with an arm proving the exemption is doing work and another holding the `drivers.*` family to its enum one-for-one — the tighter form the other prefixes cannot have, and the file says so rather than leaving the limit to be discovered. ⚠️ **And the first draft of that belt deleted eleven keys that are ALIVE, which is recorded in it because the lesson is the important part.** It derived prefixes by looking for `messages.<prefix>.'` — and `SkipReason::translationKey()` returns `'catalog.skip.'.$this->value`, with the `messages.` part added by the caller. Eleven `catalog.skip.*` keys looked dead, were removed, and three test files went red. The pattern now matches any quote-terminated dotted literal: **a false orphan deletes working code, a missed orphan leaves a dead key**, so broad is the right side to err on. The finding named thirteen keys in one family; the corrected measurement is sixteen in that family and none in the other.

- ⚠️ **The README and two source comments named the suppression order backwards, and the guard that exists for exactly this could not see them.** The resolver applies `config → audit ignore → baseline → annotation → destructive opt-in`; the README said *"four sources, applied broadest first: a repository baseline, a config ignore, an annotation …"* — wrong about the count and wrong about the first. A reader who predicted which layer a report would blame got the wrong answer, and a baseline burn-down counted differently than they expected. ⚠️ **This was the SECOND time, and the reason is the shape of the guard rather than a lapse:** two portal pages had the same defect and were corrected, and the arm that pins them roots its path at the portal directory — so it can only ever be asked about a file inside it. The cure reached exactly the documents the guard knew. The extractor now takes a repo-relative path and the README joins the same arms, table and count sentence included. ⚠️ **The README carries the order as a TABLE rather than a sentence, on the guard's own reasoning** — *"prose cannot be kept in step by remembering to"* — so the same extractor reads it without a second pattern to maintain. And the portal-publication arm keeps its own list of two: the README is not a portal page, and asserting that the portal publishes it would be a false statement inside a guard about false statements. Red proof: scrambling one row of the README table fails the order arm, and `standing before temporary` is the mnemonic the comments now carry.

- ⚠️ **Two clocks answered "which day is today", and the second one was also FROZEN.** The debt ledger judges an expired acknowledgment on UTC; the security pack judged a support window on the **local** date. An application whose `app.timezone` sits east of UTC therefore saw two different calendar days around midnight — `PatchEolRule` flipping on one and `LINT.DEBT.ACKNOWLEDGMENT_EXPIRED` on the other, with nothing in the report to say which. And the local value was injected from a **container singleton**, so it was computed once per container: under Octane, in a queue worker or across a long test suite the container outlives the day, and the date was then not merely local but stale. ⚠️ **The fix is a removal rather than an addition, and that is the interesting part.** The old comment argued for one value per RUN — *"a support window closes on a date, and two rules reading the clock either side of midnight would give one database two answers"* — which is right, and injection from a singleton is not how you get it. `Driver::rules()` runs fresh on every run and the rule set resolves its default exactly once per call, handing the same value to every rule it builds. So nothing injects a date any more: the property the comment wanted holds, and the value cannot age. The default is UTC. ⚠️ **The prose explaining this deliberately does not spell out the local-time call**, because the acceptance for the change is a grep for it over the shipped tree — a comment naming a check answers the check, which is the failure mode this repository keeps rediscovering. A guard sweeps the tree comment-stripped for a second clock, with a control proving it still finds the UTC form; a third arm holds the registry singleton to carrying no date at all, because a test cannot make the day change and what it can prove is that nothing is left to freeze.

- ⚠️ **`RunMetadata` IS REMOVED: it was a second reproducibility header, nothing read it, and every value the package put in it was wrong.** Its docblock promised that *"every reporter shows them"*; its only projection was `Result::toArray()`, which the JSON envelope does not emit and which no shipped code called — two source comments in this package already said so. And the values: `AuditRunner` wrote `mode: pretend` for a run that captures no migration at all, the label the same file calls a lie three lines higher, alongside empty server-version and tool-version lists; `LintRunner` did the same. **A public property whose every value is wrong is worse than an absent one** — the first third-party reporter to read it, which its own docblock invited, would have got all of it. The real header is `RunContext`, which every reporter is handed beside the result and which already carries the same fields plus twenty more. ⚠️ **BREAKING for a direct consumer of the aggregate:** `Result::of()` no longer takes a metadata argument and `Result::$metadata` is gone, so the `metadata` block has left the `toArray()` projection. Read the run parameters from the run context. ⚠️ **The two same-named `CaptureMode` enums STAY, and the reason was already written down** — `Reporting\CaptureMode` is the header's vocabulary with three states including `static`, `Subjects\CaptureMode` is the mechanism with two. The defect was that the removed class typed its mode on the two-case mechanism enum, so a catalog run could not say what it did and said `pretend` instead. ⚠️ **The scripted part of this was cut with a paren matcher, not a regex, and the dry run caught three ways it would have corrupted files silently:** an apostrophe in an ordinary comment made the first draft read the rest of a file as one string literal; a test that asserts on source text contains `Result::of(` inside a string, which the matcher then tried to balance from inside a literal; and a call with too few arguments needed reporting rather than assuming. 104 call sites in 48 files, and the eight occurrences inside literals were correctly left alone. A guard now holds the absence: the run-parameter field set may be declared in exactly one shipped class, the aggregate may not carry it, and no shipped code may name the removed class — with a control proving the comment-stripped scan still sees the prose that explains the removal.

- ⚠️ **The agent skill enumerated three of five `--format` values, and one of the two missing is the mode built for its reader.** The option line in the lint section said `console|json|github`; the signature names `console, json, github, sarif, or agent`. ⚠️ **The skill is not wholly silent about the missing two** — `--format=sarif` and `--format=agent` each have their own section further down, and saying so matters: the defect is a summary line that contradicts them, not an absence. It now names all five, points at the agent section, and says `--show-remediation` applies to `console` only. Separately, the doctor: the skill promised *"the real server version"* per connection, and without `--probe` that field reads `undetermined (no connection is open — pass --probe to open one)`. The README had it right; the skill was the copy that drifted. ⚠️ **And the guard had to be per COMMAND, which is the whole difficulty.** The `sqlens:format` section says `--format=console|json|github` and `FormatCommand` really does take exactly those three — the same triple that is wrong in the lint section. Content alone cannot tell them apart, so the arm resolves each enumeration against the nearest `php artisan sqlens:<x>` above it, the way a reader would, and holds it to that command's signature. ⚠️ **The first draft of that guard reported six true statements as wrong, and both causes are recorded in it:** it treated a single mention like `--format=sarif` as an enumeration and demanded all five values of it, and it swallowed the prose one description carries after its list, turning eight ordinary English words into formats. A guard with false alarms gets switched off, and that one would have deserved it.

- ⚠️ **`MY.L6.PK_NOT_BIGINT` named the SIGNED ceiling for every `int` key, and `increments()` produces `INT UNSIGNED`.** So the commonest shape the rule ever meets was told its key runs out at 2,147,483,647 when it runs out at **4,294,967,295** — wrong by a factor of two, in the one sentence a reader checks before deciding whether to trust the rest of the finding. The recommendation never changed: `BIGINT` is still the answer. ⚠️ **The catalog had read the signedness all along and nothing carried it.** The column object held `unsigned`; no projection put it on the table, so the one rule that needed it could not ask. A new `unsigned_columns` projection carries it as a column set beside `nullable_columns` — not folded into `column_types`, because the canonical type vocabulary is shared between engines and `int unsigned` is not a name in it. It is empty on PostgreSQL, which has no unsigned integers, and that is the honest answer rather than an absent key. ⚠️ **The eight ceilings are MEASURED, not transcribed:** each was verified against 8.4.10 by inserting the value and then the value plus one — accepted, then refused under a strict mode. An unknown type answers with no limit at all rather than a default, because a ceiling invented for a type the table does not know is the same class of wrong number this entry is about. ⚠️ **The finding named two places and there were five.** Besides the message: the rule's own docblock, the shipped example note, and the **published** rule page — which contradicted itself, printing the signed number directly above an `increments()` example and then saying *"unsigned doubles each ceiling"* eighty lines below. The PostgreSQL side was checked and left alone: its integers are always signed, so 2,147,483,647 is correct there.

- ⚠️ **A Unix-domain socket connection was reported as `managed_database_restriction`, and that reason says the opposite of the comment choosing it.** `inet_server_addr()` returns NULL over a socket, so the PostgreSQL identity probe has no address to report — and the reason it named tells the reader *"a managed database blocks the catalog or setting the check reads"*. The comment two lines above knew it was a socket. ⚠️ **It is not a corner case: it is every developer machine connecting over a socket**, the most ordinary local setup there is, and each of them was told their provider was withholding something. The intent — *named rather than blank*, so a reader can tell "connected over a socket" from "the reading could not say" — was right; the name was not. A new `local_socket_connection` reason carries it, and its description says there is nothing to report rather than that something is blocked. ⚠️ **The arms are transport-independent on purpose, and the file says why:** the behavior-level pair lives in the real-engine suite, which reaches its server over TCP, so the socket branch is never entered there — a guard that can only fire on a machine nobody runs CI on is not a guard. What runs every time is that the two reasons cannot be confused (one must say *blocks*, the other must not, and the socket one must name the transport), that they serialize to different values because the enum travels in the JSON envelope, and a comment-stripped code pin that the reader does not name the wrong reason again. ⚠️ **The red proof found a defect in its own control:** with the control asserting the fix was present, reverting the call site made the CONTROL fail first and printed a bare `<?php…` dump with no diagnosis in it — red for the right reason, telling the reader nothing. The control now pins what a revert leaves true, and the failure a future reader sees is the pin's own sentence.

- ⚠️ **The `ERROR_FOR_DIVISION_BY_ZERO` finding recommended the flag by describing the one harm it does not prevent.** It said *"division by zero then yields NULL instead of raising"* and named a NULL flowing into an average, a ratio or a money column as the damage. **A `SELECT` keeps returning NULL with the flag on** — the manual is explicit, and it is measured here on 8.4.10. So a reader who set the flag and still found a NULL in a report had every reason to conclude the checker was wrong about something. The flag governs the **write** path, which is the more valuable half anyway: a stored NULL outlives the query that made it, and nothing downstream can tell it from a NULL somebody meant. The message now says that a division by zero in a writing statement is an error, that a `SELECT` stays NULL and gains a warning, and which of the two the reader is looking at. ⚠️ **One row of the measurement was not in the finding and is the answer to "why set it on a schema that forbids NULL anyway":** on a `NOT NULL` column the write fails either way, but without the flag it fails as `1048 column cannot be null` — a complaint about the column, which sends the reader to the schema instead of the arithmetic. With it, `22012`. Two unit arms pin both halves of the sentence, with a red proof over the text that actually shipped, and a real-engine suite asks the server rather than trusting the prose: a pinned wording is only as true as the engine it describes, and nothing held those two together. ⚠️ **And one claim is stated rather than asserted, because neither way of reading it through the framework's handle measures it.** `PDO::ATTR_EMULATE_PREPARES` is off, so `SHOW WARNINGS` answers **1295** — a warning about the query for warnings — and `SELECT @@warning_count` answers **1 in both modes**, counting something about prepare-and-execute rather than the division. An arm over either would have been green for the wrong reason. The warning is real, measured by hand where `SHOW WARNINGS` works, and recorded in the rule's docblock with the reason it is not an assertion.

## [0.20.0] - 2026-09-20

### Fixed

- ⚠️ **Nine places said a MySQL prefix-length key indexes the first N BYTES of a column, and MySQL counts CHARACTERS.** The manual's `INFORMATION_SCHEMA STATISTICS` page defines `SUB_PART` as *"the number of indexed characters if the column is only partly indexed"*. No behavior was affected — only a boolean is derived from the value — but the unit is the whole point of the sentence: under `utf8mb4` twenty characters are up to eighty bytes, and prefix length is exactly the decision somebody is making while reading this. Two of the nine are on the **published** rule pages and one is a user-visible skip reason. ⚠️ **The finding named two sites and the sweep found nine**, which is the shape this repository keeps meeting: a finding names the instances its author happened to read. The sweep that closed it looked for a byte count within four lines of any prefix or `SUB_PART` talk across `src`, `tests`, the docs portal and the README, and carries a positive control — a count of the corrected wording — because a sweep reporting zero and a sweep measuring nothing are indistinguishable. The binary carve-out is stated where it belongs rather than dropped: `BINARY` and `VARBINARY` really do count bytes.

- ⚠️ **A finding told the reader that a table without an explicit `CHARACTER SET` takes the SERVER default, and it takes its DATABASE default.** The manual's *Table Character Set and Collation* page gives a table the database's character set and collation; only a database created without one takes `character_set_server`, per *Database Character Set and Collation*. So on a `utf8mb4` database a `latin1` server default is harmless for new tables — the rule reported damage that did not exist there, and sent the reader to audit their tables instead of the one default that decides what the next database gets. ⚠️ **The package already documented the chain correctly** — column ← table ← database ← server, in the shared text-encoding reader — and this rule did not use it. The message now names the database as what takes the variable, says a table inherits it in turn, and tells the reader which level to check. An arm extracts the sentence that says what *takes* the value and requires it to name a database and **not** a table; a second arm holds the wording to the shared reader's chain so the two cannot drift apart again. ⚠️ **The naming half alone would have passed on the broken text**, because the old sentence did mention a database — in a list that also mentioned a table. That is why the red proof runs over the sentence that actually shipped rather than a fixture, and why the guard is a per-sentence check rather than a per-message one.

- ⚠️ **Shadow mode told operators that "every connection it opens is bounded before its first query", and the defense is applied at exactly ONE call site.** Three connections of the provisioning path run without it: the maintenance link, the template connection replaying a whole schema dump, and the MySQL replay doing the same. The docblock made the same claim in the same words. That sentence is what somebody relies on when deciding whether to point shadow mode at an instance they share, so an overstated assurance is the expensive kind of wrong even where nothing is currently harmed. Both texts now say which connection is bounded and **name each one that is not** — the maintenance link deliberately, because cloning a real schema outlasts a per-statement budget and the gateway already documented that; the other two as a known gap rather than a decision. Closing that gap would be a behavior change on the provisioning path, where a per-statement budget over a schema dump aborts a legitimately long replay, so it is recorded rather than done quietly. An arm now holds both texts to the code: it refuses the unqualified promise, requires each unbounded connection to be named where the promise is made, and asserts the defense is applied at the one site the prose claims — the opposite failure, which a narrowing correction like this usually introduces. ⚠️ **Two of the finding's four parts were already overtaken, and saying so is part of the work.** It asked for the MySQL pooler probe to be given the budget its PostgreSQL sibling has, and for a shadow probe constructed without one to be corrected. Neither reader takes a budget any more: that bound was removed on purpose because it *was* the leak the probe exists to detect — a session `SET` lands on one pooled backend and the restore looks for it on another. So constructing without one is now correct, and the remaining asymmetry was in the **reasoning**: PostgreSQL explains at length why there is no bound, MySQL said nothing. A reader of the silent one would have concluded the bound was forgotten and added it back, rebuilding the leak. That paragraph is now on both.

- ⚠️ **Eleven British spellings sat in the shipped tree because the language guard could not see them, and two were in text a user reads** — a config expectation describing which connections the security suite examines, and a finding text about the combined size of the objects a rewrite touches. A third form, the past tense of *dial*, appeared in four comments about the host a client connected to. ⚠️ **The guard's blindness to the third-person verb of *analyse* was a correct decision taken one step too far:** it deliberately exempts that word because the same letters spell the US **plural noun**, and its comment says so. Right about the noun, blind to the verb. The other two stems were simply absent from the list. ⚠️ **The new verb pattern excludes NOUN SIGNALS rather than listing subjects, and that is the whole design.** The obvious narrow form is a subject list, and subjects are an **open** class — such a list misses the next verb use with a new subject while looking complete. Determiners and quantifiers are **closed**, so excluding them catches the verb and never reports the noun. Its failure mode is deliberately a miss rather than a false alarm: a miss costs one occurrence, a false alarm costs the guard. ⚠️ **The finding said ten lines; the pattern found an eleventh, in the published documentation** — on a rule page, in a sentence about the files a run examines. My own sweep had read `src`, `config`, `lang` and `resources` and not the docs portal, which the guard does read. That eleventh also confirmed the design: the determiner there attaches to the subject rather than to the word in question, so the lookbehind correctly let it through to be caught. Each of the three patterns now carries a red proof over the shapes that actually shipped **and** a control asserting that the plural noun, the singular noun, PHPStan's own British-spelled namespace, and the corrected US forms all stay quiet. ⚠️ **And this entry is written without quoting the forms it is about** — the first draft spelled them out to explain them and was rejected by the very pattern it describes, which is the failure mode the repository already records for prose about a guard.

- ⚠️ **The pre-push hook's list of required CI steps called itself "the `just all` chain" and was missing two of that chain's steps — with nothing red.** `tracker-id` (the `check-leak` recipe) and `format-backends` were absent. Neither is a release-tier step — `tracker-id` carries no condition at all and `format-backends` carries the integration one — and both existing arms looked only at release-tier steps and at whether a named step exists in the lane. **The first half of the hook's own claim was never checked.** Harmless today, because a `success` run on the release line had necessarily passed both; a drift risk, and the drift arrives the moment a step gets a non-release condition. ⚠️ **The real defect was three copies of one answer.** The contract test held the recipe-to-step mapping as a local array, the hook held a flat list of step names, and the proof test held that list to the lane and to the release tier. The mapping now lives once, in `tests/Support/CiRecipeEnforcement.php`, with the reasons beside the data; both tests read it, and a new arm derives the expected list from the chain and holds the hook's copy to it. A second arm refuses a recipe the mapping has not classified — and its reverse, a classification for a recipe the chain no longer runs, because an exemption outliving its subject reads as coverage while covering nothing. ⚠️ **The hook still carries a literal, deliberately rather than unfinished:** a hook parsing the justfile and that mapping at push time would put more machinery in the one place where a failure blocks every push, on a path no test surrounds. What changed is that a drift can no longer be merged. ⚠️ **And two test fixtures were incomplete transcriptions of real pipelines, which is how the omission stayed invisible** — one of them said "both external-binary lanes" and listed two of three. Corrected against a live run rather than from the mapping: a current push to `develop` carries `tracker-id` and `format-backends` and carries neither `perf` nor `browser`, which is exactly the tier split those two fixtures model.

- ⚠️ **The arm asserting that CI runs every `just all` recipe was satisfied by the gate lane's own header comment — five of ten markers appear there as well as in the commands.** It checked with `str_contains` over the whole file, so **deleting the `coverage` step would have left it green**: line 10 still reads `#   coverage = composer test:coverage …`, and the guard could not tell a step from a sentence describing one. Measured on the current file: `composer format:test` at comment line 7 and code line 587, `composer licenses:check` at 8 and 666, `composer test:coverage` at 10 and 864, `composer test:performance` at 14 and 945, `composer test:browser` at 16 and 1142. ⚠️ **The neighboring `PublicReleaseGuardPresenceTest` documents this exact trap for the release lane** — the lesson had been drawn in the repository and not applied here. The mapping now names each recipe's **gate step**, and the marker is resolved through that step's `commands:`, which closes both halves the old form left open: it has to be a command rather than any line, and it has to be *that* step's rather than another step that happens to mention it. **And the arm now checks what its own sentence promised:** a second arm resolves each mapped step's `when:` tier and refuses one that fires on no event a merge passes through — a step excluded from both `push` and `pull_request` enforces nothing before a merge, which a marker check cannot see. The tier is printed in the failure text for the same reason. ⚠️ **The red proof is a mechanism check rather than a fixture, and says so:** the acceptance line asked for the `coverage` step to be removed, but the CI lane is fleet-owned surface and is not edited from here. Instead the arm demonstrates both readings side by side over the real file — the old `str_contains` passes, the header comment that satisfies it is asserted to exist, and a step name that does not exist resolves to an empty command list. That is stronger than a fixture, because it shows the old method passing on the tree as it stands today.

- **The `SafeFileWriter` hardening left a branch no run can enter, and the 100 % coverage floor named it at 99.9 %.** The short-write check — `if ($written !== strlen($contents))` — cannot be entered by a test: `fwrite` to a local file does not come up short unless the disk is full or the stream is broken. ⚠️ **Deleting the check was the wrong answer**, because renaming half a file over a migration is the first failure that class's header says it prevents. So the check became a **value** instead: the write moved into its own method whose last line is an expression that always runs, and a short write is now a `false` rather than a jump. The remaining `return false` is reachable, and by a deliberately **uid-independent** route — `x` is `O_CREAT|O_EXCL`, so the open fails when the directory does not exist, which holds for root as well. An arm built on an unwritable directory would have covered the line locally and not in the container where coverage is measured.

- ⚠️ **A session value was restored with `addslashes()`, which is the wrong escaper for PostgreSQL — and the call sat on the SHARED path, so "one of the two engines" meant every PostgreSQL run.** With `standard_conforming_strings` on, the default since 9.1, a backslash inside `'…'` is an ordinary character. **Measured on 18.0:** `SELECT 'a\''` answers `ERROR: unterminated quoted string`, because the `\'` ENDS the literal and the trailing quote opens a new one — so the escaper does not merely fail to protect, it turns a value carrying a quote into a syntax error. Doubling is correct on both engines: **measured on MySQL 8.4.10**, `SELECT 'a''b', 'a\'b'` returns `a'b` twice, so MySQL takes either form and PostgreSQL only this one. The package already used doubling in `BindingSubstitutor` and `PgsqlSessionDefense`; there is one idiom now rather than one per file. ⚠️ **Nothing was exploitable and the defect is still real:** the values are validated GUC readbacks with no quote in them, which is precisely why nobody noticed — two escaping forms for one job means the next reader copies whichever they meet first, and then the safety depends on where the value came from. A behavioral arm supplies a value with a quote rather than waiting for the day one turns up, and a guard in `OneOwnerPerConventionTest` — the file whose premise is one owner per convention — refuses a second escaper anywhere in the shipped tree. ⚠️ **That guard reads comment-stripped CODE, and the acceptance line had to be refined to say so:** it asked for `grep -rn addslashes src/` to come back empty, and the method that was fixed now EXPLAINS the wrong escaper at length in its docblock. Over code the grep is empty; over prose it never will be, and a guard accusing the file that documents the rule is the failure mode this package keeps finding.
- ⚠️ **`sqlens:doctor --format=json` and `sqlens:format --format=json` answered a blank line at exit 0 when any value carried an invalid UTF-8 byte.** `json_encode` returns `false` there, a `(string)` cast turns that into `''`, `line('')` prints an empty line — and the exit code still came from the verdict. So a pipeline consumer received an **empty document with a success status**, which is the one shape this package rules out everywhere else. The bytes have real sources: doctor's payload carries tool versions and resolutions read from **process output** and `$PATH`, and format's `files[].path` comes from the **filesystem**, where a non-UTF-8 name is legal on Linux. Both now use the flags four reporters already carried — `JSON_THROW_ON_ERROR | JSON_INVALID_UTF8_SUBSTITUTE`: substitute so a malformed version string cannot suppress a whole document, throw for the structural failures substitution cannot cause. ⚠️ **The sweep found a third site the finding did not name** — a value rendered into an error message *about* a malformed file — and it takes `JSON_INVALID_UTF8_SUBSTITUTE` **without** throwing, deliberately: throwing there would replace a useful complaint with an exception raised while explaining the first one. A new guard holds the policy over the whole shipped tree. ⚠️ **Its scan window had to be widened from 260 to 700 characters, and that is recorded because the failure mode is the dangerous one:** at 260 the scan reported `DriftExcludeFile` as unguarded — its call spans more, with the flags on the closing line — so a window that is too small does not under-report, it names the **wrong files** and sends somebody to fix code that is already correct. ⚠️ **And one acceptance line cannot be met as a test, which the guard says rather than omitting:** the finding asked for a format-path arm with a non-UTF-8 filename. Measured on this machine, creating one raises `OSError 92, Illegal byte sequence` — macOS enforces UTF-8 at the filesystem layer, so such an arm would pass in a Linux container and **error on every developer machine**. The doctor path carries the behavioral arm instead, its byte arriving through a value rather than a filename, and it is proven red against the reverted source: the output is `''`.

- ⚠️ **The process runner honored RELATIVE `$PATH` entries, and one adapter hands its binary the database password.** `pathDirectories()` filtered only *empty* segments, so `.`, `vendor/bin` and `node_modules/.bin` were joined like any other directory — CWE-427, an uncontrolled search path element: whatever the process happened to be sitting in decided which binary ran. The version fence closes nothing, because `ToolLocator` compares a `--version` string and authenticates nothing. And `pgls` makes it concrete: it is the only adapter handed the audited connection's password — out as `PGPASSWORD`, because postgrestools connects to the database itself — and it ships **enabled** with `path => null`, resolved through `$PATH`. Absoluteness is now tested the way `SingleFileResolver::absolutePath()` already tests it in this package rather than with a second spelling of the question. ⚠️ **The config prose was the more important half and said nothing about it:** it explains a pinned path as a *determinism* measure, which is true and incomplete — for this one tool it is a credential boundary, and a configuration whose security consequence is unstated gets set for convenience. The note says so now, and an arm holds the claim it rests on: measured over the shipped tree by the environment variable rather than by a list of adapter names, **exactly one file** puts a credential into a subprocess — `PglsConnection`. A new adapter passing `PGPASSWORD` or `MYSQL_PWD` fails that arm whatever it is called.

- ⚠️ **`SafeFileWriter` wrote through a symlink, and its own header said it guarded "the two ways that destroys work" while there were three.** The temporary name was the path plus the PID — **predictable** — and `file_put_contents` opens `O_CREAT|O_TRUNC`, which follows a symlink placed at that name: the formatted SQL landed at a target of somebody's choosing, and the `rename()` afterwards moved the symlink onto the migration path, so the damage survived and looked like an ordinary file. Two layers now, and both are needed: the name carries `random_bytes(8)`, so there is nothing to pre-place; and the open is `fopen(..., 'x')` — `O_CREAT|O_EXCL`, which fails with EEXIST on a path that already exists, including a symlink and including one whose target does not. The random name is also what lets `x` be safe: with a PID-based name a stray from a killed run would have wedged every later write of a process, which is now impossible. ⚠️ **One arm proving this had to be written structurally and says so:** the behavioral version is no longer expressible, because a random name leaves no name for a test — or an attacker — to aim at. That is the first layer working, and it makes the second unobservable from outside; the arm therefore guards the edit that would bring the bug back, a later reader "simplifying" `x` to `w` in a class where the consequence is invisible.

- **Two architecture guards were corrected by the change above rather than worked around.** `OneWriterSeamTest` pinned `@file_put_contents(` as one of *"the three things that make the seam safe"* — it now pins the `x` open, the write, and the random name, and says that the refusal semantics are a **fourth** safety property rather than a refactor. ⚠️ And `FastPathArchTest` read `file_get_contents()` to find who wires `SingleFileResolver`, so a **docblock** naming it counted as wiring: a cross-reference pointing at the package's existing absoluteness test tripped a guard from a file that wires nothing. It reads comment-stripped code now, with a control proving a real wiring site cannot hide in a string literal. A composition site is always code, and a guard that punishes somebody for pointing at the right convention teaches the wrong lesson.

- ⚠️ **PostgreSQL 18's named not-null constraint reached NO rule at all, and the statement it hides is the one `PG.L2.SET_NOT_NULL_SCAN` exists to report.** 18 keeps not-null constraints in `pg_constraint` with `contype = 'n'` and accepts `ALTER TABLE orders ADD CONSTRAINT orders_email_nn NOT NULL email`.
