That classifies as `AddConstraint`, which `SetNotNullChange` refused — it required `AlterTable` — and `ConstraintShape::of()` fell through every arm to `null`. **Measured on PostgreSQL 18.0**, which is what makes it the same finding and not a lookalike: without `NOT VALID` the constraint lands `convalidated = t`, so the server scanned every row, and the statement holds `AccessExclusiveLock` while it does. ⚠️ **`NOT VALID` here saves the SCAN, not the lock level** — measured, the deferred variant takes `AccessExclusiveLock` too, so the lock is brief rather than absent and a remediation promising a weaker one would be wrong. ⚠️ **The real defect was that `null` carried two meanings.** It was the deliberate exit for a form the rule clears — a foreign key already `NOT VALID`, a unique promoted `USING INDEX`, a table born in this migration — *and* the fallthrough for a form nobody had thought about, so an unconsidered constraint kind was indistinguishable from a considered one. `ConstraintShape::Unrecognized` carries the second meaning now, and the rule answers `undetermined` with a reason rather than a silent pass, through the seam `ReadsMigrationStatements::verdict()` documents for exactly this case: *a classifier that meets a case its data does not cover*. ⚠️ **`EXCLUDE` was the other thing falling through, and an existing arm pinned that silence** on the stated grounds that the rule "only speaks to the kinds whose remediation it knows". Both halves are wrong: measured on 18.0 an `EXCLUDE` add takes `AccessExclusiveLock` plus a `ShareLock` while it builds the backing index, and it has no safe sequence *at all* — the server refuses `NOT VALID` on it outright (`EXCLUDE constraints cannot be marked NOT VALID`) and there is no `USING INDEX` promotion. So the honest finding names a maintenance window; saying nothing was the worse answer. The named not-null form deliberately stays `null` in the shape with the owner written beside it, because `PG.L2.SET_NOT_NULL_SCAN` reports the identical operation and two rules on one statement would be two remediations for one problem. ⚠️ **And coverage caught a control of this change that held for the wrong reason:** the arm proving the deliberate exits stay silent passed the table as *created in this migration*, so all four of its cases were cleared by a different carve-out and none of the four reasons it named was exercised. A control that holds for the wrong reason reads as proof of the thing it never touched.

- ⚠️ **`PG.L7.DATA_CHECKSUMS_DISABLED` told operators that turning checksums on means a new cluster and a dump/restore. Since PostgreSQL 12 it means a maintenance window.** `pg_checksums --enable` turns them on in the existing data directory. **Verified rather than read off the page:** `pg_checksums --help` on 18.0 lists `-e, --enable`, and run against a running cluster the tool answers `pg_checksums: error: cluster must be shut down` — so the shutdown requirement is real and is the *only* requirement. The two plans differ by orders of magnitude, and the sentence sits in the finding, which is exactly where one of them gets scheduled. ⚠️ **The cause was a missing category, not a wrong sentence.** `changeable` knew four costs — `session`, `reload`, `restart`, `initdb` — so a setting changeable in place only while the server is stopped had nowhere to go and landed in the most expensive bucket, whose text reads *"moving it means a new cluster and a dump/restore"*. `SettingChangeCost::Offline` now sits between `Restart` and `Initdb`, and it is `isFixable()`: calling an offline change unfixable suppresses a proposal somebody can carry out. ⚠️ **Three places described the right path and the fourth was the one a reader saw.** The rule's docblock named `pg_checksums`, the evidence register named it, the tool's own documentation named it — and the finding recommended a migration, because the sentence is produced by a `match` on the matrix entry and that entry said `initdb`. Nothing compared them. An arm does now, and it asserts the *relation* rather than the wording: whatever remedy tool the evidence names, the finding has to name it too, which survives a rewrite of either text. **The sweep the finding asked for came back clean:** exactly two entries in `server-settings.json` were `initdb`, and the other one is right — MySQL fixes `lower_case_table_names` at initialization and changing it afterwards is unsupported, so it keeps the category and proves it still means something. ⚠️ **Two tests pinned the wrong advice and are rewritten rather than adjusted**, because their reasoning was the defect: one required the message to say *"new cluster"*, *"dump/restore"* and *"cannot be changed on this one"* on the stated grounds that `pg_checksums` needs the cluster shut down — a right premise with a conclusion that does not follow, since shut down is not the same as unchangeable. The other explained the absent `downtime_class` with *"the setting is fixed at initdb, so there is no operation"*; the absence is still correct and the reason is not — there is an operation, it is simply not a migration step.

- ⚠️ **`PG.L3.CONCURRENTLY_IN_TRANSACTION` handed a `DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY` a fix plan that begins by CREATING the index the migration is trying to remove.** The rule keys on the `CONCURRENTLY` keyword, which is right — PostgreSQL refuses `DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY`, `REINDEX … CONCURRENTLY` and `ALTER TABLE … DETACH PARTITION … CONCURRENTLY` inside a transaction block for the same reason — but its remediation called `forCreateIndex()` unconditionally, and a docblock asserted *"the statement is a create-index either way"*, which the rule's own test disproved two arms below by firing it on a drop. **Measured by reading `steps[2]` for each form:** the create form gets `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY <name> …` (correct), the **drop** form gets the same thing, `DETACH PARTITION` gets it with an **unfilled `{{index}}` placeholder**, and `REINDEX … CONCURRENTLY` — a fourth form the finding did not name — gets it too. ⚠️ **`forDropIndex()` was in the same class the whole time**, used by the sister rule `PG.L2.DROP_INDEX_NOT_CONCURRENT`, with a docblock explaining precisely why reusing the create-side sweep is wrong: *"a template that reused the create-side sweep would tell somebody to drop an index the failure never created."* The knowledge was in the tree and the shipped material contradicted it. The material now branches on the statement's kind, and **the third arm is the load-bearing one**: a form that is neither a create nor a drop gets the two steps that are true for every form — bound the wait, and take the statement out of the migrator's transaction, which *is* the fix this finding names — and no statement whose parts this package was never handed. An unfilled placeholder is worse than no SQL: it looks like a statement, and pasting it produces a syntax error rather than an obviously missing step. A general arm now asserts that no plan for any of the four forms carries one, and a discrimination arm keeps the create side working, since every other arm would be satisfied by a rule that stopped producing index SQL altogether.

- ⚠️ **`SET LOCAL lock_timeout` does nothing outside a transaction, and this package both accepted it as a bound AND handed it to the one kind of migration that has no transaction.** `StrongLockStatements::setsTimeout()` matched either scope form without looking at the statement's transaction context, so a migration declaring `public $withinTransaction = false` — which every `CONCURRENTLY` migration must — ran its strong-lock DDL with no bound at all while `PG.L3.MISSING_LOCK_TIMEOUT` reported it as bounded. **Measured on PostgreSQL 18.0** on a real autocommit session: `SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '3s'` leaves `lock_timeout` at `0` and answers `WARNING: SET LOCAL can only be used in transaction blocks`, while a plain `SET` leaves it at `3s` and `BEGIN; SET LOCAL …` leaves it at `3s` until the commit. The statement **succeeds** either way, so the only evidence is a notice most deploys never surface. ⚠️ **The other half is the worse one: the package generated this exact combination.** `TimeoutPreambleTemplate` rendered `SET LOCAL` unconditionally while the comment on the line above promised *"a `CONCURRENTLY` migration, which has no transaction, uses the plain form"*, and `ConcurrentlyTemplate` builds precisely such a migration — so the remediation produced the configuration the rule then read as safe, and the rule's own finding text had been recommending the plain `SET` all along. The template takes the transaction context now, with **no default**: a default would have to be `true`, and a caller who simply forgot would get the form that does nothing. ⚠️ **The two tests the finding called "pinning the defect" were doing something subtler, and it is worth naming because it is the more common failure:** neither asserted anything false. `lockDigest()` defaults to in-transaction, so every scope form was only ever asked the easy question; and the template test asserted `SET LOCAL` and then checked that the **note** mentioned the concurrent index build — the promise rather than the behavior. The case was untested, not mispinned, and the arms are added rather than reversed. The finding now names **which** mistake was made, because telling an operator no timeout was set while they are looking at the line that sets it reads as a false positive, and a rule that reads as wrong gets switched off. The same predicate also masked no string literals, so an `->insert()` whose value spelled the preamble counted too — the same false green through the other door, closed with the shared mask.

- ⚠️ **Seven rules each masked string literals with their own copy of one regex, and every copy hard-coded the PostgreSQL answer to two questions MySQL answers differently — so on MySQL the content of a value was read as syntax.** The pattern `'(?:[^']|'')*'` encodes two decisions the **driver** owns, and `DriverCanonicalization` had already answered both: `stringLiteralDelimiters()` and `usesBackslashStringEscapes()`, which the statement splitter has read all along. The rules were the only layer that asked nobody. ⚠️ **The reachable half needs no exotic SQL at all:** MySQL escapes a quote with a backslash by default and PostgreSQL does not, so `DEFAULT 'it\'s LOCK=NONE'` masked to `''s LOCK=NONE'` and leaked a clause into a scan about clauses — on any MySQL migration with an escaped apostrophe, no raw SQL required. The second half is the double quote: MySQL's manual says a string is enclosed within either quote character, and `ANSI_QUOTES` is not in the default `sql_mode` — **measured on 8.4.10**, `@@sql_mode` is `ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY,STRICT_TRANS_TABLES,NO_ZERO_IN_DATE,NO_ZERO_DATE,ERROR_FOR_DIVISION_BY_ZERO,NO_ENGINE_SUBSTITUTION`, and `ANSI_QUOTES` appears **nowhere** in the framework's source. So `"` is now MySQL's second delimiter, which also fixes a refusal: `VALUES ("O'Brien"); ALTER TABLE …` came back as `unterminatedLiteral` over a batch the server accepts, because the apostrophe opened a scan that ran to the end. ⚠️ **`MY.L3.MISSING_LOCK_WAIT_TIMEOUT` was the worst case and it was NOT one of the seven** — `MetadataLockStatements::setsTimeout()` masked nothing whatsoever, so `->insert()` of the string `set lock_wait_timeout = 5` counted as a preamble and the rule went silent on every `ALTER` after it. A false green on the one rule whose entire job is to notice an absence. The mask is one driver-fed class now, and a guard in `OneOwnerPerConventionTest` — the file whose premise is *a second way to say the same thing is not a redundancy, it is a fork* — refuses an eighth copy, because that copy would be green on every test it shipped with while being wrong about the engine it was not written against. ⚠️ **Its first draft accused two innocents**, `TableMembers` reading a sequence out of `nextval('…')` and `EnumValueAddition` reading a value out of `ADD VALUE '…'`: a `preg_match` that **reads** a literal is not a mask and must not be forced into one, so the scan looks for a `preg_replace` and the two PostgreSQL catalog parsers that legitimately hard-code the grammar are declared exceptions with their reasons. The splitter's discrimination arm had to be measured rather than assumed for the same reason — the obvious control, that same batch on PostgreSQL, splits into the identical two statements there for an entirely different reason, so the backslash is the only honest fork.

- ⚠️ **`PG.L2.CONSTRAINT_NOT_VALIDATED` said adding a foreign key takes an `AccessExclusive` lock on both tables, which inverted the advice: the real lock does not block readers.** The package's own deploy check had it right — *"PostgreSQL locks it SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE"* — so two files stated one fact in opposite ways, and the wrong one was in the rule a reader acts on. **Measured on PostgreSQL 18.0**, inside the transaction: the receiving table holds `AccessShareLock` and `ShareRowExclusiveLock`, the referenced table those plus `RowShareLock` — **no `AccessExclusive` on either**. And against a held `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE`: a `SELECT` returns immediately while an `INSERT` waits out its `lock_timeout`. So this blocks **writers**; the old text's *"conflicts with every other lock"* and *"all traffic"* describes `AccessExclusive` and would send a reader to schedule a read outage they do not need — or to underestimate what they actually block. ⚠️ **The evidence register presented an eleven-year-old incident as a property of the current version:** the GoCardless outage carried `applies_to: PostgreSQL 18`, when the referenced-side lock was weakened in **9.5**. It now names the version it describes and is kept for the mechanism — the queue behind a slow validation, which is real and unchanged. A new arm takes the lock level from the **server** and holds both files to it, so the two cannot drift apart again. The other `AccessExclusive` claims in the PostgreSQL rules were sighted and verified on 18.0: `ADD COLUMN` and `DROP INDEX` without `CONCURRENTLY` really do take it.

- ⚠️ **A least-privilege audit could never come back clean, because the privilege probe asked for a table nothing in this package reads — and the published docs told operators to grant it.** The `table statistics` area probed `SELECT` on `pg_statistic`; the only occurrences of that name in `src/` were the probe itself and one docblock, while the size and estimate rules read `pg_class.reltuples`, `pg_relation_size()` and the `pg_stat_*` views. PostgreSQL withholds `pg_statistic` from the public on purpose — the user-facing view is `pg_stats`. **Measured on PostgreSQL 18.0 for a role holding exactly `pg_monitor`, which this package's own README recommends:** `pg_statistic` false, `pg_class` true, `pg_stats` true, `pg_stat_all_tables` true. So every managed instance came back partial with `AUDIT.CATALOG.UNREAD.INSUFFICIENT_PRIVILEGE` saying *"no rule may reason about table size"* while the size rules had already read it — and under `strict_undetermined`, the setting this package recommends, such an audit could never go green. The probe reads the real source now. ⚠️ **As a real read rather than `has_table_privilege`, and that distinction is measured too:** that function reports the relation's ACL, and for a view over a privileged function it can say yes where the read fails — a `pg_read_all_data` role gets `true` for `pg_hba_file_rules` and then *"permission denied for function pg_hba_file_rules"*. ⚠️ **The documentation half was the more urgent one:** `least-privilege.md` instructed operators to `GRANT SELECT ON pg_statistic`, opening column-level data distributions for no gain, and `audit.md` claimed `pg_read_all_stats` provides it, which it does not. Both corrected, and the arm that *pinned the false skip as expected* now holds the opposite.

- ⚠️ **The administrative-privilege vocabulary knew 14 names where MySQL 8.4 files 54 — an application account holding `BINLOG_ADMIN`, `BACKUP_ADMIN` or `CLONE_ADMIN` passed both privilege rules in silence.** The whole replication family the rule's own docblock names as its scope (*"change replication"*) was absent, as were the encryption, redo, audit, flush and role families. ⚠️ **The existing real-server arm could not have found this:** it offers every *listed* name to the server and requires acceptance, which proves each entry is real and by construction can never find one that is missing. A second arm now diffs the other way, and it takes its scope from the **server** rather than from a hand-kept idea of what administrative means: every privilege MySQL reports with `Context = 'Server Admin'` must be either an entry or a **declared exclusion with a reason**. It is one-directional on purpose — a listed name this server does not report is not an error, since `SET_ANY_DEFINER` exists on 8.4 and not on 8.0, and `FILE` and `GRANT OPTION` are administrative under other contexts. 37 entries added, each with its own `grants` prose rather than just a name; six exclusions declared, among them `USAGE` (every account has it) and `SET_USER_ID` (an 8.0 name 8.4 refuses, split into two that *are* listed). ⚠️ **And the reconciliation arm earned itself again:** it refused `PROXY`, because `GRANT PROXY` takes an account and never `ON *.*` — so it cannot be expressed in the shape these rules match, and it is excluded with that reason rather than quietly dropped.

- ⚠️ **`MY.L4.ENUM_CHANGE` said a member rename runs `INSTANT` and takes no lock. Measured on a real server, a rename is a table copy that FAILS.** The rule's table, its finding text, two language strings, its evidence note and its portal page all carried one claim — *"a rename takes no lock at all"* — and it was the **collation-equivalent special case generalised into a rule**. The engine compares member names positionally under the column's collation, so only a respelling the collation calls equal is a no-op. Measured on MySQL 8.4.10: `'b'` → `'B'` with `ALGORITHM=INSTANT` is accepted; `'b'` → `'x'` with `ALGORITHM=INSTANT` is refused with **1846** *"Need to rebuild the table to change column type"*; and with the default algorithm on rows still holding `'b'` it fails with **1265** *"Data truncated"*, because `STRICT_TRANS_TABLES` is in the default `sql_mode`. ⚠️ **So the rule warned about a silent reinterpretation and said nothing about an abort** — the opposite failure. The two cases are now separate rows everywhere, and a second contradiction goes with them: one language string called a rename *"the only member change MySQL performs instantly"* while the rule's own table listed an append as instant too. ⚠️ **`tests/MySql` contained no `ENUM(` statement at all**, and the unit arms pinned the message *text*, which confirms a claim instead of measuring it — including two comments that stated the false fact in prose. There are now four real-server arms holding what MySQL actually does with a member list.

- ⚠️ **Shadow mode was permanently `undetermined` on MySQL for most real schemas, because the replay threw away the one line `mysqldump` wrote for it.** `mysqldump` emits tables **alphabetically** and puts `/*!40014 SET … FOREIGN_KEY_CHECKS=0 */` in the header precisely because of that ordering — and `MysqlSchemaDumpReader` skips every whole-statement `/*!…*/` as a *client* directive, when the MySQL manual's Comments section is explicit that a versioned comment is executed **by the server**. **Verified on MySQL 8.4.10 with Laravel's own dump options** (`--skip-comments --skip-set-charset --tz-utc --no-data`), which the original finding could only assume: the header carries both `FOREIGN_KEY_CHECKS=0` and `UNIQUE_CHECKS=0`, and a dump of `orders` → `users` writes the **child first**. Replaying it header-stripped fails with `ERROR 1824 (HY000) Failed to open the referenced table 'users'`, so `orders`/`users`, `comments`/`posts` and `accounts`/`users` all made shadow mode a standing `ShadowMysqlReplayFailed`. The replay now issues `SET SESSION foreign_key_checks = 0, unique_checks = 0` before the first statement — narrower than honoring the dump's directives, because it does not depend on which `/*!…*/` lines a given mysqldump version writes. ⚠️ **No existing arm could see this:** every real-server replay test used a hand-written one-table dump with no foreign key, and the reader's unit test *pinned* the discarding as though it were free. There is now a real-server arm whose child table sorts before its parent, and it checks that the constraint **landed** — `foreign_key_checks = 0` skips the check, not the definition, and a replay that silently dropped the foreign key would leave a shadow schema that is not the project's.

- ⚠️ **`PG.L6.SERIAL_NOT_IDENTITY`'s "good" example was its "bad" example, spelled shorter — and the rule page said so in prose.** The shipped example recommended `$table->id()`, and `Blueprint::id()` is literally `return $this->bigIncrements($column)`. **Measured through the installed grammar:** both halves compile to `create table "orders" ("id" bigserial not null primary key)` — byte-identical — so a reader who followed the cure got the finding back. The page went further and asserted that *"Laravel's `id()` produces an identity column on PostgreSQL; `bigIncrements()` produces a `serial`"*, which is false in both halves. ⚠️ **The package knew at two places** — `PgsqlCanonicalization` says *"bigserial is what `$table->id()` emits"* and the rule itself says *"bigIncrements() still produces serial"*. The knowledge was in the tree and the shipped advice contradicted it, because nothing compiled the example. A guard now does, over **every** rule with Blueprint examples, and asserts the general property rather than a special case: a `good` example must not compile to the same SQL as its own `bad` half. ⚠️ Each rule is compiled with **its own** engine — `MY.L9.TYPE_IMPLICIT_CAST` contrasts `timestamp` with `dateTime`, two types on MySQL and one on PostgreSQL, so judging it as pgsql reported a correct example as broken.

- ⚠️ **The eighth suppression layer would have KILLED the console and JSON reports the first time it ever hid a finding.** `SuppressionResolver::ORDER` listed seven sources while the resolver ran eight, and `Result::countsBySuppressionSource()` seeds its counters from exactly that constant before incrementing per suppression. **Measured:** `$counts['cross_source_dedupe']++` raises *Undefined array key*, which under Laravel's `HandleExceptions` is an `ErrorException` — so the report would have died, not miscounted. The only thing preventing it was that the layer is unreachable in every run. The layer is in `ORDER` now, and the counter seeds unknown sources instead of incrementing them, because the next layer will be added by somebody editing the resolver and not that file. ⚠️ **Its source key is renamed `cross-source-dedupe` → `cross_source_dedupe`**, which was the only hyphenated key among eight snake_case siblings and appears in the JSON envelope's `suppressed_by_source` object. Renamed now because it is free now: the layer has never fired, so no consumer has ever seen the old spelling — the same rename after it starts working would be a breaking envelope change. Four arms that enumerated all seven keys to say *one is 1 and the rest are 0* now derive the key set from `ORDER`; the one literal that stays is the order documentation itself, whose whole job is to be checked against prose by a reader. And `SecurityFindingOrder`'s docblock no longer claims the cross-source merge is blocked on a mechanism that exists: `Location::inMigration()` does take an object name and `MigrationVerdicts` passes it, so the blocker is rule-level coverage — *almost no rule names one* — rather than a missing parameter.

- **The two dedupe suppression sources guard their shipped register too, and a guard now watches the policy instead of the instances.** The finding named three loaders; an arm written over the *form* found **five**. `PairedViewDedupeSuppressionSource` and `RlsDedupeSuppressionSource` carried the same `catch (JsonException) { return []; }` on a shipped file. ⚠️ **Their direction is the safe one and the note says so rather than overstating it:** an empty dedupe table suppresses nothing, so a broken artifact makes this package report a *duplicate* finding rather than hide a real one — noise, not the silent pass the privilege vocabulary had. They refuse anyway for a reason independent of direction: a shipped file that cannot be read is a broken installation, and a run quietly reporting duplicates leaves an operator with output they cannot explain. Exempting them would also mean the policy needs an allowlist, which is what let the loaders drift apart to begin with.

- ⚠️ **`sqlens:format` refuses an unresolvable dialect instead of silently formatting as PostgreSQL — and it used to REWRITE the file for the wrong engine.** The line read `?? Dialect::Pgsql` while the comment directly above it promised the run *"says so rather than silently choosing one"*. It chose one, and the cost was not a worse-formatted file but a wrong one: the core is **not** dialect-neutral at two points that change bytes. `SqlTokenizer` branches on the dialect for comment syntax, and `SqlToken` branches on it for keyword case — including the 21 MySQL-unreserved words it protects on purpose *"because MySQL on Linux compares table names by case"*. **Measured through the core formatter:** `select id from comment` comes back as `FROM comment` under MySQL and `FROM COMMENT` under PostgreSQL, so a MySQL project with a table called `comment`, `view` or `json` had its file rewritten to name a table that does not exist on a server with `lower_case_table_names=0` — and without `--check` the original was already overwritten. ⚠️ **Refusing does not cost the no-database run**, which is the objection this had to answer: `DialectResolver` states where that property actually lives — *"an explicit dialect short-circuits everything: the connection is never read … That is the property the north-star rests on."* A fresh checkout, a pre-commit hook and a machine with nothing installed all keep working by naming the dialect once, in `--dialect` or `sqlens.format.dialect`; the guess was never what made them work. The refusal is raised with the other up-front checks, before any file is touched. **`FormatterRegistry::resolve()` loses its `$dialectResolved` parameter** rather than defaulting it: the branch it guarded admitted only backends answering for both dialects, on the stated grounds that such a backend is *"safe to run blind, which is precisely what the built-in core is"* — and the core is not, so it was protection that did not protect. With the refusal upstream no run can reach that branch, and a branch no run can enter is dead code held alive by the test that covers it.

- **Two MySQL index rules stop handing out PostgreSQL.** `MY.L7.INDEX_UNUSED` told a MySQL user to `DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY` — not MySQL syntax — and called the index *one more relation for vacuum to walk*, of an engine that has no `VACUUM` at all. ⚠️ **The sister rule in the same directory knew and said so**: `MY.L7.INDEX_REDUNDANT`'s docblock reads *"there is no CONCURRENTLY to ask for"* while its own finding text repeated the vacuum sentence, so the fix is two rules rather than the one that was reported. Both now name the **buffer pool**, and the remediation is the form the standalone statement actually takes: `DROP INDEX name ON table ALGORITHM=INPLACE LOCK=NONE`. ⚠️ **Measured against a live server rather than read off the manual, because the obvious correction is also wrong** — the comma form `DROP INDEX i ON t, ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE`, which is how `ALTER TABLE` takes its options, is refused with `ERROR 1064`; shipping it would have reproduced this very defect one syntax away. The vacuum sentence is struck rather than translated: MySQL has no equivalent here, and a substitute would be another claim about the engine. A guard now scans the MySQL driver's **string literals** for PostgreSQL vocabulary — literals and not lines, because two files in that driver name those concepts in prose precisely in order to deny them, and both of those comments are correct.

- **`sqlens:drift` and `sqlens:postdeploy --expect-shadow` bound the session they read the pending state on, and the bound now lives in the resolver instead of at each call site.** Three commands read which migrations are pending; `sqlens:lint` bounded its borrowed session first and the other two went straight at the connection with no budget at all — `hasTable`, `SELECT migration FROM migrations` and `max(batch)`, every one of them through `useWritePdo()`, so the read went deliberately to the write side where a stuck lock costs the most. ⚠️ **`sqlens:drift` is the likeliest of the three to be pointed at production**, since comparing what is against what should be is its whole purpose, and an unbounded catalog read there can sit behind a lock indefinitely — the exact harm the lint path's own comment says it bounds against. `SessionGuard` is now a **required** constructor argument of `PendingMigrationResolver`, not an optional one: discipline at each call site had already been tried and had a two-in-three failure rate, and a default budget would merely have been a second answer to *how long may this run wait*, free to diverge from the configured one in silence. The bound sits after the reachability check and before the first catalog read, which is the only correct window rather than a matter of taste — `bind()` writes with `Connection::statement()`, which throws on an unreachable server, so binding any earlier would replace the named `ConnectionUnreachable` skip with a stack trace. Nesting is safe by construction and a test holds it so: `bind()` snapshots whatever it finds and restores exactly that, which is what lets the lint path keep its own outer bound rather than having the inner release quietly unbound the rest of the run.

- **`capture.shadow.connection` is read, and the second lock on the only path that creates and drops databases finally has a caller.** The key is documented in the shipped config, the schema validates it, and the shadow-mode page tells a project to point shadow mode at a dedicated `CREATEDB` role through it — the pattern Prisma calls a `shadowDatabaseUrl`. **No code read it.** A project that followed that advice got provisioning, the maintenance link and the template connection built on the role and the server of the connection *under examination*: either an insufficient-privileges failure that sends the reader to the wrong role, or — if the examining role happens to hold `CREATEDB` — `CREATE` and `DROP DATABASE` on the instance being compared. ⚠️ **`ShadowTargetIdentity` was written for exactly this and had no caller either.** Its docblock calls it *"the second lock … mechanical rather than advisory: a collision is a refusal, never a warning"*, and its refusal text names this very key. A shadow connection resolving to the same place as the examined one is now an `undetermined` with a named reason, decided from configuration **before anything is opened** — ahead of the replica probe, because a misconfiguration knowable without a server should not wait behind a round trip that can fail for its own reasons. `direct_connection` still wins when both are named, and that order is mechanical rather than a preference: template operations and `CREATE DATABASE` do not survive a transaction pooler, so the link they run on has to be the one that provably bypasses one.

- ⚠️ **The `mode` config key is removed, and a published config that still carries it gets a notice rather than a refusal.** It promised to choose *"how SQLens obtains the SQL it reasons about"* — `pretend`, `shadow` or `static` — and no code path consulted it for that choice: `sqlens:lint` reads `--shadow`, `sqlens:drift` replays into a throwaway database, and the catalog suites read a catalog. Its single reader was the run header, where it **labeled** every run with whatever the file said. That label was wrong exactly where it mattered: `sqlens:drift` announced the configured value while replaying into a shadow database, and `sqlens:postdeploy` did the same while capturing in pretend — each of them already building a `RunMetadata` carrying the honest answer, so one document held two mode fields filled from different places. Every producer passes its real mode now, and the collector requires it, so a new producer cannot forget. ⚠️ **One of the three values it offered never existed as a capture strategy at all:** `static` is the label a catalog run wears — the audit and security suites open a database and reason about no migration text — while its own docblock claimed it meant *"read statically from the migration source, without a database"*. A project could therefore configure a way of working that no code implements.

- **`sqlens:lint --pretend --shadow` is refused instead of quietly running in shadow mode.** `--pretend` was declared in the signature, documented as *"the default, do-no-harm mode"*, printed by `--help`, described in the portal and in the Boost skill — and read nowhere: the mode was decided by `--shadow` alone. So a run that named the harmless mode created and dropped a database. ⚠️ **That flag is the one a cautious script appends BECAUSE it is cautious** — an alias that sets it, a habit from another tool, a pipeline spelling the default out — and each of those turned into a database-creating run, the exact inversion the flag exists to prevent. The same command already refuses every `--roundtrip` combination by name for this reason, and says why in its own comment: *"none of them is a silent drop of the flag, which would let a user believe a roundtrip happened"*. Refused rather than made to win over `--shadow`: two flags naming opposite modes mean somebody believes something about the run that is not true, and picking one for them leaves the belief in place. `--pretend` on its own keeps working — it names the default a run already has.

- ⚠️ **`use_statistics` does something now, and for a project that set it to `false` that is a behavior change.** The key is documented as deciding *"whether checks may reason about the server's table statistics"*, it is validated by the schema and a profile may override it — and the only code reading it was a lint branch filtering for rules implementing `StatisticsDependent`, which no shipped rule does. The filter was therefore always empty, ON and OFF were identical, and meanwhile the deploy gate escalated severities by table size without ever asking. A project that switched it off *"where reproducibility matters more than depth"* got statistics-driven severities anyway, invisibly: a severity that was escalated looks exactly like a severity that was assigned. The preflight now withholds the statistics **reader** when the key is off, which reuses the path the escalation already handles and explains — findings stand at the severity lint gave them, and the checks that needed a reader have already said so by name. ⚠️ **On the shipped default (`false`) the deploy gate stops raising severities by table size.** That is what the configuration has always said it does; the switch is being honored rather than changed, and a project that wants the escalation sets `use_statistics` to `true`.

- **Four shipped sentences stop promising a suite that has already landed.** `config/sqlens.php`, the `StatisticsDependent` marker, the `statistics_unavailable` reason and the lint notice all said the statistics reader *"arrives with the audit suite"* or *"will run once the audit suite lands"*. It landed. The honest reason a lint run has no statistics is not a schedule but the run itself: it reads migration source and opens no catalog session, while the audit and preflight runs do. The notice now says that, and names the two commands that can answer instead. ⚠️ **One of those sentences was the problem it claimed to solve:** the marker's docblock said it *"keeps `use_statistics` an honest three-valued switch rather than a config key with no effect"* — while nothing implemented the marker, so the key had precisely the effect it was said to be saved from.

- ⚠️ **`Driver::readers()` is removed from the driver contract, which is a breaking change for a third-party driver — and it is the second time this was found.** The method sat on the contract for eight months answering `[]` from both shipped drivers, with no caller anywhere in the source: the live-catalog readers come from `CatalogReaderFactory`, which describes itself as *"the ONE place that knows which engine gets which"*. `Driver` calls itself the extension API third-party rule packs implement, so a method nothing reads costs an implementer a decision, a stub and a docblock and returns nothing for any of it. ⚠️ **A test pinned the emptiness**, which is what made it read as deliberate rather than dead — a documented intermediate state on the way to being filled. What replaces it guards the general form instead of that one method: every method the contract declares has a caller in the shipped source, read out of the interface by reflection so a method added tomorrow is covered too. **A third-party driver removes its own `readers()` stub; nothing else changes.** The `rules()` docblock is rewritten in the same pass — it still said *"Empty for now, filled later by the PostgreSQL and MySQL rule packs"* while the PostgreSQL driver contributes 108 rules and the MySQL one 100. A sentence describing an intermediate state has to be retired when the state is left, or it starts describing the reader's expectations instead of the code.

- **`sqlens:doctor` reports what the configuration validator found; it does not refuse on it.** Every other command stops, because a key this package does not know is a key it **ignores** and ignoring is silent. This one describes an environment, and an operator reaches for it precisely when something is off — a doctor that will not start because the configuration is broken has inverted its own purpose, and its suite has said so since long before the validator arrived there: *"it DESCRIBES a broken profile rather than dying on it, the one command that must survive it"*. The console report gains a line per violated key and the JSON document a `config` section carrying `status`, the violations and the notices. ⚠️ **`status` is present when everything is fine too**, for the same reason the guard section reports `off` as a value rather than an absence: a field that appears only when something is wrong answers "is my configuration alright?" with silence, and silence reads exactly like a doctor that never looked. Nothing is lost by reporting instead of refusing — a run that *matters* still stops, at whichever command was going to do the work.

- **`sqlens:postdeploy` resolves a profile like every other suite command, instead of announcing one it never looked up.** `--profile=postdeploy` was an option default, and `postdeploy` is not a profile: there is no such enum case and no such block under `sqlens.profiles`, so the name could not have selected anything even if something had tried. Three consequences, all gone: `SQLENS_PROFILE=ci sqlens:postdeploy` ran as postdeploy with nothing saying so, though the configuration promises a name "outranks those defaults for every command"; `--profile=bogus` was accepted and written into every finding's subject, where every other command answers `Misconfiguration` and names the legal set; and the report contradicted itself, the header reading `local` while the findings underneath said `postdeploy`. **The default is now `predeploy`**, because this command runs in the deploy window one step after the gate that already uses it. On the shipped base that changes nothing — `predeploy` overrides only `security.min_severity`, to the value the base already carries — and what it buys is the reason `ci` and `predeploy` name that key at all: a project that lowers its base floor while working through a backlog no longer lowers this gate with it.

- **The MCP server's second transport check is gone, because it had become unreachable.** Its own comment predicted this: *"the configuration validator already refuses anything else, so reaching this means it was bypassed"* — a sentence that was false while the validator had no caller on that command, and true the moment it got one. What it did happens a step earlier and says more: the validator names the **key** and the legal value, where the old line only said which transport this build speaks, so a project with a typo'd `agent.mcp.transort` was told about stdio and never about the typo.

- **Every command checks the configuration before it does anything, not three of eleven.** A key this package does not know is a key it **ignores**, and ignoring is silent: a run with `levl: 3` uses the default level and comes back green because it checked less. The validator that catches this reached exactly the commands that write a report — a coincidence of where the method happened to live. The eight it missed are the ones where it matters most: `predeploy` and `postdeploy` run **inside the deploy window**, `baseline`, `drift` and `format` write files into the repository, and the MCP server binds tools to whatever the configuration says. Two of those carry comments asserting the validator has already refused the shape they are about to read — one justifies falling back on an unusable threshold that way, the other says reaching a branch "means it was bypassed". Neither sentence was true of the run. The check now lives in its own trait, self-sufficient rather than borrowing two helpers from two other traits, and a check over the service provider's own registration keeps a twelfth command from being added without it. ⚠️ **The arm asserts the message, not the exit code:** several of these answer with the misconfiguration exit for reasons of their own — an unreachable database, no migration paths — so an exit-code check was green on ten of eleven against a tree where nothing called the validator at all.

- **A configured baseline whose file is missing now says so, instead of reading as a baseline that accepts nothing.** The two produce an identical report and mean opposite things: one is a project with a clean slate, the other is a project whose accepted findings have all come back at once — and the reader sees a wall of new findings either way, with no reason given. Both runners now emit a run notice for it, `LINT.BASELINE.ABSENT` and `CAP.L0.BASELINE_ABSENT`, under the new undetermined reason `baseline_absent`. ⚠️ **A notice and not a refusal, deliberately:** `sqlens:baseline` has to be runnable before the file exists — that is how a project creates one — so a configured path with nothing behind it yet is an ordinary state on the way to having a baseline, and a project that knows that about itself can name the reason in `allow_undetermined`. The JSON envelope gains the new reason in its histogram, as every reason before it did; `schema_version` stays at 6. **The shipped registry holds 240 entries**, two more than 0.19.0 — both of them these notices. By category: **145 safety**, 58 security, 17 idiom, 10 performance, 7 convention and 3 privacy. By maturity: **235 stable**, 3 preview, 2 experimental. **160** answer to the level gate; the other **80** carry a severity — 7 critical, 32 high, 29 medium, 8 low and 4 info. Neither carries a `downtime_class` — a missing baseline file is not an operation on a schema — so that count stays at 58 entries.
- **The baseline is found where the configuration says it is, not where the process happened to start.** `sqlens.baseline.path` is documented as repo-relative and the schema refuses an absolute one, and it was then resolved against the **working directory**. That is the normal case rather than an exotic one: `php path/to/artisan` from a monorepo root, a deploy script that changed directory, a queue worker started by a daemon, `Artisan::call()` from a request where the working directory is `public/`. The three sibling keys carrying the same promise — the debt ledger in both runners, the agent's findings path — were already anchored at the application root; only this one was not. It fails in the quiet direction: the file is not found, the baseline reads as **empty**, every accepted finding comes back at once, and nothing says a baseline was configured. Writing had the mirror problem — `sqlens:baseline` created the file wherever the process was, outside the repository the relative path exists to keep it portable within. Both now resolve through one call. ⚠️ An **absolute** path is left alone: a configuration that slips past the validator must not then be read from a third place nobody named.

- **A `url`-configured connection is no longer half-supported.** Laravel runs every connection through `ConfigurationUrlParser`, so `'url' => 'pgsql://…'` **has** a driver — derived from the scheme — while `database.connections.<name>.driver` is simply absent. That is the shape Laravel Cloud, Heroku and every `DATABASE_URL` deployment produce, and nine readers in this package read the key raw. The user-visible symptom was the confusing one: `sqlens:lint`, `sqlens:audit` and `sqlens:doctor` accepted the connection, because they go through the resolver, while `sqlens:drift`, `sqlens:postdeploy`, `sqlens:format` and `sqlens:agent-rules` refused the **same** configuration. ⚠️ **And one of the nine had a side effect**: the runner refuses to open mariadb, sqlite and sqlsrv precisely so that SQLite does not create the file it is pointed at, and a url-configured SQLite fell outside that list — the connection opened, the file appeared, and only then was the driver refused as a non-goal. All nine now go through one call, and a check over the shipped tree keeps the raw key from coming back. The advice text for a connection that really declares no driver names both ways now, not just the key: a url whose scheme names the driver configures one just as much. ⚠️ **This is the second pass at the same class** — the 0.1.0 audit found two of these readers, both were fixed, and the rest of the tree was never swept. The check is what makes a third pass unnecessary.

- **An application that renames its migrations ledger is no longer told the table is missing.** `database.migrations` has two shapes — the name itself, and an array carrying it under `table` — and the two places that build the pending resolver passed the literal `migrations` instead. That was not a default: it **overrode** a decision the application had already made. The consequence is not a silent green, which is what made it easy to miss — `hasTable('migrations')` returns false and the run reports "no migration table". The table exists. The reader goes looking for one that is not missing, and in that application `sqlens:lint` never judges a pending migration and `sqlens:drift` never resolves a pending state, permanently rather than as an edge case. The narrowing now lives in **one** place, which the driver registry — the only reader that had it right — uses as well, and a check over the shipped tree keeps any future caller from naming the table by hand. Both shapes are covered, on both engines, against real servers.

- **"My own time budget" and "the database is in trouble" are told apart correctly on both engines now, by one classifier instead of two wrong ones.** A SQLSTATE alone cannot answer that question. On PostgreSQL the catalog path knew only `57014`, so the reader's own `lock_timeout` — which answers `55P03` — was reported as an unexpected database error with the raw driver message, sending an operator to look for a server fault that does not exist. On MySQL the answer was worse in the other direction: `HY000` was read as "the server was not at fault", and `HY000` is the **general** error class, worn equally by a gone server (2006), a lost connection (2013) and a full disk (1030). Each of those was reported as a bound this package set on itself. `70100` was read as our own timeout too, and it is `ER_QUERY_INTERRUPTED` — somebody else's `KILL QUERY`, the opposite of a bound we chose. All five shapes were **measured** against PostgreSQL 18.0 and MySQL 8.4.10 by provoking each bound rather than taken from the references. The classification now goes through the one classifier the shadow path has always used, and the driver's own error number travels with the SQLSTATE, because on MySQL the SQLSTATE carries no information at all. ⚠️ Two arms that pinned the old behavior are **reversed rather than deleted**, and the detector now reads the driver code off the exception itself as well as off a wrapped one — several catalog readers catch the PDO error directly, and for those it had always answered "no code", which on MySQL means "not a timeout".

- **The PostgreSQL schema-security adapter names the connection it could not reach, not the connection's coordinates.** When `postgrestools` cannot open the database it exits 1 and writes plain text where the JSON report belongs — the ordinary shape of the most likely misconfiguration — and the adapter turned that into a finding whose message spelled out the host, the port and the database name. That message goes through every reporter and leaves the machine. The package masks exactly those values elsewhere, and the stderr excerpt appended to the same sentence is redacted, so the protection reached around precisely the part that needed it. The message now names the **connection**, which is what the reader is holding the configuration for: they find the host under that name, and the report gains nothing by repeating it. ⚠️ A unit test pinned the old text, which means it was an assurance rather than an oversight — it is **reversed rather than deleted**, so the shape stays held and the sign is what changed.

- **The MySQL deploy gate no longer counts a neighbor account's privileges as your own.** `GRANTEE` is rendered `'user'@'host'`, and the grant check matched it with `LIKE "'<role>'@%"`. In `LIKE` an underscore is a **single-character wildcard**, so `'deploy_user'` also matched `'deploy-user'` — and if that neighbor held `ALTER`, the gate read it as held and waved the deploy through. It then dies halfway into `migrate --force`, on the grant error the gate exists to find. A gate that fails **open** is worse than no gate, because it replaces the check somebody would otherwise have made by hand. Measured on MySQL 8.4.10 with exactly that pair of accounts. ⚠️ **The obvious repair is wrong in the other direction and is pinned against:** `substring_index(grantee, '@', 1)` splits at the first `@`, so an email-shaped account — `sqlens@mail.test`, an ordinary thing to call a user — is cut in half, matches nothing, and reads as holding no privileges at all, which this check turns into a false `MISSING_PRIVILEGE` finding. What replaces the `LIKE` is a prefix comparison: no wildcard to escape, no separator to guess at. ⚠️ And one half of the finding did **not** reproduce: a quote in an account name causes no false finding, because `information_schema` renders it **undoubled** (measured on the same server) and the pattern was bound rather than interpolated. The arm stays as a regression guard and says so.

- ⚠️ **The fifth redaction sink was wrong and is not in this release — it was built, shipped to `develop`, and taken back out.** `CheckResult::undetermined()` looked like a safe sink for the same reason the four real ones are: the field explains why something could **not** be read, and a measurement over its forty-three call sites found no identifier in any check's own prose. The measurement searched for `role "…"`. The prose uses **backticks** — `GrantCheck` writes ``the migration connection names the role `deploy` `` — which is one of the three quote styles the redactor handles and the one the scan did not look for. So the sink removed the role name a reader had typed into their own configuration, which is the answer the check exists to give. What the gate caught is worth naming: an arm that pins `names the role on the run it produced, so the withholding is checkable`. The ten deploy checks that compose a driver's message are redacted individually instead, at the seventeen places where the text is a driver's and nothing else, and the factory's docblock carries the reason so the generalization does not look obvious a second time.
- **A driver's error message no longer carries the connection's coordinates into a finding.** Eighteen places wrote `Throwable::getMessage()` into finding and notice text with no redactor at all, and `QueryException::formatMessage()` appends ` (Connection: …, Host: …, Port: …, Database: …, SQL: …)` to **every** query exception on both engines. The leak path is the expected path, not an edge case: a refused catalog read on a managed instance — which the collectors' own comments call the ordinary case — therefore arrived wearing the host, the port and the database name, and went straight into a report. One of the fields involved had a docblock saying in so many words that its text must never be shown as-is because it can carry a host and a database name; it was shown as-is, by two readers, through the audit's notices. The redaction now happens where that text **arrives** — the cross-fact detail, a failed settings reading, an undetermined pooler reading, and a crashed half of a security run — so each covers a whole family of producers and a nineteenth inherits it without knowing it exists. ⚠️ **Deliberately not at `Finding`**, which would be a filter rather than a sink: the shape redactor removes `role "…"`, `user "…"` and `database "…"`, and that is precisely what a **security** finding about a role or a database is made of. These four fields carry error text and nothing else. The diagnosis still travels — `SELECT command denied` survives the redaction, because a report that lost the reason would be a worse one rather than a safer one.

- **A hostname that does not resolve no longer reaches any report, on either engine.** A wrong `DB_HOST` is the most common misconfiguration there is, and it is the one error whose message the redactor could not see: there is no `host=` pair in it, it is not the libpq `connection to server at "…"` preamble, and Laravel's own `Host:` suffix is appended to a **query** exception, which a connect failure never becomes. Every pattern walked straight past the name. Both shapes are now removed, and both were **measured against the real drivers** rather than reconstructed — `could not translate host name "…"` on PostgreSQL, `getaddrinfo for … failed:` on MySQL, the second carrying the name unquoted so the pattern has to stop at `failed` rather than at the first space. ⚠️ There is no server to measure this against and that is the point rather than a gap: a name that does not resolve never reaches one, so the message is the client library's and the shape is the same whatever is running. An ordinary schema error is still untouched — `relation "users" does not exist` passes through whole, because a redaction that ate the diagnosis would trade one unusable report for another.
- **And it is removed for every `--format`, not only for the one that happened to redact.** The two halves of credential redaction were wired to different callers: the value-based pass, which knows the configured password, url and dsn, ran on the `agent` reporter alone; the shape-based pass, which knows what the drivers print, ran at a handful of translation sites. Console, JSON, SARIF and GitHub got whichever one the producer happened to apply. They are now one object, resolved from the container, applied where the notice is built — values first, then shapes, an order that is a correctness property and is stated as one: the shape pass rewrites its matches, so a configured secret sitting inside a recognized shape would be gone before the value pass could learn to recognize it anywhere else in the message.

- **Every catalog function the PostgreSQL driver calls now carries its schema, so no user function can answer for it.** `pg_catalog` is searched implicitly and FIRST, which covers relations — and does not cover functions. Function resolution picks the best candidate among **different** signatures, so a user function taking `text` where the catalog one takes `name`, or matching a type exactly where the catalog one needs a cast, wins. Measured on PostgreSQL 18.0 with a user function in `public`: `array_to_string`, `obj_description` and `array_agg(name)` all resolved to the substitute, and `pg_has_role(current_user, 'no_such_role', 'member')` answered **true for a role that does not exist**. That last one is why this is a security fix and not hygiene — a hijacked privilege check does not crash an audit, it **answers** it, and the report then says green over a forged answer. `SET TRANSACTION READ ONLY` is no defense: the attack needs no write. The precondition is ordinary rather than exotic — any role with `CREATE` on a schema in the audit role's path, which every database created before PostgreSQL 15 and carried forward by `pg_upgrade` still grants to `PUBLIC` on `public`, and which a `"$user"` schema grants by construction. **A pinned `search_path` was tried first and rejected**, because this package deliberately reads the session's own path as data in three places — the default schema scope, `current_schema()` for an unqualified RLS entry, and `pg_table_is_visible()` — and pinning it makes all three answer about `pg_catalog`, which scopes every reading to the system catalog and reports a project's own schemas as empty. Qualification fixes the hole without touching what the session resolves. **Nothing about a reading changes**; the same rows come back, from a catalog that cannot have been substituted.
- **A source guard keeps that true, and it asks the server rather than carrying a list of names.** Of the bare identifiers the query-issuing PostgreSQL surfaces call inside SQL, it asks `pg_proc` which ones PostgreSQL itself resolves in `pg_catalog` — those are exactly the ones a user function can outrank — and requires each to carry the schema. SQL constructs that merely parse like a call (`extract`, `coalesce`, `nullif`, …) and type names used as casts are declared exemptions with their reason, because a schema in front of either is a syntax error rather than a hardening. `Rules/` and `Remediation/` are out of scope on purpose: the SQL there is the user's migration being matched or advice being printed for somebody else to run.

- **Nothing SQLens sets on PostgreSQL outlives its own transaction any more.** The reader bounded its session twice — once as `SET LOCAL` inside the read transaction, once as a plain session `SET` before it — and called the second one "the belt to that brace". Behind PgBouncer in transaction pooling the belt is the damage. A session `SET` there lands on whichever backend carried that one statement and stays on it: `server_reset_query` runs only in session pooling by default, and `track_extra_parameters` can only reset parameters the server announces through `ParameterStatus`, which `statement_timeout`, `lock_timeout` and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` are not. The next session of the **host application** handed that backend inherits a five-second statement timeout and an idle-in-transaction timeout it never chose, and starts losing queries to `57014` and transactions to a FATAL `25P03` — and the reader connection is a copy of the application's own configuration, so it goes through the same pooler. Every bound is now `SET LOCAL`, including `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` and `application_name`, both of which accept it; the transaction is the only scope where the first one has anything to do at all. Nothing is dropped and nothing is weaker: `SET LOCAL` covers a direct connection exactly as well, which is why this is unconditional rather than dependent on a pooler verdict the probe itself admits can read wrong under load. **The pooler probe no longer bounds itself either** — it is the one read in the package that cannot run inside a transaction, because pooling is invisible in one, so its session `SET statement_timeout` was the exact leak it exists to detect, performed by the detector; four constant-time statements now run with whatever bound the connection already carries. **And `sqlens:lint` no longer writes onto a borrowed connection that measures as multiplexed**: before bounding the host's session it asks `pg_backend_pid()` twice, and a changed backend is conclusive evidence of multiplexing that nothing else produces. On that evidence it writes nothing and restores nothing, because a snapshot read on one backend and written back on another is the same leak wearing the word "restore". A stable pid is treated as *no evidence of pooling* rather than proof of a direct connection, so the bound a direct connection has always had is unchanged. ⚠️ **The pooler semantics rest on PgBouncer's documentation, not on a probe**: a confirming lane with a real pooler is CI infrastructure and is tracked separately.

- **The shadow guard, the confirmation prompt, `sqlens:drift` and `sqlens:doctor --probe` now resolve the connection the way every run does.** `sqlens.connection` is an ordinary documented key and it comes FIRST — before `database.default` — for lint, audit, baseline and the agent rules. Four places re-implemented that resolution and read the host default alone, so the moment a project set the key each of them answered about a connection nothing was running against. The worst of the four is the production guard: with `sqlens.connection` pointing at a production database and `database.default` at something harmless, the detector was handed the harmless name, found nothing to refuse, and a mode whose whole purpose is to CREATE and DROP a database was cleared to run against the other one. The detector calls itself the last line and is deliberately generous for exactly that reason; it was being asked about the wrong database, so its generosity never came into play. The confirmation prompt inherited the same name, which means the person typing yes was told about a database the run would not have touched — an informed consent that was not informed about the right thing. `sqlens:drift` compared a different database than `sqlens:lint` linted, and it creates a reference database to do the comparison; `sqlens:doctor --probe` with no value opened a connection nobody asked about, from the command whose entire job is reporting what SQLens sees. All four now ask `DriverManager::defaultConnectionName()` instead of re-deriving the answer, which is the only version of this that cannot drift apart again. **Nothing changes for a project that does not set `sqlens.connection`**, which is why this went unnoticed: the two keys agree until one of them is used.

## [0.19.0] - 2026-09-19

### Added

- **Every answer a predeploy check could not give now carries its reason as a NAME, and the gate's escape hatch takes a list of those names.** The reasons have always been sentences — specific on purpose, because "which view refused" and "which privilege is missing" are what somebody needs in order to act — but a sentence is not something a configuration can hold. So `deploy.predeploy.allow_undetermined` could only ever be one bit: waive everything that could not answer, or waive nothing. That made two very different situations one decision. "The tool that formats a plan is not installed on this runner" is a fact about the runner a project may reasonably deploy past; "the privilege table could not be read" is that same project not knowing whether the deploy is allowed to do what it is about to do. A project that needed the first had to grant the second. The key now also accepts a list — `'allow_undetermined' => ['missing_external_tool']` — and the gate proceeds only when **every** unanswered question on the run is named on it. Partial coverage stays shut: the run is blocked by nothing else, so one uncovered reason is a question the deploy would otherwise proceed past unanswered. `true` and `false` mean exactly what they meant, `--allow-undetermined` still waives whatever could not answer for the run somebody is watching, and all three are recorded the same way in the report's `undetermined_waiver`.
- **The JSON report names it too: an undetermined finding carries `undetermined_reason` beside its sentence**, and the run summary's histogram counts every reason this build knows. Both are additive — nothing that was in the document has moved or changed shape.
- Every name is validated against the reason vocabulary, on both hatches, so a misspelled entry is reported at the index that is wrong rather than quietly narrowing what a project thought it had waived. An unknown name, a value that is not a list, a list naming nothing valid: each closes the hatch rather than opening it.

### Changed

- **The waiver line says what was waived, not only that something was.** A predeploy that proceeds past unanswered questions has always recorded the fact — `undetermined-waiver=on` beside the result, so a green can be told from a waved-through one. With a named list that line was no longer specific enough: a project that decided it can deploy past one reason and a project that opened the hatch for everything printed the same word. It now reads `undetermined-waiver=missing_external_tool` when a project named what it accepts, and still `on` when the decision was the wide one — `--allow-undetermined` and `true` are not a list of every case, and rendering the reasons that happened to come up would claim somebody chose them. What it names is what the run actually raised, not what the config holds: a project may name three reasons and meet one, and saying all three would claim the run waived more than it did. **The JSON envelope is unchanged and `schema_version` stays at 6** — adding a key bumps it for every consumer, and the boolean there already answers the question that document exists to answer.
- **The reason text of an undetermined result now opens with its name, as `reason_name: the sentence`.** Most of these read that way already — the convention was there long before anything enforced it — but nineteen of them did not, and their wording on the console and in the report gains the prefix. The sentence itself is unchanged. It is composed rather than left beside the name so that the token is legible exactly where the failure is read: an operator who wants to allow that answer has to name it, and prose alone would send them to look it up.

### Fixed

- **The bug-report form no longer tells a Laravel 12 user they are unsupported.** Its checkbox asked a reporter to confirm "PHP ^8.4, Laravel ^13" while `composer.json` declares `^12.0 || ^13.0` across every illuminate component. The form ships to the public mirror and is where every bug report starts, so the first thing a reporter on the older major read was that this package excludes them — and it never did. It now names both majors, and a new arm holds the form and the manifest to each other so widening or raising either floor cannot leave the form behind again.

- **The `predeploy` MCP tool now reads the same waiver the command does, so one run cannot get two verdicts.** The escape hatch became a list of reason names in this release, and `sqlens:predeploy` honors it — but the tool over the protocol read only its own boolean parameter and never looked at the configuration at all. A project that had declared `'allow_undetermined' => ['missing_external_tool']` therefore got a clean exit on the command line and a block from the tool, for the same database at the same moment. The tool's own comment says the two "must not be able to disagree about whether a deploy proceeds, and two readings of the same results are two chances to"; this makes that true again. The parameter is unchanged in meaning and still wins in both directions — it is the counterpart of the `--allow-undetermined` flag, a per-run judgment by somebody watching, and all-or-nothing on both sides. What changed is its **absence**: leaving it out used to mean `false`, and now means what the project declared. The answer's `allow_undetermined` field says whether the hatch was open at all, including on a run where nothing went unanswered and it changed nothing; whether it was actually used is `undetermined_waiver` in the run header, which on this path was previously `null` — "this producer has no gate" — on every run. **The envelope is unchanged and `schema_version` stays at 6.** The tool reaches the waiver as a value object the console command also uses, which is the only relationship the agent layer has to the engine — it gained no path into the console layer, and the architecture guard that holds that line names the new door with its reason.

## [0.18.0] - 2026-09-17

### Added

- **A unique index over `lower(column)` is now reported as the guarantee it is: `PG.L8.UNIQUE_CASE_FOLDED`.** `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX users_email_lower_unique ON users ((lower(email)))` promises that no two rows hold addresses equal once case is folded away — and nothing about the column says so. The column is still `email`, the model still says `unique:users,email`, and an ordinary `where('email', $input)` compares something the index does not constrain. For an address stored as `Taken@Example.com` a check for `taken@example.com` finds nothing, the code concludes the address is free, and the index then refuses the write: the caller was told two different things by two parts of one system. On an endpoint deliberately built not to reveal whether an account exists, the difference between "not found" and "refused" is the disclosure, and that is the case this rule was built from — found by hand in a real application, because no run could say anything about the index. **The remedy is never to drop it.** Fold on the way in so the stored value is the comparison key, or make every lookup fold the way the index does; the rule's page states both and says which one a job or a second application argues for. It sits at level 8 with the other convention rules rather than at level 6, because levels are cumulative and a lower one reaches more consumers: this is a statement about a deliberate structural decision, not a defect, and it belongs to the band a project turns on when it wants to hear about those. It is deliberately not a `security` entry for the same reason — security and privacy bypass the level gate, and an entry there that names an intact schema is how a security finding stops meaning something is broken. **Silence is not an all-clear**: a `citext` column or a case-insensitive collation makes the same promise with no expression to recognize, and this rule cannot see either.
- **The reading behind it takes the spelling the server produces, which is not the one the migration wrote.** `pg_get_indexdef()` prints a key position from the parsed tree: the doubled parentheses a portable migration needs for MySQL are gone, and a cast has appeared wherever the column is not `text` — `lower(email)` over a `varchar` column arrives as `lower(email::text)`. Laravel's `string()` column is `varchar`, so the cast form is the ordinary one and a check written against the bare spelling would match nothing at all while looking entirely correct. A fold wrapped in anything else is refused rather than approximated: `lower(btrim(email))` is unique over a value that is folded *and* trimmed, and a sentence about the column's case would be false about it.
- **The shipped registry holds 238 entries**, one more than 0.17.0. By category: **143 safety**, 58 security, 17 idiom, **10 performance**, 7 convention and 3 privacy. By maturity: **233 stable**, 3 preview, 2 experimental. **158** answer to the level gate; the other **80** carry a severity — 7 critical, 32 high, **29 medium**, 8 low and 4 info. The new entry carries no `downtime_class` — it reports on a schema decision rather than on an operation — so that count stays at 58 entries.

## [0.17.0] - 2026-09-16

### Fixed

- **A functional index on MySQL is now compared instead of skipped, the same way it already was on PostgreSQL.** `CREATE INDEX ... ((lower(display_name)))` came back understood on one engine and as a `not_understood` skip on the other, and nothing said whether that was a limit of MySQL or of this package's reading. It was the reading. Measured against a real MySQL 8.4.10: `information_schema.STATISTICS` returns one row per key position, with `COLUMN_NAME` null and `EXPRESSION` filled on the expression part — the same shape PostgreSQL's per-position `pg_get_indexdef()` produces. The reader was already asking for `EXPRESSION` and then set it aside, recording it beside the index with a flag saying so, and the comprehension check believed the flag over the key list. The expression now enters the key list, so a redundancy question about a functional index is answered rather than declined. **Expect findings you have not seen before**: an index this package used to decline to reason about is now one it reasons about, and on a schema with functional indexes that is new output, not a regression. The flag remains as a fact about the index, beside `visible` and `prefixed_columns`. The same measurement found a second defect: for `KEY (a, (lower(b)))` the expression was read off the index's leading key part, which carries none, so an index that plainly had one reported `null` — it is collected across the key parts now.

## [0.16.0] - 2026-09-16

### Changed

- **Two absences in the rule set are now stated rather than left as gaps.** MySQL's `--skip-grant-tables` gets no rule, because the server enables `skip_networking` alongside it and therefore refuses every remote connection — a run that connected at all proves it is not in that state, so the rule could never have a subject. And there is no composite id for a `pg_hba.conf` line that authenticates with `password` on a server whose `ssl` is off: `SEC.AUTH.HBA_CLEARTEXT` and `SEC.PG.TLS.DISABLED` are both already in the report on exactly those runs, and a third id would only restate their conjunction. Both reasons now sit where the question comes up, on the scope page and beside the rule.

### Fixed

- **On MySQL, `sqlens:predeploy` no longer judges the server settings when no migration is pending.** MySQL ships `lock_wait_timeout` at one year, which this check reports as an unbounded wait at `high` — and the MySQL side never asked whether anything was about to run, so every deploy on a server at its defaults stopped over it, including the ones that carried no migration at all. The messages argued from "a migration is about to run" either way. It now makes the split the PostgreSQL side has made since 0.11.0: with nothing pending every variable is `not_applicable` with the reason `nothing_pending`, named with its value and without the sentence about the change; with a migration pending nothing changes. The provenance note stays a `pass` in both cases, because it describes the reading, not the change.

## [0.15.3] - 2026-09-16

### Fixed

- **`sqlens:predeploy` stops a deploy only for a `high` server setting; the lower ones are reported beside a passing result.** Every finding of `DEPLOY.CONTEXT.SETTING` blocked, whatever its severity, and a consumer hit the cost on a managed PostgreSQL: a deploy with a pending migration was refused over `statement_timeout = 0`, which cannot be set server-wide without bounding the very migration the deploy runs, and over `max_wal_size` at its default, which such a host does not let anybody change. The rule's own page called both "reported rather than judged". Now `high` stops the deploy — `lock_timeout = 0` on PostgreSQL, and `lock_wait_timeout`, `foreign_key_checks` and `sql_mode` on MySQL — while `medium`, `low` and `info` travel as `pass` findings with their values and sentences. On MySQL that also ends a deploy stopped by nothing but the `info` note about a role that cannot read `performance_schema`. A `lock_timeout` set with `SET` inside a session still does not clear the finding, because the check reads what a fresh session gets; a role default does.

## [0.15.2] - 2026-09-15

### Fixed

- **The MCP handshake answers the one revision this build speaks, or it refuses — it never negotiates a third one.** A client asking for a revision SQLens does not implement was being served `2025-11-25` instead of a refusal naming `2025-06-18`, which is the quiet direction this pin exists against: the client gets a message shape it did not ask for, cannot check, and has no reason to doubt. The refusal is back, by name and with the supported revision in it, exactly as before. A revision substituted for the pinned one is now refused as well, rather than served — a server that announces a revision it does not implement is worse than one that will not start.

### Changed

- **The dev toolchain carries `laravel/mcp`, `laravel/ai` and `laravel/boost`.** They sit in `require-dev`, so nothing a consumer installs, calls or configures changes, and a Renovate rule keeps their constraints following each release.

## [0.15.1] - 2026-09-15

### Fixed

- **A partial index on a number is read: `WHERE tenant_id = 1` was not.** The predicate model refuses *"a comparison against a non-literal"*, and its literal pattern was quoted-only — so an equality against a number fell under that sentence while meaning nothing of the kind. A number is exactly as unambiguous there as a string, and `tenant_id = 1` is the partial index of a multi-tenant schema. Under `--profile=ci` each one was an `undetermined`, which `strict_undetermined` turns into exit 3.

  **Found before it was reported.** Twenty-two realistic partial indexes were created on a real PostgreSQL 18, their printed predicates read back and put through the parser: sixteen read, six refused — and only one of the six was a gap. The other five are refusals the model states on purpose: an ordering comparison, the pair a `BETWEEN` prints, an `OR`, and a jsonb operator.

  Equality, inequality and their `ANY`/`ALL` set forms now take a number, in the parenthesised cast shape the server prints inside an array (`(1)::bigint`). **An ordering comparison stays refused**, and an arm holds that in four spellings — without it the widening would have traded the defect for its mirror image, because two indexes at `> 100` and `> 99` are not the same index and would now look it. Every new shape is in the corpus the PostgreSQL lane checks against a live server.

- **A partial index whose excluded values carry their own cast is read instead of refused.** PostgreSQL prints the same `NOT IN` predicate two ways depending on how the migration wrote it: the cast on the whole array — `(ARRAY['a'::character varying])::text[]` — or on each member inside its own parentheses, `('a'::character varying)::text`. The first was read; the second was not. Stripping the casts left `('a')`, which is no longer a literal, so every member failed to parse and the whole predicate came back `not_understood`.

  That is not a cosmetic refusal. Under `--profile=ci` the `strict_undetermined` policy escalates an undetermined verdict to exit 3, so a consuming application whose entitlement index carries this shape could not get a clean run — and the three ways out were all worse than the defect: suppressing the reason would have silenced **every** unreadable catalog row rather than this one, no per-rule configuration exists, and rewriting a correct index for a reader's benefit puts the tool above the thing it inspects.

  The literal is now unwrapped the same way a parenthesised column reference already was, and for the same reason: the parentheses belong to a cast that has just been removed. One literal and nothing else between them — `('a' || 'b')` keeps its parentheses, because dropping them there would change what the text says.
